Sticky Kakeya sets and the sticky Kakeya conjecture
Hong Wang, Joshua Zahl

Abstract
A Kakeya set is a compact subset of ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} that contains a unit line segment pointing in every direction. The Kakeya conjecture asserts that such sets must have Hausdorff and Minkowski dimension n n . There is a special class of Kakeya sets, called sticky Kakeya sets. Sticky Kakeya sets exhibit an approximate multi-scale self-similarity, and sets of this type played an important role in Katz, Łaba, and Tao’s groundbreaking 1999 work on the Kakeya problem. We propose a special case of the Kakeya conjecture, which asserts that sticky Kakeya sets must have Hausdorff and Minkowski dimension n n . We prove this conjecture in three dimensions.



1
Introduction

A compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is called a
Kakeya set
if it contains a unit line segment pointing in every direction. A surprising construction by Besicovitch
[
2
]
shows that such sets can have measure 0. The Kakeya set conjecture asserts that every Kakeya set in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
has Hausdorff and Minkowski dimension
n
. There is also a slightly more technical, single-scale variant of this conjecture, which is called the Kakeya maximal function conjecture: let
\delta>0
and let
\mathbb{T}
be a set of
1\times\delta
tubes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
whose coaxial lines point in
\delta
-separated directions. Then the tubes must be almost disjoint, in the sense that for every
1\leq d\leq n
and every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
C=C(n,d,\varepsilon)
(independent of
\delta
) so that

\Big\|\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}\chi_{T}\Big\|_{\frac{d}{d-1}}\leq C\Big(\frac{1}{\delta}\Big)^{\frac{n}{d}-1+\varepsilon}\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|T|\Big)^{\frac{d-1}{d}}.

(1.1)

The Kakeya set conjecture and Kakeya maximal conjecture were proved for
n=2
by Davies
[
8
]
and Cordoba
[
7
]
, respectively. The conjectures remain open in three and higher dimensions.

The Kakeya conjecture is closely related to questions in Fourier analysis. This connection was first explored by Fefferman
[
13
]
, who used a variant of Besicovitch’s construction to show that the ball multiplier
\widehat{Tf}=\chi_{B}\hat{f}
is unbounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
when
p\neq 2
. In
[
3
]
, Bourgain obtained new estimates for Stein’s Fourier restriction conjecture in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by first proving, and then using estimates of the form (
1.1
) (for certain
d<3
) in three dimensions. In brief, a function whose Fourier transform is supported on a curved manifold
M
can be decomposed into a sum of “wave packets,” each of which is supported on a tube
T
. The curvature of
M
ensures that many of these wave packets point in different directions, and estimates of the form (
1.1
) can be used to analyze the possible interection patterns of these wave packets.

Since Bourgain’s seminal work
[
3
]
, Kakeya estimates have served as an input when studying the Fourier restriction problem
[
5
,
9
,
30
]
, and methods that were developed in the context of the Kakeya problem have been successfully applied to the Fourier restriction problem, Bochner-Riesz problem, and related questions. Indeed, the modern renaissance in polynomial method techniques was sparked by Zeev Dvir’s proof
[
11
]
of Wolff’s finite field Kakeya conjecture. These polynomial method techniques have since revolutionized discrete math, combinatorial geometry, and harmonic analysis. See e.g.
[
15
,
26
]
for a modern survey of these developments.

In this paper we will restrict our attention to the Kakeya set conjecture in three dimensions. In
[
31
]
, Wolff proved that every Kakeya set in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
has Hausdorff dimension at least
5/2
. In
[
17
]
, Katz, Łaba, and Tao made the following improvement: every Kakeya set in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
has upper Minkowski dimension at least
5/2+c
, where
c>0
is a small absolute constant. Katz, Łaba, and Tao’s argument began by carefully analyzing the structure of a (hypothetical) Kakeya set that had dimension exactly 5/2. At scale
\delta
, such a set
K
would contain a union of roughly
\delta^{-2}

1\times\delta
tubes that point in
\delta
-separated directions, and the union of these tubes would have volume roughly
\delta^{1/2}
. Next, Katz, Łaba, and Tao considered how these
1\times\delta
tubes would arrange themselves at scale
\rho=\delta^{1/2}
. Since the
1\times\delta
tubes point in different directions, each
1\times\rho
tube can contain at most
\rho^{2}/\delta^{2}

1\times\delta
tubes. If equality (or near equality) holds (i.e. if the
1\times\delta
tubes arrange themselves into roughly
\rho^{-2}

1\times\rho
tubes, each of which contain
\rho^{2}/\delta

1\times\delta
tubes) then we say the arrangement of
1\times\delta
tubes is
sticky
at scale
\rho
. As a starting point for their arguments in
[
17
]
, Katz, Łaba, and Tao used a result by Wolff
[
33
]
to prove that that if a Kakeya set
K
has upper Minkowski dimension
5/2
, then the corresponding set of
1\times\delta
tubes must be sticky at every scale
\rho\in(\delta,1)
.

Motivated by this observation, we introduce a special class of Kakeya sets, which we call sticky Kakeya sets.
Let
\mathcal{L}
be the set of (affine) lines in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, equipped with the metric
d(l,l^{\prime})=|p-p^{\prime}|+\angle(u,u^{\prime})
. Here
u
(resp
u^{\prime}
) is a unit vector parallel to
\ell
and
p
(resp.
p^{\prime}
) is the unique point on
\ell
with
p\perp u
. Since the direction map from
\mathcal{L}
to
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
is Lipschitz, if
L\subset\mathcal{L}
contains a line in every direction then
\dim_{P}(L)\geq n-1
. (Here
\dim_{P}
denotes packing dimension; similar statements hold for other metric notions of dimension, but this is less relevant to the current discussion). We say a Kakeya set is sticky if equality holds:

Definition 1.1
.

A compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is called a
sticky Kakeya set
if there is a set of lines
L
with packing dimension
n-1
that contains at least one line in each direction, so that
\ell\cap K
contains a unit interval for each
\ell\in L
.

Observe that if we drop the requirement that
L
has packing dimension
n-1
, then we have recovered the usual definition of a Kakeya set. In particular, every sticky Kakeya set is also a (classical) Kakeya set. As we will see in Section
2
, when sticky Kakeya sets (in the sense of Definition
1.1
) are discretized at a small scale
\delta>0
, the corresponding collection of
1\times\delta
tubes is sticky in the sense of Katz, Łaba, and Tao.
With these definitions, we can introduce the sticky Kakeya set conjecture.

Conjecture 1.1
.

Every sticky Kakeya set in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
has Hausdorff and Minkowski dimension
n
.

A defining feature of sticky Kakeya sets is that if
K
is a (hypothetical) counter-example to the sticky Kakeya conjecture, then
K
must be roughly self-similar at many different scales. This was first observed in
[
17
]
and discussed in greater detail in
[
29
]
. A precise version of this principle is stated in Proposition
3.2
below.

Since every sticky Kakeya set is also a (classical) Kakeya set, the Kakeya set conjecture implies the sticky Kakeya conjecture, and partial progress toward the Kakeya set conjecture implies the same partial progress for the sticky Kakeya conjecture. In particular, Davies’ solution
[
8
]
to the Kakeya set conjecture in the plane implies that Conjecture
1.1
holds when
n=2
. In this paper, we will prove Conjecture
1.1
for
n=3
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Every sticky Kakeya set in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
has Hausdorff dimension 3.

Our proof of Theorem
1.1
is inspired by, and partially follows, the arguments recorded in Terence Tao’s blog entry
[
29
]
. That blog entry sketches an approach to solving the Kakeya conjecture in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
that was explored by Nets Katz and Terence Tao in the early 2000s. In brief,
[
29
]
begins by conjecturing that a (hypothetical) counter-example
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
to the Kakeya conjecture in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
must be sticky, and must also have two additional structural properties, which are called planiness and graininess. We will discuss this conjecture in Section
1.2
below. Next, the blog entry describes how these three properties can be used to obtain multi-scale structural information about
K
. Finally, the blog entry suggests how to use this structural information to produce a counter-example to Bourgain’s discretized sum-product theorem
[
4
]
, and hence obtain a contradiction.

1.1
A sketch of the proof

We will briefly outline our approach to proving Theorem
1.1
, and discuss how it relates to the strategy from
[
29
]
. Our proof can be divided into three major steps. The first step follows some of the arguments described in
[
29
]
. For steps 2 and 3 our arguments take a different path from those presented in
[
29
]
; we do this in part because sticky Kakeya sets can have structural properties that were not discussed in
[
29
]
.

Step 1: Discretization and multi-scale self-similarity

In Section
2
, we set up a discretization of the sticky Kakeya problem that allows us to exploit multi-scale self-similarity. The technical details of this procedure are new, and we hope that this setup will be useful when studying the sticky Kakeya conjecture in higher dimensions.

In Section
3
, we flesh-out the ideas from
[
29
]
to obtain multi-scale structural information about sticky Kakeya sets. The main conclusion of this section is as follows. Suppose that the sticky Kakeya conjecture in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
was false, i.e.
\inf\dim_{H}K=n-\sigma_{n}
, where the infimum is taken over all sticky Kakeya sets in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
\sigma_{n}>0
. Let
K
be a sticky Kakeya set with dimension
n-\sigma_{n}+\varepsilon
for some small
\varepsilon>0
(we will call this an
\varepsilon
-extremal sticky Kakeya set), and let
E
be the discretization of
K
at a small scale
\delta>0
. Then
E
contains a union of
1\times\delta
-tubes, which are sticky in the sense of Katz, Łaba, and Tao. Furthermore, this collection of
\delta
-tubes is coarsely self-similar: for each intermediate scale
\rho\in(\delta,1)
, the
\rho
-thickening of
K
again resembles an
\varepsilon
-extremal sticky Kakeya set, and the union of
\delta
-tubes inside each
\rho
-tube resembles a (anisotropically re-scaled)
\varepsilon
-extremal sticky Kakeya set. The precise statement is given by Proposition
3.2
.

In Section
4
we restrict attention to the case
n=3
and again follow arguments proposed by Katz and Tao in
[
29
]
. We show that extremal sticky Kakeya sets must have a structural property called planiness. Specifically, with
K
and
E
as above, if
\mathbb{T}
is the set of
1\times\delta
tubes contained in
E
, then there is a function
V\colon E\to S^{2}
so that for each point
x\in E
, the tubes in
\mathbb{T}
containing
x
must make small angle with the subspace
V(x)^{\perp}
.
V(x)
is called a plane map, and we use the multi-scale self-similarity of extremal Kakeya sets to show that
V
must be Lipschitz. An important consequence is that inside each ball of radius
\delta^{1/2}
, the set
E
can be decomposed into a disjoint union of parallel rectangular prisms of dimensions roughly
\delta^{1/2}\times\delta^{1/2}\times\delta
; these rectangular prisms are called grains. After an anisotropic re-scaling, we obtain a new collection of
1\times\delta
tubes whose union
E^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfies the following
Property (P):
For each
z_{0}\in[0,1]
, the slice
E^{\prime}\cap\{z=z_{0}\}
is a union of parallel
1\times\delta
rectangles whose spacing forms an Ahlfors-regular set of dimension
1-\sigma_{3}
; the direction of these rectangles is determined by a “slope function”
f(z_{0})
, which is Lipschitz.

Step 2: Regularity of the slope function

In Section
5
, we show that the slope function
f(z)
described above is
C^{2}
(or more precisely, it agrees at scale
\delta
with a function that has controlled
C^{2}
norm). To do this, it suffices to show that at every scale
\rho\in(\delta,1)
and for every interval
I
of length
\rho^{1/2}
, the graph of
f
above
\rho
can be contained in the
\rho
-neighborhood of a line. We will briefly describe how this is done. Fix a scale
\rho
and an interval
I
, and let
F\subset[0,1]^{2}
be a re-scaled copy of the graph of
f
above
I
. We will also construct a set
G\subset[0,1]^{2}
, which is the graph of a Lipschitz function
g\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
that encodes a local analogue of the plane map
V
. We construct the set
G
so that certain closed paths inside
E^{\prime}
are encoded by arithmetic operations on the sets
F
and
G
. Specifically, whenever we select points
p\in F
and
q,q^{\prime}\in G
, we have that
p\cdot(q-q^{\prime})
specifies the location of one of the
1\times\delta
rectangles in the set
E^{\prime}\cap\{z=z_{0}\}
that was described above in Property (P). Since the spacing of these rectangles form an Ahlfors-regular set, we conclude that

F\cdot(G-G)\quad\textrm{is contained in an Ahlfors regular set of dimension}\ 1-\sigma_{3}.

(1.2)

Recall that
F
and
G
are the graphs of Lipschitz functions, and thus satisfy a non-concentration condition analogous to having Hausdorff dimension 1. Is it possible for (
1.2
) to occur? If
F
and
G
are contained in orthogonal lines, then
F\cdot(G-G)
is a point, so the answer is yes. The next result says that this is the only way that (
1.2
) can occur.

Theorem
5.2
, informal version
.

Let
F
and
G
be one-dimensional sets that have been discretized at a small scale
\rho>0
. Then either (A): (most of)
F
and
G
are contained in the
\rho
-neighborhoods of orthogonal lines, or (B): there exists some scale
\rho\leq t<\!\!<1
so that the
t
-neighborhood of
F\cdot(G-G)
contains an interval.

The precise version of Theorem
5.2
is stated in Section
5
and proved in Section
8
. Theorem
5.2
appears to be a result in additive combinatorics, but our proof of Theorem
5.2
uses recent ideas from projection theory that were developed in the context of the Falconer distance problem
[
23
,
27
,
24
]
. In brief, if
G
is not contained in a line, then by an analogue of Beck’s theorem due to Orponen, Shmerkin, and the first author
[
24
]
, we might expect
G
to span a two-dimensional set of lines, and hence a one-dimensional set of directions, i.e. there is a one-dimensional set
\Theta\subset S^{1}
so that for each
\theta\in\Theta
, there are points
q,q^{\prime}\in G
with
\theta=\frac{q-q^{\prime}}{|q-q^{\prime}|}
. If this happens, then Kaufman’s projection theorem says that there exists a direction
\theta\in\Theta
for which
F\cdot\theta
(and hence
F\cdot(q-q^{\prime})
) has dimension 1. The actual proof of Theorem
5.2
must overcome several difficulties when executing the above strategy. First, Theorem
5.2
has weaker hypotheses than the ones stated above; the set
G\cdot(F-F)
is replaced by a smaller set, where the differences and dot products are taken along a sparse (but not too sparse!) subset of
F\times G\times G
. Second, the proof sketch above supposes a dichotomy, where either
G
is contained in a line, or it spans a one-dimensional set of directions. In the discretized setting, however, this dichotomy is less apparent because
G
can behave differently at different scales.

Returning to the proof of Theorem
1.1
, if Item (B) from Theorem
5.2
holds, then by an observation of Dyatlov and the second author
[
10
, Proposition 6.13]
,
F\cdot(G-G)
cannot be contained in an Ahlfors-regular set of dimension strictly smaller than 1. Thus Theorem
5.2
implies that the graph of
f
above
I
must be contained in the
\rho
neighborhood of a line. Since this holds for every interval
I
at every scale
\rho
, we conclude that
f
is
C^{2}
.

Step 3: Twisted projections

In Section
6
we exploit the fact that lines in a Kakeya set point in different directions to show that the slope function
f
has (moderately) large derivative, i.e.
|f^{\prime}|
is bounded away from 0.

In Section
7
, we begin by observing the following consequence of Property (P) from Step 1 above. Since each slice
E^{\prime}\cap\{z=z_{0}\}
is a union of parallel
1\times\delta
rectangles whose spacing forms an Ahlfors regular set and whose slope is given by
f(z_{0})
, if we define
\pi_{f}(x,y,z)=\big(x+yf(z),\ z\big)
, then
\pi_{f}(E^{\prime})\subset[0,1]^{2}
has measure roughly
\delta^{\sigma_{3}}
. This is because each slice
\pi_{f}(E^{\prime})\cap\{z=z_{0}\}
is a union of
\delta
-intervals that are arranged like a
1-\sigma_{3}
dimensional Ahlfors regular set.

If
T
is a
1\times\delta
tube, then
\pi_{f}(T)
is the
\delta
-neighborhood of a
C^{2}
curve. Since the tubes in
\mathbb{T}
point in different directions and since
|f^{\prime}|
is bounded away from 0, the corresponding curves
\pi_{f}(T)
satisfy a separation property related to Sogge’s cinematic curvature condition
[
21
,
28
]
. The next result says that the union of these curves must be large

Proposition
7.1
, informal version
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a set of
1\times\delta
tubes pointing in
\delta
-separated directions, with
\#\mathbb{T}\gtrsim\delta^{-2}
. Let
f\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\|f\|_{C^{2}}\lesssim 1
and
|f^{\prime}|\sim 1
. Then for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
c_{\varepsilon}>0
so that
\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}}\pi_{f}(T)
has measure at least
c_{\varepsilon}\delta^{\varepsilon}
.

The precise version of Proposition
7.1
is a maximal function estimate analogous to (
1.1
). It is proved using a variant of Wolff’s
L^{3}
bound for the Wolff circular maximal function
[
32
]
, where circles are replaced by a family of
C^{2}
curves that satisfy Sogge’s cinematic curvature condition. This latter result was recently proved by Pramanik, Yang, and the second author
[
25
]
. Since
E^{\prime}
contains about
\delta^{-2}
tubes that point in
\delta
-separated directions, Proposition
7.1
says that
\pi_{f}(E^{\prime})
must have measure at least
c_{\varepsilon}\delta^{\varepsilon}
, for each
\varepsilon>0
. On the other hand, Property (P) says that
\pi_{f}(E^{\prime})
has measure at most
\delta^{\sigma_{3}}
. We conclude that
\sigma_{3}=0
, which finishes the proof.

1.2
The sticky Kakeya conjecture versus the Kakeya conjecture

In this section, we will informally discuss what Theorem
1.1
tells us about the Kakeya conjecture. In particular, how close does Theorem
1.1
get us to proving the Kakeya conjecture in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
? Katz and Tao’s arguments from
[
29
]
begin by conjecturing that a (hypothetical) counter-example
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
to the Kakeya conjecture in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
must have the structural properties stickiness, planiness, and graininess. The results from
[
1
]
and
[
14
]
show that such a counter-example must be plainy and grainy, but it is unclear whether
K
must be sticky. To reformulate the remaining part of Katz and Tao’s conjecture in our language, we need the following definition.

Definition 1.2
.

A Kakeya set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
\varepsilon
-close to being a sticky if there is a set of lines
L
with packing dimension at most
n-1+\varepsilon
that contains at least one line in each direction, so that
\ell\cap K
contains a unit interval for each
\ell\in L
.

In particular, a sticky Kakeya set is
\varepsilon
-close to being sticky for every
\varepsilon>0
. The following version of Katz and Tao’s conjecture says that Kakeya sets that are nearly extremal are close to being sticky.

Conjecture 1.2
.

Suppose the Kakeya conjecture in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is false, i.e.
\inf\dim_{H}K=d<n
, where the infimum is taken over all Kakeya sets in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta>0
so that the following holds. Let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Kakeya set with
\dim_{H}(K)\leq d+\eta
. Then
K
is
\varepsilon
-close to being sticky.

If Conjecture
1.2
is true for
n=3
, then the following generalization of Theorem
1.1
would imply the Kakeya conjecture in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Theorem
1.1
′
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta>0
so that the following holds. Let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a Kakeya set that is
\eta
-close to being sticky. Then
\dim_{H}K\geq 3-\varepsilon
.

Remark 1.1
.

A consequence of Theorem
1.1
′
is that if an X-ray estimate holds in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
at dimension
d<3
, then there exists
c>0
so that every Kakeya set in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
must have upper Minkowski dimension at least
d+c
.

One piece of evidence in favor of Conjecture
1.2
at the time it was formulated in
[
29
]
was that the only known example of a Kakeya-like object with Hausdorff dimension less than 3 was the (second) Heisenberg group

\mathbb{H}=\{(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\colon\operatorname{Im}(z_{3})=\operatorname{Im}(z_{1}\bar{z}_{2})\}.

\mathbb{H}
is a counter-example to a strong form of the Kakeya conjecture, and it has (complex analogues of) the structural properties stickiness, planiness, and graininess.

More recently, however, Katz and the second author
[
20
]
discovered a second Kakeya-like object that has small volume at certain scales—this is analogous to having Hausdorff dimension less than 3. This object is called the
SL_{2}
example, and it is not sticky. The
SL_{2}
example is not a counter-example to Conjecture
1.2
(nor to the Kakeya conjecture) because it is not a Kakeya set in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Nonetheless, the existence of the
SL_{2}
example suggests that in order to resolve the Kakeya conjecture in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, it will be necessary to study Kakeya sets that are far from sticky.

As a starting point for this latter program, it seems reasonable to show that that the
SL_{2}
example cannot be realized in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. To make this statement precise, define
\mathcal{L}_{SL_{2}}
be the set of lines in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
that can either be written in the form
(a,b,0)+\mathbb{R}(c,d,1)
with
ad-bc=1
, or
(0,0,0)+\mathbb{R}(c,d,0)
. Equivalently,
\mathcal{L}_{SL_{2}}
is the set of horizontal lines in the first Heisenberg group.

We conjecture that lines from this set cannot be used to construct a counter-example to the Kakeya conjecture.

Conjecture 1.3
.

Let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be compact, and suppose there is a set of lines
L\subset\mathcal{L}_{SL_{2}}
that contains at least one line in each direction, so that
\ell\cap K
contains a unit interval for each
\ell\in L
. Then
K
has Hausdorff and Minkowski dimension 3.

We will not discuss Conjectures
1.2
or
1.3
further. The remainder of this paper will be devoted to proving Theorem
1.1
and
1.1
′
.

1.3
Thanks

The authors would like to thank Larry Guth, Nets Katz, Pablo Shmerkin, and Terence Tao for helpful conversations and suggestions during the preparation of this manuscript. Hong Wang was supported by NSF Grant DMS-2055544. Joshua Zahl was supported by a NSERC Discovery grant.

2
Discretization

2.1
Lines, tubes and shadings

We begin by recalling some standard definitions and terminology associated with the Kakeya problem. Through this section, we will fix an integer
n\geq 2
; all implicit constants may depend on
n
. Slightly abusing the notation we introduced in the introduction, we will define
\mathcal{L}_{n}
to be the set of lines in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of the form
(\underline{p},0)+\mathbb{R}v
, where
\underline{p}\in[-\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{n}]^{n-1}
and
v\in S^{n-1}
has final coordinate
v_{n}\geq 1/2
. We define
d(\ell,\ell^{\prime})=|\underline{p}-\underline{p}^{\prime}|+\angle(v,v^{\prime})
. This definition of distance is comparable to the definition from Section
1
. We define the measure
\lambda_{n}
on
\mathcal{L}_{n}
to be the product measure of
(n-1)
-dimensional Euclidean measure and normalized surface measure on
S^{n-1}
(the latter we denote by
\nu_{n-1}
). With these definitions of distance and measure, the packing dimension of a set
L\subset\mathcal{L}_{n}
agrees with its upper modified box dimension.

For
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
and
\delta>0
, we define the
\delta
-tube
T
with coaxial line
\ell
to be
N_{2n\delta}(\ell)\cap[-1,1]^{n}
, where
N_{t}(X)
denotes the
t
-neighborhood of
X
. This definition is slightly nonstandard, since we use the
2n\delta
-neighborhood rather than the
\delta
-neighborhood of
\ell
; we do this so that a cube of side-length
\delta
intersecting
\ell
will be contained in the associated tube
T
. Observe that any tube of this type has volume comparable to
\delta^{n-1}
. If
T
is a
\delta
-tube and
C>0
, we will use
CT
to denote the
C\delta
-tube with the same coaxial line. We say two
\delta
-tubes
T,T^{\prime}
are
essentially identical
if their respective coaxial lines satisfy
d(\ell,\ell^{\prime})\leq\delta
; otherwise
T
and
T^{\prime}
are
essentially distinct
. We say that
T
and
T^{\prime}
are
essentially parallel
if their respective coaxial lines satisfy
|\operatorname{dir}(\ell)-\operatorname{dir}(\ell^{\prime})|\leq\delta
, where
\operatorname{dir}(\ell)
is the unit vector
v
described above.

A
\delta
-
cube
is a set of the form
[0,\delta)^{n}+p
, where
p\in(\delta\mathbb{Z})^{n}.
A
shading
of a
\delta
-tube
T
is a set
Y(T)\subset T
that is a union of
\delta
-cubes. We will write
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
to denote a set of
\delta
tubes and their associated shadings
\{Y(T)\colon T\in\mathbb{T}\}
. If
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
is a set of tubes and their shadings, we write
E_{\mathbb{T}}
to denote the set
\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}}Y(T)
; the shading
Y
will always be apparent from context.

We say that
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
is a
sub-collection
of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
if
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{T}
and
Y^{\prime}(T^{\prime})\subset Y(T^{\prime})
for each
T^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
. We say that
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
is a
refinement
of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
if in addition,
\sum_{T^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}|Y^{\prime}(T)|\geq(\log(1/\delta))^{-C}\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|Y(T)|
, where
C\geq 0
is a constant that depends only on
n
. We will denote this by
\sum_{T^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}|Y^{\prime}(T)|\gtrapprox_{\delta}\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|Y(T)|.
In practice, we will only study collections of tubes
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
with
\#\mathbb{T}\leq\delta^{-100n}
. By dyadic pigeonholing, every collection of tubes with this property has a refinement
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
with
\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\chi_{Y^{\prime}(T)}=\mu\chi_{E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}}
for some integer
\mu\geq 1
; this is called a
constant multiplicity
refinement of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
.

Let
T
be a
\delta
-tube and let
\tilde{T}
be a
\rho
-tube, with
\delta\leq\rho
. We say that
\tilde{T}

covers

T
if their respective coaxial lines satisfy
d(\ell,\tilde{\ell})\leq\rho/2
. If
\mathbb{T}
is a collection of
\delta
-tubes and
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
is a collection of
\rho
-tubes, we say
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
covers
\mathbb{T}
if every tube in
\mathbb{T}
is covered by some tube in
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
. If this is the case, we write
\mathbb{T}[\tilde{T}]
to denote the set of tubes in
\mathbb{T}
that are covered by
\tilde{T}
. A similar definition holds for collections of tubes and their associated shadings: we say that
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}

covers

(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
if
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
covers
\mathbb{T}
, and for each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
and each
T\in\mathbb{T}[\tilde{T}]
we have
Y(T)\subset\tilde{Y}(\tilde{T})
. Such a cover is called
balanced
if
|E_{\mathbb{T}}\cap Q|
is the same for each
\rho
-cube
Q\subset E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}}

2.2
Defining
\sigma_{n}

In this section we will define a quantity, called
\sigma_{n}
, that will allow us to formulate a discretized version of the sticky Kakeya conjecture.

Definition 2.1
.

For
s,t,\delta\in(0,1)
, define

M(s,t,\delta)=\inf_{\mathbb{T},Y}\Big|\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}}Y(T)\Big|.

(2.1)

Here, the infimum is taken over all pairs
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
with the following properties.

(a)

The tubes in
\mathbb{T}
are essentially distinct.

(b)

For each
\delta\leq\rho\leq 1
,
\mathbb{T}
can be covered by a set of
\rho
tubes, at most
\delta^{-t}
of which are pairwise parallel.

(c)

\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|Y(T)|\geq\delta^{s}

Next, let

N(s,t)=\limsup_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\frac{\log M(s,t,\delta)}{\log\delta}.

(2.2)

N(s,t)
is defined for all
(s,t)\in(0,1)^{2}
. Observe that for
\delta\in(0,1)
, if
s\leq s^{\prime}
and
t\leq t^{\prime}
and, then
M(s,t,\delta)\geq M(s^{\prime},t^{\prime},\delta)
, and hence
N(s,t)\leq N(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
. In particular, since
0\leq N(s,t)\leq n
, we have that

\lim_{(s,t)\to(0,0)}N(s,t)=\inf_{(s,t)\in(0,1)^{2}}N(s,t).

Denote this common value by
\sigma_{n}
. Our definition of
\sigma_{n}
was chosen so that two properties hold. First,
\sigma_{n}
allows us to bound the Hausdorff dimension of sticky Kakeya sets. This will be described in Section
2.3
. Second, collections
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
that nearly extremize the infimum in (
2.1
) will have useful structural properties at many scales. This will be described in Section
3
.

2.3
\sigma_{n}
and the dimension of sticky Kakeya sets

In this section we will relate
\sigma_{n}
to the dimension of sticky Kakeya sets. Our goal is to prove the following.

Proposition 2.1
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta>0
so that the following holds. Let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Kakeya set that is
\eta
-close to being sticky. Then
\dim_{H}K\geq n-\sigma_{n}-\varepsilon
. In particular, if
K
is a sticky Kakeya set then
\dim_{H}K\geq n-\sigma_{n}
.

Our first task is to analyze families of lines in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that point in many directions and have packing dimension close to
n-1
. For each
\rho>0
and
\varepsilon>0
, define the class of “Quantitatively Sticky” sets
\operatorname{QStick}(n,t,\rho)
to be the collection of sets
L\subset\mathcal{L}_{n}
that satisfy

\Big|\big\{\underline{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\colon(\underline{p},v)\in N_{\delta}(L)\big\}\Big|\leq\delta^{n-1-t}\quad\textrm{for all}\ v\in S^{n-1},\ \delta\in(0,\rho),

where in the above expression,
N_{\delta}(L)
denotes the
\delta
-neighborhood of
L
in the metric space
\mathcal{L}_{n}
.

For example, if
L=\{(\underline{p},v)\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\colon\underline{p}=f(v)\}
for some Lipschitz function
f
, then for each
t>0
we have
L\in\operatorname{QStick}(n,t,\rho)
for all sufficiently small
\rho
. The next lemma explains how
\operatorname{QStick}(n,t,\rho)
is related to sticky Kakeya sets.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
L\subset\mathcal{L}_{n}
with
\nu_{n-1}(\operatorname{dir}(L))>0
. Then for all
t>\dim_{P}(L)-(n-1)
, there exists
L^{\prime}\subset L
and
\rho>0
with
\nu_{n-1}(\operatorname{dir}(L^{\prime}))>0
and
L^{\prime}\in\operatorname{QStick}(n,t,\rho)
.

Proof.

Let
d=\dim_{P}L
, and let
\varepsilon=t-d+(n-1)
. Since
d=\dim_{P}(L)=\overline{\dim}_{MB}(L)
, we can write
L
as a countable union of sets
\{L_{i}\}
, with
\overline{\dim}_{M}L_{i}\leq d+\varepsilon/4
for each
i
. Since
\nu_{n-1}\big(\bigcup\operatorname{dir}(L_{i})\big)=\nu_{n-1}(\operatorname{dir}(L))>0
, there exists an index
i
so that
\nu_{n-1}(\operatorname{dir}L_{i})>0
. Re-indexing if necessary, we can suppose that
\nu_{n-1}(\operatorname{dir}L_{1})>0
. Since
\overline{\dim}_{M}L_{1}\leq d+\varepsilon/4
, we can select
\rho_{1}>0
sufficiently small so that

\lambda_{n}\big(N_{\delta}(L_{1})\big)\leq\delta^{(2n-2)-d-\varepsilon/2}\quad\textrm{for all}\ \delta\in(0,\rho_{1}).

(2.3)

We say a direction
v\in S^{n-1}
is
over-represented
at scale
\delta
if

\Big|\big\{\underline{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\colon(\underline{p},v)\in N_{2\delta}(L_{1})\big\}\Big|>\delta^{n-1-t}=\delta^{(2n-2)-\delta-\varepsilon}.

By (
2.3
) and Fubini (recall that
\lambda_{n}
is a product measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times S^{n-1}
), for each
\delta\in(0,\rho_{1})
, we have

\nu_{n-1}\big(\{v\in S^{n-1}\colon v\ \textrm{is over-represented at scale}\ \delta\}\big)\lesssim\delta^{\varepsilon/2}.

(2.4)

Thus for each integer
k_{1}
with
2^{-k_{1}}\leq\rho_{1}
, we have

\sum_{k\geq k_{1}}\nu_{n-1}\big(\{v\colon v\ \textrm{is over-represented at scale}\ 2^{-k}\}\big)\lesssim k_{1}^{-\varepsilon/2}.

(2.5)

Selecting
k_{1}
appropriately, we can ensure that the LHS of (
2.5
) is at most
\nu_{n-1}(\operatorname{dir}(L_{1}))/2
. Note that if
v\in S^{n-1}
is not over-represented at any dyadic scale
2^{-k}\leq 2^{-k_{1}}
, then

\Big|\big\{\underline{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\colon(\underline{p},v)\in N_{\delta}(L_{1})\big\}\Big|\leq\delta^{n-1-t}\quad\textrm{for all}\ \ \delta\in(0,2^{-k_{1}}).

To conclude the proof, let
\rho=2^{-k_{1}}
and let

L^{\prime}=L_{1}\ \backslash\ \bigcup_{k=k_{1}}^{\infty}\operatorname{dir}^{-1}\big(\{v\colon v\ \textrm{is over-represented at scale}\ 2^{-k}\}\big).\qed

With this lemma, Proposition
2.1
now follows from standard discretization arguments. The details are as follows.

Proof of Proposition
2.1
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be given. By the definition of
\sigma_{n}
, there exists
s,t\in(0,1)
so that

N(s,t)\leq\sigma_{n}+\varepsilon/2.

(2.6)

We will show that Proposition
2.1
holds with
\eta=t/4
.

Let
K
be a Kakeya set that is
\eta
-close to being sticky. Since
K
is compact, after a translation and dilation we can suppose that
K\subset[-\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{n}]^{n}
. In particular, there exists a number
w>0
and set
L\subset\mathcal{L}_{n}
with
\dim_{P}(L)\leq n-1+\eta
so that for each unit vector
v\in S^{n-1}
with final coordinate
v_{n}\geq 1/2,
there is a line
\ell\in L
with
\operatorname{dir}(\ell)=v
and
|\ell\cap K|\geq w
. Use Lemma
2.2
to select a subset
L^{\prime}\subset L
and a number
\rho_{1}>0
with
L^{\prime}\in\operatorname{QStick}(n,t/2,\rho_{1})
and
\nu_{n-1}(\operatorname{dir}(L^{\prime}))>0
.

Let
k_{1}
be an integer to specified below, with
2^{-k_{1}}<\rho_{1}^{2n/t}
. By the definition of Hausdorff dimension, we can cover
K
by a union of cubes
K\subset\bigcup_{k=k_{1}}^{\infty}\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}}Q
, where
\mathcal{Q}_{k}
is a set of
2^{-k}
-cubes with

\#\mathcal{Q}_{k}\lesssim(2^{k})^{\dim_{H}K+\varepsilon/2}.

(2.7)

For each
\ell\in L^{\prime}
, we have

K\cap\ell\ \subset\ \bigcup_{k=k_{1}}^{\infty}\Big(\ell\cap\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}}Q\Big).

Since
|K\cap\ell|\geq w
, there exists at least one index
k
so that

\Big|\ell\cap\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}}Q\Big|\geq\frac{w}{100k^{2}}.

(2.8)

For each
k\geq k_{1}
, let
L_{k}
be the set of lines
\ell\in L^{\prime}
for which (
2.8
) holds for that choice of
k
. Then
L^{\prime}=\bigcup_{k}L_{k}
, so there exists an index
k_{2}\geq k_{1}
with
\nu_{n-1}(\operatorname{dir}(L_{k_{2}}))\geq\frac{\nu_{n-1}(\operatorname{dir}(L^{\prime}))}{100k_{2}^{2}}
.

Let
\delta=2^{-k_{2}}
, so in particular
\frac{1}{k_{2}}\geq\frac{\log 2}{\log(1/\delta)}
. Let
\Omega
be a
\delta
-separated subset of
\operatorname{dir}(L_{k_{2}})
of cardinality
\gtrsim\frac{\nu_{n-1}(\operatorname{dir}L^{\prime})\delta^{1-n}}{\log(1/\delta)^{2}}
. For each direction
v\in\Omega
, let
T
be a
\delta
-tube with coaxial line
\ell\in L_{k_{2}}
pointing in direction
v
. By (
2.8
), we can choose
T
so that the set

Y(T)=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{k_{2}}\\
Q\cap\ell\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}Q

satisfies
Y(T)\subset T
and
|Y(T)|\gtrsim w\log(1/\delta)^{-2}\delta^{n-1}
.

We claim that if
k_{1}
is chosen sufficiently large, then
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
satisfies Items
(a)
,
(b)
, and
(c)
from Definition
2.1
. Item
(a)
is immediate, since
\Omega
is
\delta
-separated. Item
(c)
follows from the inequality

\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|Y(T)|\gtrsim\frac{(\#\Omega)w\delta^{n-1}}{\log(1/\delta)^{2}}\gtrsim\frac{w\nu_{n-1}(\operatorname{dir}L^{\prime})}{\log(1/\delta)^{4}}.

This quantity is larger than
\delta^{s}
provided we select
k_{1}
sufficiently small large, depending on
\nu_{n-1}(\operatorname{dir}L^{\prime}),

w
, and
n
.

Our final task is to show that
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
satisfies Item
(b)
. Let
\rho\in[\delta,1]
. Our goal is to find a set of
\rho
-tubes
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
that covers
\mathbb{T}
, so that at most
\delta^{-t}
of them are pairwise parallel. We will divide our analysis into cases.

Case 1:
\rho\in[\delta,\rho_{1}).
let
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
be a collection of
\rho
-tubes, so that the set of coaxial lines
\tilde{L}
has the following properties.

(i)

Each
\tilde{\ell}\in\tilde{L}
is contained in
N_{\rho}(L^{\prime})
.

(ii)

The
\rho/2
-neighborhoods of the lines in
\tilde{L}
cover
L^{\prime}
.

(iii)

The
\rho/6
neighborhoods of the lines in
\tilde{L}
are disjoint.

Such a collection of tubes can be chosen greedily, as in the proof of the Vitali covering lemma.

Each tube
T\in\mathbb{T}
has a coaxial line
\ell\in L_{1}\subset L^{\prime}
. Hence there is a tube
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
so that
d(\ell,\tilde{\ell})\leq\rho/2,
and thus
\tilde{T}
covers
T
. We conclude that
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
covers
\mathbb{T}
.

It remains to show that at most
\rho^{-t}
tubes from
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
can be pairwise parallel. Let
\tilde{T}_{1},\ldots\tilde{T}_{N}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
be pairwise parallel, and let
v=\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{1})
. In particular,
|v-\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{j})|\leq\rho
for each index
j
. By Item (i) above, this means
\tilde{T}_{j}\in N_{\rho}(L^{\prime})\cap\operatorname{dir}^{-1}(N_{\rho}(v))
. By item (iii) above, we conclude that

\lambda_{n}\big(N_{\rho}(L^{\prime})\cap\operatorname{dir}^{-1}(N_{\rho}(v))\big)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{n}\big(N_{\rho/6}(\tilde{\ell}_{i})\big)\gtrsim N\rho^{2n-2}.

Thus by Fubini, there exists
v^{\prime}\in N_{\rho}(v)
with

|N_{\rho}(L^{\prime})\cap\operatorname{dir}^{-1}(v^{\prime})|\gtrsim N\rho^{n-1}.

But since
L^{\prime}\in\operatorname{QStick}(n,t/2,\rho_{1})
and
\rho<\rho_{1}
, if
\rho_{1}>0
is selected sufficiently small (depending on the implicit constants above, which in turn depend on
n
) then
N\leq\rho^{-t}\leq\delta^{-t}
.

Case 2:
\rho\in[\rho_{1},1]
.
Let
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
be a set of
\rho
-tubes, whose corresponding coaxial lines form a
\rho/2
-net in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
. Then
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
clearly covers
\mathbb{T}
. The maximum number of pairwise parallel tubes in
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
is at most
\#\tilde{\mathbb{T}}\sim\rho^{2-2n}
, and this is
\leq\delta^{-t}
(provided
\rho_{1}
, and hence
\delta
is selected sufficiently small compared to
n
), since
\delta\leq 2^{-k_{1}}\leq\rho_{1}^{2n/t}
.

We have shown that
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
satisfies Items
(a)
,
(b)
, and
(c)
from Definition
2.1
. Thus we have that

M(s,t,\delta)\leq\Big|\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}}Y(T)\Big|\leq\Big|\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{k_{2}}}Q\Big|\leq(\#\mathcal{Q}_{k_{2}})\delta^{n}\leq\delta^{n-\dim K-\varepsilon/2},

where the final inequality used (
2.7
). We conclude that

\frac{\log M(s,t,\delta)}{\log\delta}\geq n-\dim_{H}K-\varepsilon/2.

(2.9)

We have just shown that for every
k_{1}
sufficiently large, there exists
\delta\in(0,2^{-k_{1}}]
for which (
2.9
) holds. In particular,

N(s,t)\geq n-\dim_{H}K-\varepsilon/2.

(2.10)

To conclude the proof, we compare (
2.6
) and (
2.10
).
∎

Proposition
2.1
says that Theorems
1.1
and
1.1
′
will follow from the estimate
\sigma_{3}=0
.

3
Extremal families of tubes and multi-scale structure

In this section, we will study families of tubes that nearly extremize the quantity
M
from Definition
2.1
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta>0
. We say a pair
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
is
\varepsilon
-extremal if it satisfies Items
(a)
,
(b)
, and
(c)
from Definition
2.1
with
s=t=\varepsilon
, and furthermore
\Big|\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}}Y(T)\Big|\leq\delta^{\sigma_{n}-\varepsilon}
.

From the definition of
\sigma_{n}
, we immediately conclude that extremal collections of tubes always exist for small
\delta
. More precisely, we have the following.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta_{0}>0
. Then there exists a
\varepsilon
-extremal collection of tubes
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
for some
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
.

The main result of this section says that extremal collections of tubes must be coarsely self-similar at every scale. To state this precisely, we need the following definition.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a collection of
\delta
tubes and let
\tilde{T}
be a
\rho
tube that covers each tube in
\mathbb{T}
. The
unit rescaling of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
relative to
\tilde{T}
is the pair
(\hat{\mathbb{T}},\hat{Y})_{\delta/\rho}
defined as follows. The coaxial lines of the tubes
\hat{T}\in\hat{\mathbb{T}}
are the images of the coaxial lines of the tubes
T\in\mathbb{T}
under the map
\phi\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, defined as follows. If
\tilde{\ell}
is the coaxial line of
\tilde{T}
, then
\phi
is the composition of a rigid transformation that sends the point
\tilde{\ell}\cap\{x_{n}=0\}
to the origin and sends the line
\tilde{\ell}
to the
x_{n}
-axis, with the anisotropic dilation

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\mapsto\big(cx_{1}/\rho,\ cx_{2}/\rho,\ldots,cx_{n-1}/\rho,\ cx_{n}\big).

(3.1)

Each shading
\hat{Y}(\hat{T})
is the union of
\delta/\rho
cubes that intersect
\phi(Y(T))
. The constant
c=c(n)\sim 1
is chosen so that the coaxial lines of the tubes in
\hat{T}
are in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
, and
\hat{Y}(\hat{T})\subset\hat{T}
. See Figure
1

Figure 1:
The unit rescaling of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
. For clarity, only a few cubes in
Y(T)
have been drawn.

We can now state the main result of this section. It says that extremal collections of tubes look self-similar at all scales.

Proposition 3.2
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta>0
and
\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a
\eta
-extremal collection of tubes, and let
\rho\in[\delta^{1-\varepsilon},\delta^{\varepsilon}]
. Then there is a refinement
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
and a balanced cover
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
of
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
with the following properties.

(i)

(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
is
\varepsilon
-extremal.

(ii)

For each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
, the unit rescaling of
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime}[\tilde{T}],Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
relative to
\tilde{T}
is
\varepsilon
-extremal.

(iii)

For each
p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\#\tilde{\mathbb{T}}(p)\leq\rho^{-\sigma_{n}-\varepsilon}

(iv)

For each
p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
,
\#\mathbb{T}^{\prime}[\tilde{T}](p)\leq(\delta/\rho)^{-\sigma_{n}-\varepsilon}.

Before proving Proposition
3.2
, we will analyze the unit rescaling of extremal collections of tubes that are covered by a single
\rho
-tube. A precise formulation is given below. In the arguments that follow, we will make frequent use of the fact that if
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
is
\varepsilon
-extremal, then
\delta^{1-n+\varepsilon}\lesssim\#\mathbb{T}\lesssim\delta^{1-n-\varepsilon},
and if
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
is a sub-collection with
\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}|Y^{\prime}(T)|\geq\delta^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
for some
\varepsilon^{\prime}\geq\varepsilon
, then
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
is
\varepsilon^{\prime}
-extremal.

Lemma 3.3
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}].
Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a set of tubes that are covered by a
\rho
-tube
\tilde{T}
, with
\rho\in[\delta^{1-\varepsilon},1]
.

Suppose that
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
satisfies Items
(a)
and
(b)
from Definition
2.1
with
\eta
in place of
s
, and that
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
satisfies the following analogue of Item
(c)
:
\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|Y(T)|\geq\delta^{\eta}\rho^{n-1}
.

Then there is a refinement
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
so that the unit rescaling of
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
relative to
\tilde{T}
satisfies Items
(a)
,
(b)
, and
(c)
from Definition
2.1
with
\varepsilon
in place of
s
and
t
. Furthermore,

\#\mathbb{T}^{\prime}(p)\leq(\rho/\delta)^{\sigma+\varepsilon}\quad\textrm{for all}\ p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(3.2)

Proof.

To begin, observe that the transformation
\phi
from Definition
3.2
has the following properties.

•

If
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
are lines with
d(\tilde{\ell},\ell_{i})\leq\rho
for
i=1,2
. Then

\begin{split}\rho^{-1}d(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\lesssim d(\phi(\ell_{1}),&\ \phi(\ell_{2}))\leq\rho^{-1}d(\ell_{1},\ell_{2}),\\
\rho^{-1}\angle\big(\operatorname{dir}(\ell_{1}),\ \operatorname{dir}(\ell_{2})\big)\lesssim\angle\big(\operatorname{dir}(\phi(\ell_{1})),\ &\operatorname{dir}(\phi(\ell_{2}))\big)\leq\rho^{-1}\angle\big(\operatorname{dir}(\ell_{1}),\ \operatorname{dir}(\ell_{2})\big).\end{split}

(3.3)

•

If
\tau\in[\delta,\rho]
and
T^{\dagger}
is a
\tau
-tube covered by
2\tilde{T}
, then
\phi(T^{\dagger})
is contained in the
\tau/\rho
tube with coaxial line
\phi(\ell^{\dagger})
.

•

For every measurable
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have

|\phi(X)|\sim\rho^{1-n}|X|.

(3.4)

By (
3.3
), there is a refinement of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
whose unit rescaling relative to
\tilde{T}
consists of essentially distinct tubes. Let
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
be a refinement with this property, which is also constant multiplicity, i.e. there is a number
\mu
so that
\#\mathbb{T}^{\prime}(p)=\mu
for all
p\in E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}
. Let
(\hat{\mathbb{T}},\hat{Y})_{\delta/\rho}
be the unit rescaling of
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
relative to
\tilde{T}
. In particular,
(\hat{\mathbb{T}},\hat{Y})_{\delta/\rho}
satisfies Property
(a)
from Definition
2.1
.

By (
3.4
), we have

\Big|\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}}Y(T)\Big|\sim\rho^{n-1}\Big|\bigcup_{\hat{T}\in\hat{\mathbb{T}}}\hat{Y}(\hat{T})\Big|.

(3.5)

Our next task is to obtain a lower bound on the RHS of (
3.5
). Let
s,t>0
be chosen so that

N(s,t)\leq\sigma_{n}+\varepsilon/4.

(3.6)

Decreasing
s
and
t
if necessary, we may suppose that
s,t\leq\varepsilon/2
. We will show that
(\hat{\mathbb{T}},\hat{Y})_{\delta/\rho}
satisfies Properties
(b)
and
(c)
from Definition
2.1
for this value of
s
and
t
, and all sufficiently small
\delta
.

Property
(c)
is straightforward: by (
3.4
) we have

\sum_{\hat{T}\in\hat{\mathbb{T}}}|\hat{Y}(\hat{T})|\gtrsim\rho^{1-n}\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|Y(T)|\geq\delta^{\eta}\geq(\rho/\delta)^{\eta/\varepsilon}.

(3.7)

If
\eta,\delta_{0}>0
are selected sufficiently small, then the LHS of (
3.7
) is at most
(\rho/\delta)^{s}
.

For property
(b)
, let
\tau\in(\delta/\rho,1)
. Let
\mathbb{T}^{*}
be a collection of
\tau\rho
-tubes that cover
\mathbb{T}
, at most
\delta^{-\eta}
of which are pairwise parallel (such a collection exists by hypothesis, since
\delta\leq\tau\rho\leq 1
). We can suppose that each
T^{*}\in\mathbb{T}^{*}
covers at least one tube from
\mathbb{T}
, and thus by the triangle inequality, each
T^{*}
is covered by
2\tilde{T}
. Define
\hat{\mathbb{T}}^{*}
to be the set of
\tau
tubes whose coaxial lines are given by
\{\phi(\ell^{*})\colon\ell^{*}\in\mathbb{T}^{*}\}
. By (
3.3
), if
T\in\mathbb{T}
is covered by
T^{*}\in\mathbb{T}^{*}
, then the corresponding tube
\hat{T}
is covered by
\hat{T}^{*}
; in particular,
\hat{\mathbb{T}}^{*}
covers
\hat{\mathbb{T}}
. By (
3.3
), if
\delta_{0}
is chosen sufficiently small then at most
(\delta/\rho)^{-2\eta/\varepsilon}
tubes from
\hat{\mathbb{T}}^{*}
are pairwise parallel. Thus if
\eta>0
is chosen sufficiently small, then
(\hat{\mathbb{T}},\hat{Y})_{\delta/\rho}
satisfies property
(b)
for the value of
t
specified in (
3.6
).

We have shown that
(\hat{\mathbb{T}},\hat{Y})_{\delta/\rho}
satisfies Properties
(a)
,
(b)
, and
(c)
from Definition
2.1
with
\varepsilon
in place of
s
and
t
.

All that remains is to establish (
3.2
), i.e. to bound
\mu\leq(\rho/\delta)^{\sigma_{n}+\varepsilon}
. We have also shown that
(\hat{\mathbb{T}},\hat{Y})_{\delta/\rho}
satisfies Properties
(a)
,
(b)
, and
(c)
from Definition
2.1
for the values of
s
and
t
specified by (
3.6
). Thus if
\delta/\rho
is sufficiently small, then

\Big|\bigcup_{\hat{T}\in\hat{\mathbb{T}}}\hat{Y}(\hat{T})\Big|\geq(\delta/\rho)^{\sigma_{n}+\varepsilon/2}.

(3.8)

We can ensure that
\delta/\rho
is sufficiently small for (
3.8
) to hold by selecting
\delta_{0}>0
appropriately, since the hypothesis
\rho\geq\delta^{1-\varepsilon}
forces
\delta/\rho\searrow 0
as
\delta\searrow 0
.

On the other hand, since
(\hat{\mathbb{T}},\hat{Y})_{\delta/\rho}
satisfies Property
(b)
from Definition
2.1
with
s\leq\varepsilon/2
, we have

\mu=\Big(\sum_{\hat{T}\in\hat{\mathbb{T}}}|\hat{Y}(\hat{T})|\Big)\Big(\Big|\bigcup_{\hat{T}\in\hat{\mathbb{T}}}\hat{Y}(\hat{T})\Big|\Big)^{-1}\lesssim(\delta/\rho)^{\varepsilon/2}\Big(\Big|\bigcup_{\hat{T}\in\hat{\mathbb{T}}}\hat{Y}(\hat{T})\Big|\Big)^{-1}\leq(\delta/\rho)^{\sigma_{n}+\varepsilon}.\qed

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Step 1: Constructing
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
.

To begin, we will construct a set of
\rho
-tubes that covers a refinement of
\mathbb{T}
, with the property that each
T\in\mathbb{T}
is contained in exactly one
\rho
-tube, and
T
is close to the coaxial line of this
\rho
-tube. Here are the details. Let
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{0}
be a set of
\rho/(2n)
tubes that covers
\mathbb{T}
, so that the coaxial line of each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{0}
coincides with the coaxial line of a tube from
\mathbb{T}
, and at most
O(\delta^{-\eta})
tubes from
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{0}
are pairwise parallel.
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{0}
can be constructed by first selecting a cover of
\mathbb{T}
as described in Item
(b)
of Definition
2.1
, and then replacing each tube
\tilde{T}
in this cover by
O(1)
tubes
\tilde{T}^{\prime}
with
d(\tilde{T},\tilde{T}^{\prime})\lesssim\rho
.

Next, let
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}\subset\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{0}
be chosen so that the tubes in
2n\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
are essentially distinct; each
T\in\mathbb{T}
is covered by at most one tube in
2n\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
; and

\sum_{\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}}\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}[\tilde{T}]}|Y(T)|\sim\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|Y(T)|.

Define
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}=2n\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
. Then
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
is a set of essentially distinct
\rho
-tubes, and hence they satisfies Property
(a)
from Definition
2.1
. Our final set
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
will be a subset of
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
, and thus it will continue to satisfy Property
(a)
from Definition
2.1
.

Next, we will show that
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
satisfies Property
(b)
from Definition
2.1
with
t=\eta/\varepsilon
. To do this, let
\tau\in[\rho,1].
Then there is a set
\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}
of
\tau
tubes that covers
\mathbb{T}
, at most
\delta^{-\eta}\leq\rho^{-\eta/\varepsilon}
of which are pairwise parallel. But since each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
shares a coaxial line with some
T\in\mathbb{T}
, and
\tau\geq\rho
,
\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}
also covers
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}.
Our final set
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
will be a subset of
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
, and thus it will continue to satisfy Property
(b)
from Definition
2.1
with this same value of
t
.

Define
\mathbb{T}_{2}\subset\mathbb{T}
to be the set of tubes that are covered by some tube from
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
, and let
Y_{2}(T)=Y(T)
for each
T\in\mathbb{T}_{2}
. Then
(\mathbb{T}_{2},Y_{2})
is a refinement of
(\mathbb{T},Y)
. Furthermore, each
T\in\mathbb{T}_{2}
is covered by exactly one tube from
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
; and if
\tilde{T}
covers
T
, then
T
is close to the coaxial line of
\tilde{T}
, in the sense that
\frac{1}{2n}\tilde{T}
also covers
T
.

Step 2: Fine scale structure
.
In this step we will analyze the structure of the sets
\mathbb{T}_{2}[\tilde{T}]
. By dyadic pigeonholing, we can select a set
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3}\subset\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
and a refinement
(\mathbb{T}_{3},Y_{3})
of
(\mathbb{T}_{2},Y_{2})
so that for each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3}
, we have

\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{3}[\tilde{T}]}|Y_{3}(T)|=(\#\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3})^{-1}\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{3}}|Y_{3}(T)|.

(3.9)

Since
O(\delta^{-\eta})
tubes from
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3}
are pairwise parallel, we have
\#\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3}\lesssim\delta^{-\eta}\rho^{1-n}
and thus
\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{3}[\tilde{T}]}|Y_{3}(T)|\gtrapprox_{\delta}\delta^{2\eta}\rho^{n-1}
.

If
\eta
and
\delta_{0}
are chosen sufficiently small, then we can apply Lemma
3.3
to each set
(\mathbb{T}_{3}[\tilde{T}],Y_{3})_{\delta}
with
\varepsilon^{2}/3
in place of
\varepsilon
. Let
(\mathbb{T}_{4},Y_{4})_{\delta}
be the union of the refined collections
\{(\mathbb{T}_{3}[\tilde{T}],Y_{3})_{\delta}\colon\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3}\}
that are the output of Lemma
3.3
. Let
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}=\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3}
. Then for each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}
, we have

\#\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}](p)\leq(\rho/\delta)^{\sigma_{n}+\varepsilon^{2}/3},

(3.10)

so in particular
(\mathbb{T}_{4},Y_{4})_{\delta}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}
satisfy Item
(iv)
from the statement of Proposition
3.2
.

Furthermore, for each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}
, the unit rescaling of
(\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}],Y_{4})_{\delta}
relative to
\tilde{T}
satisfies Items
(a)
,
(b)
, and
(c)
from Definition
2.1
with
s=t=\varepsilon^{2}
. Items
(a)
and
(b)
will continue to hold if
\mathbb{T}_{4}
is replaced by a refinement.

Step 3: Coarse scale structure
.

Thus far, we have analyzed the fine-scale behavior of the “thin” tubes
\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]
for each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}
. Our next task is to analyze the coarse-scale behavior of the “fat” tubes
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}
.

First, we will construct a shading on the fat tubes. Observe that for each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}
and each
\rho
-cube
Q
, we have that

Q\ \cap\!\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]}Y_{4}(T)\neq\emptyset\ \ \textrm{implies}\ \ Q\subset\tilde{T}.

(3.11)

Indeed, as noted at the end of Step 1, if
T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]
, then
T
is covered by
\frac{1}{2n}\tilde{T}
, and thus
Q
intersects
\frac{1}{2n}\tilde{T}
. But this implies
Q\subset\tilde{T}
.

Each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}
contains
\sim\rho^{-1}

\rho
-cubes, and for each such cube we have

\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]}|Y_{4}(T)\cap Q|\lesssim\rho\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]}|T|.

The directions
\{\operatorname{dir}(T)\colon T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]\}
are contained in a disk of radius
\rho
in
S^{n-1}
. Since at most
\delta^{-\eta}
tubes from
\mathbb{T}_{4}
can be pairwise parallel, we have

\#\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]\lesssim\delta^{-\eta}(\rho/\delta)^{n-1},

(3.12)

and thus

\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]}|Y_{4}(T)\cap Q|\lesssim\delta^{-\eta}\rho^{n}.

(3.13)

Let
c_{1}\sim 1
be a constant to be chosen below. We say a
\rho
-cube is
heavy
for the tube
\tilde{T}
if

\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]}|Y_{4}(T)\cap Q|\geq c_{1}\rho\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]}|Y_{4}(T)|.

Denote the set of heavy cubes by
\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\tilde{T})
. If the constant
c_{1}
is chosen appropriately, then for each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}
we have

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\tilde{T})}\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]}|Y_{4}(T)\cap Q|\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]}|Y_{4}(T)|.

By dyadic pigeonholing, there exists a weight
w
so that if we define

\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\tilde{T})=\Big\{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\tilde{T})\colon w\leq\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]}|Y_{4}(T)\cap Q|<2w\Big\},

(3.14)

then

\sum_{\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\tilde{T})}\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]}|Y_{4}(T)\cap Q|\gtrapprox_{\delta}\sum_{\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}}\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}[\tilde{T}]}|Y_{4}(T)|=\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}}|Y_{4}(T)|.

(3.15)

Comparing (
3.13
) and (
3.15
), we have

\sum_{\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}}\#\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\tilde{T})\gtrapprox_{\delta}\delta^{2\eta}\rho^{-n}.

(3.16)

For each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}
, define
\tilde{Y}_{4}(\tilde{T})=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\tilde{T})}Q
. By (
3.11
),
Y_{4}
is a shading of
\mathbb{T}_{4}
. (
3.16
) is precisely the statement that

\sum_{\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}}|\tilde{Y}_{4}(\tilde{T})|\gtrapprox_{\delta}\delta^{2\eta}.

(3.17)

Note that
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4},\tilde{Y}_{4})_{\rho}
satisfies Items
(a)
,
(b)
, and
(c)
from Definition
2.1
with
t=\eta/\varepsilon
and
s=3\eta/\varepsilon
.

Let
(\mathbb{T}_{5},Y_{5})_{\delta}
be a refinement of
(\mathbb{T}_{4},Y_{4})_{\delta}
and let
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5},\tilde{Y}_{5})_{\rho}
be a refinement of
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4},\tilde{Y}_{4})_{\rho}
, so that the following holds

•

There is a number
\mu_{\operatorname{fine}}
so that for each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}
and each
p\in E_{\mathbb{T}_{5}[\tilde{T}]}
, we have
\#\mathbb{T}_{5}[\tilde{T}](p)=\mu_{\operatorname{fine}}
; we do this by refining the shading
Y_{4}
to ensure that this property holds.

•

There is a number
w>0
so that for each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}
and each
\rho
-cube
Q\subset Y_{5}(\tilde{T})
, we have
|Q\cap E_{\mathbb{T}_{5}[\tilde{T}]}|=w
; we do this by refining the shading
Y_{4}
, while preserving the previous property (each
\delta
-cube is either preserved or deleted from all shadings in
\mathbb{T}_{5}[\tilde{T}]
)

•

There is a number
\mu_{\operatorname{coarse}}
so that for each
p\in E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}}
we have
\#\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}(p)=\mu_{\operatorname{coarse}}
; we do this by refining the shading
\tilde{Y}_{4}
, while preserving the previous properties.

•

(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5},\tilde{Y}_{5})_{\rho}
covers
(\mathbb{T}_{5},Y_{5})_{\delta}
.

Since
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5},\tilde{Y}_{5})_{\rho}
satisfies Items
(a)
,
(b)
, and
(c)
from Definition
2.1
with
t=\eta/\varepsilon
and
s=4\eta/\varepsilon
, if
\eta
and
\delta_{0}
are chosen sufficiently small (which forces
\rho
to be sufficiently small), then
|E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}}|\geq\rho^{\sigma_{n}+\varepsilon/2}
, and thus

\mu_{\operatorname{coarse}}\lesssim\rho^{-\sigma_{n}-\varepsilon/2-\eta/\varepsilon}.

(3.18)

Thus if
\eta
is chosen sufficiently small, then Item
(iii)
from the statement of Proposition
3.2
holds.

At this point,
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5},\tilde{Y}_{5})_{\rho}
is a balanced cover of
(\mathbb{T}_{5},Y_{5})_{\delta}
, and this pair satisfies Items
(iv)
and
(iii)
. In addition,
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5},\tilde{Y}_{5})_{\rho}
satisfies Items
(a)
,
(b)
, and
(c)
from Definition
2.1
with
t=\eta/\varepsilon
and
s=4\eta/\varepsilon
, and for each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}
, the unit rescaling of
(\mathbb{T}_{5}[\tilde{T}],Y_{5})_{\delta}
relative to
\tilde{T}
satisfies Items
(a)
and
(b)
from Definition
2.1
with
t=\varepsilon^{2}.

Next we will analyze the multiplicities
\mu_{\operatorname{fine}}
and
\mu_{\operatorname{coarse}}
defined above. (
3.18
) gives an upper bound on
\mu_{\operatorname{coarse}}
, while by (
3.10
) we have
\mu_{\operatorname{fine}}\leq(\rho/\delta)^{\sigma_{n}+\varepsilon^{2}/3}\leq\rho^{-\varepsilon/3}(\rho/\delta)^{\sigma_{n}}
. On the other hand,

\int\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{5}}\chi_{Y_{5}(T)}(p)dp\gtrapprox_{\delta}\delta^{\eta}.

(3.19)

Since the original collection of tubes
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
is
\eta
-extremal, the integrand in (
5.20
) is supported on a set of size at most
\delta^{\sigma_{n}-\eta}
, and it is pointwise bounded by
\mu_{\operatorname{fine}}\mu_{\operatorname{coarse}}
. We conclude that

\mu_{\operatorname{fine}}\gtrapprox_{\delta}\delta^{2\eta}\rho^{\varepsilon/2+\eta/\varepsilon}(\rho/\delta)^{\sigma_{n}},\quad\mu_{\operatorname{coarse}}\gtrapprox_{\delta}\delta^{2\eta}\rho^{-\sigma_{n}+\varepsilon/3}.

(3.20)

Selecting
\eta>0
sufficiently small, we conclude that
|E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}}|\leq\rho^{\sigma_{n}-\varepsilon}
, and for each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}
, the unit-rescaling
(\hat{\mathbb{T}},\hat{Y})_{\delta/\rho}
of each set
(\mathbb{T}_{5}[\tilde{T}],Y_{5})_{\delta}
satisfies
|E_{\hat{T}}|\leq(\delta/\rho)^{\sigma_{n}-\varepsilon}
.

We are almost done, except it is possible that the unit-rescaling described above might fail to satisfy

\sum_{\hat{T}\in\hat{\mathbb{T}}}|\hat{Y}(\hat{T})|\geq(\rho/\delta)^{\varepsilon}

(3.21)

for some choices of
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}
. To fix this problem, we define
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{6}\subset\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}
to be the set of tubes for which (
3.21
) holds. We define
\tilde{Y}_{6}=\tilde{Y}_{5}
,
Y_{6}=Y_{5}
, and
\mathbb{T}_{6}=\bigcup_{\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{6}}\mathbb{T}_{5}[\tilde{T}]
. Then
(\mathbb{T}_{6},Y_{6})
is a refinement of
(\mathbb{T},Y)
which, together with
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{6},\tilde{Y}_{6})_{\rho}
, satisfies the conclusions of Proposition
3.2

∎

Remark 3.1
.

The conclusions of Lemma
3.1
and Proposition
3.2
are the only consequences of stickiness that we will use to prove Theorem
1.1
. In particular, if Lemma
3.1
and Proposition
3.2
hold for some other class of Kakeya sets, then it should be possible to prove the analogue of Theorem
1.1
in that setting as well.

We conclude this section with a few final observations about extremal collections of tubes. First, if
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
is an extremal collection of tubes, then the directions of the tubes passing through a typical point cannot focus too tightly. This phenomena is sometimes called “robust transversality.” We will record a precise version below.

Lemma 3.4
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta>0
and
\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a
\eta
-extremal collection of tubes. Then after replacing
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
by a refinement, for each
p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and each
v\in S^{n-1}
, we have

\#\{T\in\mathbb{T}(p)\colon\angle(v,\operatorname{dir}(T)<\delta^{\varepsilon}\}\leq\delta^{-\sigma_{n}+\varepsilon\sigma_{n}/2}.

(3.22)

Proof.

Apply Proposition
3.2
with
\varepsilon\sigma_{n}/2
in place of
\varepsilon
, and
\rho=\delta^{\varepsilon}
. Since the resulting collection
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
of tubes are essentially distinct, the
\delta
-tubes contributing to (
3.22
) must be covered by
O(1)

\rho
-tubes from
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
. (
3.22
) now follows from Item
(i)
from Proposition
3.2
.
∎

The next lemma says that extremal collections of tubes remain extremal after a mild re-scaling. The proof is straightforward (though somewhat tedious), and is omitted.

Lemma 3.5
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta>0
and
\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a
\eta
-extremal collection of tubes. Let
Q\subset[-1,1]^{n}
be an axis-parallel rectangular prism, and suppose
\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|Y(T)\cap Q|\geq\delta^{\eta}
. Let
\phi
be a translation composed with a dilation of the form
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\mapsto(r_{1}x_{1},\ldots,r_{n}x_{n})
, where
\delta^{\eta}\leq r_{i}\leq\delta^{-\eta}
, and suppose
\phi(Q)\subset[-1,1]^{n}
.

Then the image of
(\mathbb{T},Y\cap Q)_{\delta}
under
\phi
is
\varepsilon
-extremal. More precisely, there exists an
\varepsilon
-extremal collection of tubes
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
for some
\delta\leq\rho\leq\delta^{1-\eta}
so that each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
has a coaxial line of the form
\phi(\ell)
, where
\ell
is the coaxial line of a tube
T\in\mathbb{T}
. Furthermore, for each such pair
T,\tilde{T}
, we have that
\tilde{Y}(\tilde{T})
is the set of
\rho
-cubes that intersect
\phi(Y(T)\cap Q).

4
Planiness and graininess

For the remainder of the paper we will specialize to the case
n=3
, and we will define
\sigma=\sigma_{3}
. Our goal is to prove that
\sigma=0
. In this section we will establish the existence of extremal collections of tubes
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
with additional structural properties. The first of these is the existence of a Lipschitz plane map.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a set of
\delta
-tubes. A
plane map
for
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
is a function
V\colon E_{\mathbb{T}}\to S^{2}
so that

|\operatorname{dir}(T)\cdot V(p)|\leq\delta\quad\textrm{for all}\ p\in E_{\mathbb{T}},\ T\in\mathbb{T}(p).

Next, we will show that each
\{z=z_{0}\}
slice of the Kakeya set is contained in a union of long thin rectangles, which we will call global grains. These global grains will be arranged like an Ahlfors-David regular set. The specific property we need is the following.

Definition 4.2
.

For
n\geq 1,\ \alpha\in(0,n]
,
C\geq 1
and
\delta>0
, We say a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
(\delta,\alpha,C)_{n}
-ADset if for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, all
\rho\geq\delta
, and all
r\geq\rho
, we have

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}(E\cap B(x,r))\leq C(r/\rho)^{\alpha}.

(4.1)

Remark 4.1
.

Note that if
E
is a
(\delta,\alpha,C)_{n}
-ADset, then so is every subset of
E
. This observation will be used extentensively in the arguments that follows. Definition
4.2
only imposes upper bounds on the size of
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}(E\cap B(x,r))
, while Ahlfors regularity usually requires a matching lower bound. However, if
E
is a
(\delta,\alpha,C)_{n}
-ADset that has bounded diameter and
\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(E)
has size roughly
\delta^{-\alpha}
, then a large subset of
E
will satisfy a lower bound analogue of (
4.1
).

With these definitions, we can now state the main result of this section.

Proposition 4.1
.

For all
\varepsilon,\delta_{0}>0
, there exists
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
and a
\varepsilon
-extremal set of tubes
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
with the following two properties.

1.

E_{\mathbb{T}}
is a union of global grains with Lipschitz slope function.

There is a 1-Lipschitz function
f\colon[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
so that
\big(E_{\mathbb{T}}\cap\{z=z_{0}\}\big)\cdot(1,f(z_{0}),0)
is a
(\delta,1-\sigma,\delta^{-\varepsilon})_{1}
-ADset for each
z_{0}\in[-1,1]
.

2.

E_{\mathbb{T}}
is a union of local grains with Lipschitz plane map

(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
has a 1-Lipschitz plane map
V
. For all
\rho\in[\delta,1]
and all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
V(p)\cdot\big(B(p,\rho^{1/2})\cap E_{\mathbb{T}}\big)
is a
(\rho,1-\sigma,\delta^{-\varepsilon})_{1}
-ADset.

Remark 4.2
.

Item
1
implies that each slice
E_{\mathbb{T}}\cap\{z=z_{0}\}
is contained in a union of at most
\delta^{\sigma-1-\epsilon}
rectangles of dimensions roughly
1\times\delta
, and thus by Fubini,
|E_{\mathbb{T}}|\lesssim\delta^{\sigma-\epsilon}
. On the other hand, this containment is nearly sharp, since
|E_{\mathbb{T}}|\geq\delta^{\sigma+\varepsilon}
.

Item
2
implies that each ball
B(p,\rho^{1/2})\cap N_{\rho}(E_{\mathbb{T}})
is a union of at most
\rho^{\sigma/2-1/2}
parallel rectangular slabs of dimensions roughly
\rho^{1/2}\times\rho^{1/2}\times\rho
. Since
E_{\mathbb{T}}
can be covered by
\delta^{-O(\varepsilon)}\rho^{\sigma/2-3/2}
balls of radius
\rho^{1/2}
, this implies that
|N_{\rho}(E_{\mathbb{T}})|\lesssim\delta^{-O(\varepsilon)}\rho^{\sigma}
. On the other hand, this containment is nearly sharp, since
|N_{\rho}(E_{\mathbb{T}})|\gtrsim\delta^{-O(\varepsilon)}\rho^{\sigma}
.

Our main tool for proving Proposition
4.1
will be the multilinear Kakeya theorem of Bennett-Carbery-Tao
[
1
]
. The version stated below can be found in
[
6
]
.

Theorem 4.2

(Multilinear Kakeya)
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a set of
\delta
-tubes in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Then

\int\Big(\sum_{T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\in\mathbb{T}}\chi_{T_{1}}\chi_{T_{2}}\chi_{T_{3}}|v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3}|\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim\big(\delta^{2}\#\mathbb{T})^{3/2},

(4.2)

where in the above expression,
v_{i}=\operatorname{dir}(T_{i})
is a unit vector pointing in the direction of the tube
T_{i}
, and
|v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3}|
is the area of the parallelepiped spanned by
v_{1},v_{2}
, and
v_{3}
.

4.1
Finding a plane map

In this section, we will show that every extremal set of tubes has a refinement with a plane map. First, we will show that every extremal set of tubes has a refinement that “weakly” agrees with a plane map.

Lemma 4.3
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a
\eta
-extremal set of
\delta
-tubes. Then there is a refinement
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
and a function
V\colon E_{\mathbb{T}}\to S^{2}
with the following property: for each
p\in E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}
, we have

|V(p)\cdot\operatorname{dir}(T)|\leq\delta^{\sigma/2}\quad\textrm{for all}\ T\in\mathbb{T}^{\prime}(p).

(4.3)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can suppose that
\varepsilon\leq\sigma/4
. We will select
\eta
sufficiently small to ensure that
\eta\leq\min(\varepsilon/40,\ \varepsilon\sigma/24)
. Apply Lemma
3.4
to
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
with
\varepsilon/4
in place of
\varepsilon
to obtain a refinement
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})_{\delta}
so that for each
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and each unit vector
v\in S^{2}
, we have

\#\{T\in\mathbb{T}_{1}(p)\colon\angle(v,\operatorname{dir}(T))\leq\delta^{\varepsilon/4}\}\leq\delta^{-\sigma+(\varepsilon/4)\sigma/2}\leq\delta^{-\sigma+3\eta}.

(4.4)

Let
(\mathbb{T}_{2},Y_{2})
be a constant multiplicity refinement of
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})
, with multiplicity
\delta^{-\sigma+2\eta}\lessapprox\mu\lesssim\delta^{-\sigma-2\eta}
.
By Theorem
4.2
we have

\int\Big(\sum_{T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\in\mathbb{T}_{2}}\chi_{Y_{2}(T_{1})}\chi_{Y_{2}(T_{1})}\chi_{Y_{2}(T_{3})}|v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3}|\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim\big(\delta^{2}\#\mathbb{T}_{2})^{3/2}\lesssim\delta^{-(3/2)\eta}.

(4.5)

We say a point
p\in E_{\mathbb{T}_{2}}
is
broad
if

\#\{(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})\in(\mathbb{T}_{2}(p))^{3}\colon|v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3}|\geq\delta^{\sigma-10\eta}\}\geq\delta^{-3\sigma+3\eta},

otherwise we say it is narrow (in the above expression and the expressions to follow, we set
v_{i}=\operatorname{dir}(T_{i})
). Both the set of broad and the set of narrow points are unions of
\delta
-cubes. Observe that each broad point contributes at least
\delta^{-\sigma-(7/2)\eta}
to the integrand on the LHS of (
4.5
), and thus by (
4.5
) the measure of the set of broad points
\lesssim\delta^{\sigma+2\eta}
. On the other hand,
E_{\mathbb{T}_{2}}
has measure
\gtrapprox\delta^{\sigma+\eta}
.

Define
Y_{3}(T)=\{p\in Y_{2}(T)\colon p\ \textrm{narrow}\}
, and define
\mathbb{T}_{3}=\mathbb{T}_{2}
. Then it is still true that
\#\mathbb{T}_{3}(p)=\mu
for each
p\in E_{\mathbb{T}_{3}}
, and
|E_{\mathbb{T}_{3}}|\geq\frac{1}{2}|E_{\mathbb{T}_{2}}|
.

For each
p\in E_{\mathbb{T}_{3}}
, we have

\#\{(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})\in\mathbb{T}_{3}(p)^{3}\colon|v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3}|<\delta^{\sigma-10\eta}\}\geq\mu^{3}-\delta^{-3\sigma+3\eta}\geq\frac{1}{2}\mu^{3}.

(4.6)

On the other hand, by (
4.4
), for each
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
with
i\neq j
we have

\#\{(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})\in\mathbb{T}_{3}(p)^{3}\colon|v_{i}\wedge v_{j}|<\delta^{\varepsilon/4}\}\leq\mu^{2}\delta^{-\sigma+3\eta}\leq\frac{1}{100}\mu^{3}.

(4.7)

Comparing (
4.6
) and (
4.7
), we conclude that there are at least
\mu^{3}/4
triples
(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})
with
|v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3}|<\delta^{\sigma-2\eta}\leq\delta^{\sigma-\varepsilon/4}
and
|v_{i}\wedge v_{j}|\geq\delta^{\varepsilon/4}
for each
i\neq j
. By pigeonholing, there exists
T_{1},T_{2}
so that there are at least
\mu/4
choices of
T_{3}
so that
(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})
is a triple of the above form. Define

V(p)=\frac{\operatorname{dir}(T_{1})\times\operatorname{dir}(T_{2})}{|\operatorname{dir}(T_{1})\times\operatorname{dir}(T_{2})|}.

Then for each
T_{3}
of the type described above, we have

|V(p)\cdot v(T_{3})|=\frac{|\operatorname{dir}(T_{1})\wedge\operatorname{dir}(T_{2})\wedge\operatorname{dir}(T_{3})|}{|\operatorname{dir}(T_{1})\times\operatorname{dir}(T_{2})|}\leq\frac{\delta^{\sigma-10\eta}}{\delta^{\varepsilon/4}}\leq\delta^{\sigma/2}.

(4.8)

Finally, let
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}=\mathbb{T}_{3}
, and define the shading

Y^{\prime}(T)=\{p\in Y_{3}(T)\colon|V(p)\cdot v(T)|\leq\delta^{\sigma/2}\}.

(4.9)

(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y)_{\delta}
is a refinement of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
that satisfies (
4.3
).
∎

The next lemma shows that by moving to a coarser scale, we can ensure that a suitable thickening of the tubes from Lemma
4.3
strongly agree with their plane map.

Lemma 4.4
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a
\eta
-extremal set of
\delta
-tubes, and let
\rho\in[\delta^{1-\varepsilon},\delta^{\varepsilon}]
. Suppose there exists a function
V\colon E_{\mathbb{T}}\to S^{2}
so that for each
p\in E_{\mathbb{T}}
, we have

|V(p)\cdot v(T)|\leq\rho/2\quad\textrm{for all}\ T\in\mathbb{T}^{\prime}(p).

(4.10)

Then there exists a refinement
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
and a
\varepsilon
-extremal set of
\rho
-tubes
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
with plane map
\tilde{V}
so that
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
covers
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
. Furthermore,
\tilde{V}
is constant on each
\rho
-cube, and
V
and
\tilde{V}
are consistent on each
\rho
-cube, in the sense that there exists
p\in Q
with
\tilde{V}(Q)=V(p)
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon_{1}>0
be a small constant to be chosen below. We will select
\eta
very small compared to
\varepsilon_{1}
, and
\varepsilon_{1}
small compared to
\varepsilon
. Apply Proposition
3.2
to
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
, with
\varepsilon_{1}
in place of
\varepsilon
and with
\rho
as stated in the lemma. We obtain a refinement
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})_{\delta}
of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
and a
\varepsilon_{1}
-extremal collection of
\rho
-tubes
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1},\tilde{Y}_{1})_{\rho}
that covers
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})_{\delta}
.

After refining
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1},\tilde{Y}_{1})_{\rho}
(which induces a refinement on
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})_{\delta}
), we may suppose that for each
\rho
-cube
Q\subset E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}}
, there is a point
p_{Q}\in Q
with
\#\mathbb{T}_{1}(p_{Q})\gtrapprox\delta^{-\sigma+2\eta}
. We say the tube
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
is
associated
to the
\rho
-cube
Q
if
p_{Q}\in\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{1}[\tilde{T}]}Y_{1}(T)
; this necessarily implies
Q\subset\tilde{Y}_{1}(\tilde{T})
. By Item
(iv)
from Proposition
3.2
, each
\rho
-cube
Q\subset E_{\tilde{T}_{1}}
satisfies

\#\{\tilde{T}\in\tilde{T}_{1}\colon\tilde{T}\ \textrm{associated to}\ Q\}\gtrapprox\frac{\delta^{-\sigma+2\eta}}{(\rho/\delta)^{\sigma+\varepsilon_{1}}}\gtrapprox\delta^{2\eta+\varepsilon_{1}}\rho^{-\sigma}.

On the other hand, by Item
(iii)
from Proposition
3.2
, the number of tubes in
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
whose shading contains
Q
is at most
\rho^{-\sigma-\varepsilon_{1}}
. Thus if we define
\tilde{Y}_{2}(\tilde{T})
to be the union of
\rho
-cubes associated to
\tilde{T}
and define
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}=\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
then

\sum_{\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}|\tilde{Y}_{2}(\tilde{T})|\gtrapprox\delta^{2\eta+2\varepsilon_{1}}\sum_{\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}}|\tilde{Y}_{1}(T)|.

(4.11)

Selecting
\varepsilon_{1}
sufficiently small, we can ensure that the LHS of (
4.11
) is at least
\rho^{\varepsilon}
.

Next, we shall define the function
\tilde{V}(p)\colon E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}\to S^{2}
as follows. For each
\rho
-cube
Q\subset E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}
, we define
\tilde{V}(p)=V(p_{Q})
for all
p\in Q
. Clearly
\tilde{V}
is constant on
\rho
-cubes, and satisfies the consistency condition with
V
stated in Lemma
4.4
.
It remains to show that
\tilde{V}
is a plane map for
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2},\tilde{Y}_{2})_{\rho}.
Let
p\in E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}
be contained in a
\rho
-cube
Q
, and let
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}(p)
. Then there exists a tube
T\in\mathbb{T}_{2}(T)\cap\mathbb{T}(p)
. We have

|\tilde{V}(p)\cdot\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T})|=|V(p_{Q})\cdot\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T})|\leq|V(p_{Q})\cdot\operatorname{dir}(T)|+|V(p_{Q})\cdot(\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T})-\operatorname{dir}(T)|\leq\rho/2+|\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T})-\operatorname{dir}(T)|\leq\rho,

where the final inequality used (
4.10
) and the observation that
|\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T})-\operatorname{dir}(T)|\leq\rho/2
(since
\tilde{T}
covers
T
).
∎

4.2
Lipschitz regularity of the plane map

In this section we will show that if an extremal collection of tubes has a plane map, then after restricting to a sub-collection, this plane map must be Lipschitz with controlled Lipschitz norm.

Lemma 4.5
.

For all
\varepsilon>0,
there exists
\eta>0,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a
\eta
-extremal collection of tubes with plane map
V
, and let
\rho\in[\delta^{1-\varepsilon},\delta^{\varepsilon}]
. Then there exists a
\varepsilon
-extremal sub-collection
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
, so that if
p,q\in E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}
are contained in a common
\rho
-cube, then
|V(p)-V(q)|\leq\rho
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}>0
be small constants to be chosen below. We will select
\eta
very small compared to
\varepsilon_{1}
,
\varepsilon_{1}
very small compared to
\varepsilon_{2}
, and
\varepsilon_{2}
very small compared to
\varepsilon
.

Let
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})_{\delta}
be the refinement obtained by applying Lemma
3.4
to
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
with
\varepsilon_{1}
in place of
\varepsilon
. Next, apply Proposition
3.2
to
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{2})_{\delta}
with
\varepsilon_{2}
in place of
\varepsilon
, and
\rho
as specified in the lemma. We obtain a refinement
(\mathbb{T}_{2},Y_{2})_{\delta}
of
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})
and a
\varepsilon_{2}
-extremal set of
\rho
-tubes
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
.

After refining
(\mathbb{T}_{2},Y_{2})_{\delta}
and
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
, we may suppose that
(\mathbb{T}_{2},Y_{2})_{\delta}
has constant multiplicity
\mu\gtrapprox\delta^{-\sigma+2\eta}
, and
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
is still a balanced cover of
(\mathbb{T}_{2},Y_{2})_{\delta}
. If
\eta>0
is selected sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\sigma
), then for each
p\in E_{\mathbb{T}_{2}}
, there are at least
\mu^{2}/2
pairs
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{2}(p)
with
\angle(\operatorname{dir}(T_{1}),\operatorname{dir}(T_{2}))\geq\delta^{\varepsilon_{1}}
. Thus by Item
(iv)
from Proposition
3.2
, there are at least
(\mu^{2}/2)/\big((\rho/\delta)^{\sigma+\varepsilon_{2}}\big)^{2}\gtrapprox\delta^{4\eta+2\varepsilon_{2}}\rho^{-2\sigma}
pairs
\tilde{T}_{1},\tilde{T}_{2}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
with
p\in\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{2}[\tilde{T}]}Y_{3}(T)
,
p\in\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{2}[\tilde{T}^{\prime}]}Y_{3}(T)
, and
\angle\big(\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{1}),\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{2})\big)\geq\delta^{\varepsilon_{1}}-2\rho
; we will choose
\varepsilon_{1}
sufficiently small so that
\delta^{\varepsilon_{1}}-2\rho\geq\delta^{\varepsilon_{1}}/2
. In particular, for each
\rho
-cube
Q\subset E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}}
, we have

\begin{split}\Big|\Big\{(p,\tilde{T},\tilde{T}^{\prime})\in(Q\cap E_{\mathbb{T}_{3}})\times\tilde{\mathbb{T}}^{2}\colon p\in\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{2}[\tilde{T}]}Y_{3}(T)\cap\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{2}[\tilde{T}^{\prime}]}Y_{3}(T),\ &\angle\big(\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{1}),\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{2})\big)\geq\delta^{\varepsilon_{1}}/2\Big\}\Big|\\
&\gtrapprox|Q\cap E_{\mathbb{T}_{3}}|\delta^{4\eta+2\varepsilon_{2}}\rho^{-2\sigma}\\
&\gtrapprox\delta^{4\eta+4\varepsilon_{2}}|Q\cap E_{\mathbb{T}_{3}}|\big(\#\tilde{\mathbb{T}}(Q)\big)^{2},\end{split}

(4.12)

where
|\cdot|
on the LHS of (
4.12
) denotes the product of Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and counting measure, and the final inequality used Item
(iii)
from Proposition
3.2
. Thus by pigeonholing, we can select two tubes from
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}
, which we will denote by
\tilde{T}_{Q}
and
\tilde{T}^{\prime}_{Q},
with
\angle\big(\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{1}),\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{2})\big)\geq\delta^{\varepsilon_{1}}/2,
so that

\Big|Q\ \cap\Big(\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{2}[\tilde{T}_{Q}]}Y_{3}(T)\Big)\cap\Big(\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{2}[\tilde{T}_{Q}^{\prime}]}Y_{3}(T)\Big)\Big|\gtrapprox\delta^{4\eta+4\varepsilon_{2}}|Q\cap E_{\mathbb{T}_{3}}|.

(4.13)

\begin{overpic}[figures/figLipInCube]
\put(15.0,57.0){$p$}
\put(10.0,52.0){$Q$}
\put(70.0,35.0){$\tilde{T}_{Q}$}
\put(67.0,68.0){$\tilde{T}_{Q}^{\prime}$}
\put(55.0,24.0){$\tilde{T}_{p}$}
\put(55.0,78.0){$\tilde{T}_{p}^{\prime}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 2:
V(p)
is almost orthogonal to
\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{Q})
and
\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{Q}^{\prime})

Define
F_{Q}
to be the set on the LHS of (
4.13
). Let
\mathbb{T}_{3}=\mathbb{T}_{2}
, and for each
T\in\mathbb{T}_{3}
define
