Y_{3}(T)=Y_{2}(T)\cap\bigcup_{Q}F_{Q}.

Then
\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{3}}|Y_{3}(T)|\gtrapprox\delta^{4\eta+4\varepsilon_{2}}
. Let
Q
be a
\rho
-cube, and let
p\in E_{\mathbb{T}_{3}}\cap Q
. Then there exists
T_{p}\in\mathbb{T}_{3}[\tilde{T}_{Q}]
and
T_{p}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{3}[\tilde{T}_{Q}^{\prime}]
so that
p\in Y_{3}(T_{p})\cap Y_{3}(T_{p}^{\prime})
, and hence

|V(p)\cdot\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{Q})|\leq|V(p)\cdot\operatorname{dir}(T_{p})|+|V(p)\cdot(\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{Q})-\operatorname{dir}(T_{p}))|\leq\delta+\rho,

and similarly for
|V(p)\cdot\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}^{\prime}_{Q})|
. See Figure
2
. We conclude that for all
p\in Q
, we have

\angle\Big(V(p),\ \frac{\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{Q})\times\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}^{\prime}_{Q})}{|\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{Q})\times\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}^{\prime}_{Q})|}\Big)\lesssim\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\rho.

(4.14)

(
4.14
) says that
(\mathbb{T}_{3},Y_{3})
almost satisfies the conclusion of Lemma
4.5
, except the RHS of (
4.14
) is
\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\rho
rather than
\rho
. To fix this, we will pigeonhole to select a set
E_{4}\subset\mathbb{T}_{T_{4}}
that is a union of
\delta
-cubes so that (i): for each
\rho
-cube
Q
,
\angle(V(p),V(q))\leq\rho
for all
p,q\in Q
, and (ii) if we define
Y_{4}(T)=Y_{3}(T)\cap E_{4}
and
\mathbb{T}_{4}=\mathbb{T}_{3}
, then
\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{4}}|Y_{4}(T)|\gtrsim\delta^{2\varepsilon_{1}}\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{3}}|Y_{3}(T)|
. If we choose
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\eta
sufficiently small, then
(\mathbb{T}_{4},Y_{4})_{\delta}
will be
\varepsilon
-extremal.
∎

Applying Lemma
4.5
iteratively
O(1)
times to various translates of the standard grid, we obtain the following

Corollary 4.6
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta>0,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a
\eta
-extremal collection of tubes with plane map
V
, and let
\rho\in[\delta^{1-\varepsilon},\delta^{\varepsilon}]
. Then there exists a
\varepsilon
-extremal sub-collection
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
, so that if
p,q\in E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}
satisfy
|p-q|\leq\rho
, then
|V(p)-V(q)|\leq 2\rho
.

Corollary
4.6
says that after restricting to a sub-collection, the plane map
V
will be Lipschitz at a specific scale
\rho
. The next lemma iterates this result at many scales.

Lemma 4.7
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta>0,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a
\eta
-extremal collection of tubes with plane map
V
that is constant on
\delta
-cubes. Then there exists a
\varepsilon
-extremal sub-collection
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
so that
V
is
\delta^{-\varepsilon}
-Lipschitz on
E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

After a refinement, we can suppose that if
p,q\in E_{\mathbb{T}}
satisfy
|p-q|\leq\delta
, then
p
and
q
are contained in a common
\delta
-cube, and hence
V(p)=V(q)
. Let
N=2/\varepsilon
. If
\eta,\delta_{0}
are selected sufficiently small, then we can apply Corollary
4.6
iteratively to
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
for
\rho=\rho_{j}=\delta^{j/N},

j=1,\ldots,N-1
, and the resulting sub-collection
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
will be
\varepsilon
-extremal. We claim that
V
is
2\delta^{-1/N}
-Lipschitz on
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
. To see this, let
p,q\in E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}
. If
|p-q|\leq\delta
then
V(p)=V(q)
and we are done. If
|p-q|>\delta^{1/N},
then
|V(p)-V(q)|\leq 2
, and again we are done. Otherwise, there is an index
1\leq k\leq N-1
so that
\delta^{(k+1)/N}<|p-q|\leq\delta^{k/N}
, and

|V(p)-V(q)|\leq\delta^{k/N}\leq\delta^{-1/N}|p-q|.\qed

Combining the results of this section with Lemma
3.1
, we have the following

Lemma 4.8
.

For all
\varepsilon,\delta_{0}>0
, there exists
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
and a
\varepsilon
-extremal set of tubes
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
that has a
\delta^{-\varepsilon}
-Lipschitz plane map.

4.3
Grains

In this section, we will show that if an extremal set of tubes has a Lipschitz plane map, then after restricting to a sub-collection, the tubes arrange themselves into parallel slabs, which are called grains. In what follows,
V
will be a plane map for a collection of tubes. If
Q
is a cube with center
p
, we define
V(Q)=V(p_{Q})
(this is a unit vector in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
), and we define the projection
\pi_{Q}(p)=p\cdot V(Q)
.

Our goal is to show that the set of grains inside a cube arrange themselves into a AD-regular set, in the sense of Definition
4.2
. As a first step, the next lemma controls how many grains can occur inside a cube at a given scale.

Lemma 4.9
.

For all
\varepsilon>0,
there exists
\eta>0,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a
\eta
-extremal collection of tubes with
\delta^{-\eta}
-Lipschitz plane map
V
. Let
\rho\in[\delta^{1-\varepsilon},\delta^{\varepsilon}]
and let
\tau\in[\rho^{1-\varepsilon},\rho^{1/2}]
.

Then there exists a
\varepsilon
-extremal sub-collection
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
, so that for each
\tau
-cube
Q
, we have

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\Big(V(Q)\cdot\big(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\cap Q\big)\Big)\leq\rho^{-\varepsilon}(\tau/\rho)^{1-\sigma}.

(4.15)

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}>0
be small constants to be chosen below. We will select
\eta
very small compared to
\varepsilon_{1}
,
\varepsilon_{1}
very small compared to
\varepsilon_{2}
,
\varepsilon_{2}
very small compared to
\varepsilon_{3}
, and
\varepsilon_{3}
very small compared to
\varepsilon
.

Applying Proposition
3.2
to
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
with
\varepsilon_{1}/2
in place of
\varepsilon
, and then again to the output
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
with
\tau
in place of
\rho
and
\varepsilon_{2}
in place of
\varepsilon
, we obtain a refinement
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})_{\delta}
of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
that is covered by a
\varepsilon_{1}
-extremal set
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
, which in turn is covered by a
\varepsilon_{2}
-extremal set
(\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{T}},\accentset{\approx}{Y})_{\tau}
. Note that
V
is also a plane map for
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
and
(\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{T}},\accentset{\approx}{Y})_{\tau}
.

After refining
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})_{\delta}
and
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
, we can ensure that
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
is a balanced cover of
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})_{\delta}
. In the arguments below, we will find a sub-collection of
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
with certain favorable properties. Since
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
is a balanced cover of
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})_{\delta}
, this sub-collection of
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
will induce a sub-collection of
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})_{\delta}
of the same relative density.

Apply Lemma
3.4
to
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
, with
\varepsilon_{3}
in place of
\varepsilon
. After further refinement, we can find a collection
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1},\tilde{Y}_{1})_{\rho}
with the following property (
P
): For each
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}
and each
\tau
-cube
Q
that intersects
\tilde{Y}_{1}(\tilde{T})
, we have
|Q\cap\tilde{Y}_{1}(\tilde{T})|\geq\rho^{2+2\varepsilon_{1}}\tau
(for comparison, the maximal size of an intersection of the form
\tilde{T}\cap Q
is bounded by
100\rho^{2}\tau
).

Let
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2},\tilde{Y}_{2})_{\rho}
be a constant multiplicity refinement of
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1},\tilde{Y}_{1})_{\rho}
, and let
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3},\tilde{Y}_{3})_{\rho}
be a refinement of
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2},\tilde{Y}_{2})_{\rho}
that again has property (P). The reason for the above refinements is as follows: If
\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3}
and if
Q
is a
\tau
-cube that intersects
\tilde{Y}_{3}(\tilde{T})
, then
|Q\cap\tilde{Y}_{3}(\tilde{T})|\geq\rho^{2+2\varepsilon_{1}}\tau
. Thus we can choose a set of points
p_{1},\ldots p_{k}\in Q\cap\tilde{Y}_{3}(\tilde{T})
that are
\geq\rho^{1-\varepsilon_{3}}
separated, with
k\gtrsim\rho^{2\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{3}}\rho^{-1}\tau
. For each such point
p_{i}
, we have
\#\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}(p_{i})=\mu\gtrapprox\rho^{-\sigma+\varepsilon_{1}}
. But since we refined
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
using Lemma
3.4
, if
\varepsilon_{1}
is sufficiently small compared to
\varepsilon_{3}
, then there is a tube
\tilde{T}_{i}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}(p_{i})
with
\angle(\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}),\operatorname{dir}(\tilde{T}_{i}))\geq\rho^{\varepsilon_{3}}
.

We claim that the union of the sets
\{Q\cap\tilde{Y}(\tilde{T}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k}
has large volume. Since the points
p_{i}
are
\rho^{1-\varepsilon_{3}}
separated and the tubes
\tilde{T}_{i}
make angle
\geq\rho^{\varepsilon_{3}}
with
\tilde{T}
, we can label the points
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}
so that
\operatorname{dist}\big(\tilde{T}_{i}\cap\tilde{T},\ \tilde{T}_{j}\cap\tilde{T}\big)\gtrsim\rho^{1-\varepsilon_{3}}|i-j|
. Then if distinct tubes
\tilde{T}_{i}
and
\tilde{T}_{j}
intersect inside
Q
, we must have
\angle(v(\tilde{T}_{i}),v(\tilde{T}_{j}))\gtrsim\rho^{1-\varepsilon_{3}}|i-j|/\tau
. But this implies that for distinct
\tilde{T}_{i}
and
\tilde{T}_{j}
we have

|Q\cap\tilde{T}_{i}\cap\tilde{T}_{j}|\lesssim\frac{\rho^{3}}{\angle(v(\tilde{T}_{i}),v(\tilde{T}_{j}))+\rho}\lesssim\frac{\rho^{2+\varepsilon_{3}}\tau}{|i-j|}.

Thus we have the following Cordoba-type
L^{2}
estimate.

\Big|Q\cap\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\tilde{Y}_{1}(\tilde{T}_{i})\Big|\geq\frac{\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}|Q\cap\tilde{Y}_{1}(\tilde{T}_{i})|\Big)^{2}}{\big\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}\chi_{\tilde{T}_{i}}\big\|_{L^{2}(Q)}^{2}}\gtrapprox\frac{k^{2}\big(\rho^{2\varepsilon_{1}+2}\tau\big)^{2}}{(k\rho^{2+\varepsilon_{3}}\tau)}\geq\rho^{6\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{3}}\tau^{2}\rho.

(4.16)

\begin{overpic}[figures/figFullGrain]
\put(26.0,35.0){{\tiny$\tilde{T}_{1}$}}
\put(33.0,35.0){{\tiny$\tilde{T}_{2}$}}
\put(65.0,33.0){{\tiny$\tilde{T}_{k}$}}
\par\end{overpic}

Figure 3:
The union of the sets
Q\cap\tilde{Y}_{1}(T_{i}),\ i=1,\ldots,k
fill out most of the slab (blue) inside
Q

(
4.16
) says that
\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\tilde{Y}_{1}(\tilde{T}_{i})
has large volume inside of
Q
. On the other hand, we claim that this set is contained inside a rectangular prism of dimensions roughly
\tau\times\tau\times\delta^{-\eta}\rho
. Indeed, since the plane map
V
is
\delta^{-\eta}
-Lipschitz and
Q
has diameter
\leq 2\rho
, if
q\in Q\cap T
then we have

Q\cap\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\tilde{T}_{i}\ \subset\ Q\cap N_{4\delta^{-\eta}\rho}(q+V(Q)^{\perp}).

This is illustrated in Figure
3
. Thus

V(q)\cdot\Big(Q\cap\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\tilde{T}_{i}\Big)\subset[V(Q)\cdot q-4\delta^{-\eta}\rho,\ V(Q)\cdot q+4\delta^{-\eta}\rho].

(4.17)

Note that
V(Q)\cdot(Q\cap T)
is also contained in this same interval.

Combining (
4.16
) and (
4.17
), we conclude that if
t\in V(Q)\cdot\big(Q\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}\big)
, then

\Big|Q\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}}\cap\pi_{Q}^{-1}([t-4\delta^{-\eta}\rho,\ t+4\delta^{-\eta}\rho])\Big|\gtrapprox\rho^{6\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{3}}\tau^{2}\rho.

(4.18)

Informally, (
4.18
) says that each
\rho
-separated point
t\in V(Q)\cdot\big(Q\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}\big)
corresponds to a
\tau\times\tau\times\rho
grain (i.e. rectangular prism whose short axis is parallel to
V(Q)
) that has nearly full intersection with
Q\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}}
.

If
|t-t^{\prime}|>8\delta^{-\eta}\rho
then the sets
\pi_{Q}^{-1}([t-4\delta^{-\eta}\rho,\ t+4\delta^{-\eta}\rho]
and
\pi_{Q}^{-1}([t^{\prime}-4\delta^{-\eta}\rho,\ t^{\prime}+4\delta^{-\eta}\rho]
are disjoint, and hence (
4.18
) implies that

\mathcal{E}_{8\delta^{-\eta}\rho}\Big(V(Q)\cdot\big(Q\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}\big)\Big)\lessapprox\frac{|Q\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}}|}{\rho^{6\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{3}}\tau^{2}\rho}\leq\frac{\rho^{\sigma-\varepsilon_{1}}\tau^{3-\sigma-\varepsilon_{2}}}{\rho^{6\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{3}}\tau^{2}\rho}\lesssim\rho^{-7\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{3}}(\tau/\rho)^{1-\sigma},

and thus

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\Big(V(Q)\cdot\big(Q\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}\big)\Big)\leq 8\delta^{-\eta}\mathcal{E}_{8\delta^{-\eta}\rho}\Big(V(Q)\cdot\big(Q\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}\big)\Big)\lessapprox\delta^{-\eta}\rho^{-7\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{3}}(\tau/\rho)^{1-\sigma}.

(4.19)

To conclude the proof, select
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3},\eta
sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
) so that the LHS of (
4.19
) is at most
\rho^{-\varepsilon}(\tau/\rho)^{1-\sigma}
(this is possible since
\rho\leq\delta^{\varepsilon}
). Let
(\mathbb{T}_{2},Y_{2})_{\delta}
be the refinement of
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})_{\delta}
induced by the refinement
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2},\tilde{Y}_{2})_{\rho}
. Since
E_{\mathbb{T}_{2}}\subset E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}
, (
4.19
) implies that
(\mathbb{T}_{2},Y_{2})_{\delta}
satisfies (
4.15
).
∎

Lemma
4.9
controls the size of
V(Q_{\tau})\cdot\big(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\cap Q_{\tau})
at scale
\rho
, where
Q_{\tau}
is a cube of side-length
\tau\in[\rho^{1-\varepsilon},\rho^{1/2}]
; note that this set is contained in an interval of length
\sim\tau
. The next lemma establishes a similar bound for the (potentially larger) quantity
J\cap\big(V(Q_{\rho^{1/2}})\cdot\big(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\cap Q_{\rho^{1/2}})\big),
where
Q_{\rho^{1/2}}
is a cube of side-length
\rho^{1/2}
, and
J
is an interval of length
\tau
.

Lemma 4.10
.

For all
\varepsilon>0,
there exists
\eta>0,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a
\eta
-extremal collection of tubes with
\delta^{-\eta}
-Lipschitz plane map
V
. Let
\rho\in[\delta^{1-\varepsilon},\delta^{\varepsilon}]
and let
\tau\in[\rho^{1-\varepsilon},\rho^{1/2}]
.

Then there exists a
\varepsilon
-extremal sub-collection
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
, so that the following holds. Let
Q
be a
\rho^{1/2}
-cube and let
F=V(Q)\cdot(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\cap Q)
. Then for each interval
J
of length
\tau
, we have

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}(J\cap F)\leq\rho^{-\varepsilon}(\tau/\rho)^{1-\sigma}.

(4.20)

Proof.

For notational convenience, we will suppose that
\rho^{1/2}/\tau
is an integer, and thus every
\tau
-cube is contained in a single
\rho^{1/2}
cube. The case where
\rho^{1/2}/\tau
is not an integer can be handled similarly. Let
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{4}>0
be small constants to be chosen below. We will select
\eta
very small compared to
\varepsilon_{1}
, and
\varepsilon_{i}
very small compared to
\varepsilon_{i+1}
. We will select
\varepsilon_{4}
small compared to
\varepsilon
.

Apply Proposition
3.2
to
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
with
\varepsilon_{1}
in place of
\varepsilon
, and
\rho
as above. We obtain a refinement of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
, and a
\varepsilon_{1}
-extremal collection of
\rho
-tubes,
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1},\tilde{Y}_{1})_{\rho}
. In what follows, we will describe various sub-collections of
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
. Since
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
is a balanced cover of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
, each sub-collection of
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
will induce an analogous sub-collection of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
, of the same relative density.

Applying Lemma
4.9
to
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1},\tilde{Y}_{1})_{\rho}
with
\varepsilon_{2}
in place of
\varepsilon
and
\tau
as above, and then again to the output with
\varepsilon_{3}
in place of
\varepsilon
and
\rho^{1/2}
in place of
\tau
, we obtain a
\varepsilon_{3}
-extremal sub-collection
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2},\tilde{Y}_{2})_{\rho}
of
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1},\tilde{Y}_{1})_{\rho}
with the following two properties.

•

For each
\rho^{1/2}
-cube
Q
, we have

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\big(V(Q)\cdot(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}\cap Q)\big)\leq\rho^{-\varepsilon_{3}}\rho^{\frac{\sigma-1}{2}}.

(4.21)

•

For each
\rho^{1/2}
-cube
Q
, and each
\tau
-cube
W
, we have

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\big(V(Q)\cdot(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}\cap W)\big)\lesssim\delta^{-\eta}\rho^{-\varepsilon_{2}}(\tau/\rho)^{\sigma-1}.

(4.22)

Note that in (
4.22
) we have the unit vector
V(Q)
rather than
V(W)
. This requires justification. By Lemma
4.9
we get the estimate
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\big(V(W)\cdot(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}\cap W)\big)\lesssim\rho^{-\varepsilon_{2}}(\tau/\rho)^{\sigma-1}
. To obtain (
4.22
), we bound

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\big(V(Q)\cdot(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}\cap W)\big)&\leq\delta^{-\eta}\mathcal{E}_{\delta^{-\eta}\rho}\big(V(Q)\cdot(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}\cap W)\\
&\lesssim\delta^{-\eta}\mathcal{E}_{\delta^{-\eta}\rho}\big(V(W)\cdot(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}\cap W)\\
&\leq\delta^{-\eta}\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\big(V(W)\cdot(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}\cap W)\\
&\leq\delta^{-\eta}\rho^{-\varepsilon_{2}}(\tau/\rho)^{\sigma-1}.\end{split}

The key step is the second inequality, which used the fact that
\angle(V(Q),V(W))\lesssim\rho^{1/2}
,
\operatorname{diam}(W)\lesssim\rho^{1/2}
, and
V
is
\delta^{-\eta}
-Lipschitz.

Apply Proposition
3.2
to
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2},\tilde{Y}_{2})_{\rho}
with
\varepsilon_{4}
in place of
\varepsilon
and
\rho^{1/2}
in place of
\rho
. We obtain a refinement
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3},\tilde{Y}_{3})_{\rho}
of
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{2},\tilde{Y}_{2})_{\rho}
, which is contained in a union of
\lesssim\rho^{-\varepsilon_{4}}\rho^{\frac{\sigma-3}{2}}

\rho^{1/2}
-cubes.

After a further refinement, we can suppose that each
\rho^{1/2}
-cube
Q
that intersects
E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3}}
satisfies
|E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3}}\cap Q|\gtrapprox\rho^{2\varepsilon_{4}}\rho^{\frac{\sigma+3}{2}}.

The two properties described above continue to hold for
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3},\tilde{Y}_{3})_{\rho}
. Therefore, if
Q
is a
\rho^{1/2}
-cube that intersects
E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3}}
, then by (
4.21
),
E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3}}\cap Q
is contained in a union of at most
\rho^{-\varepsilon_{3}}\rho^{\frac{\sigma-1}{2}}
“grains,” which are sets of the form
Q\cap N_{\rho}(\pi_{Q}^{-1}(t))
, where
t\in\mathbb{R}
. The union of these grains has volume
\lesssim\rho^{-\varepsilon_{3}}\rho^{\frac{\sigma+3}{2}}
, while on the other hand
E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3}}\cap Q
has volume
\gtrapprox\rho^{2\varepsilon_{4}}\rho^{\frac{\sigma+3}{2}}
. This means that after further refining
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3},\tilde{Y}_{3})_{\rho}
to obtain a pair
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4},\tilde{Y}_{4})_{\rho}
, we can suppose each for each
\rho^{1/2}
-cube
Q
and each
t\in V(p_{Q})\cdot(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}}\cap Q)
, we have

|E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}}\cap Q\ \cap\ \pi_{Q}^{-1}(N_{\rho}(t))|\gtrapprox\rho^{2\varepsilon_{4}+\varepsilon_{3}}\rho^{2}.

(4.23)

Next, let
Q
be one of the
\rho^{1/2}
cubes described above. By Fubini, there must be a point
q\in Q
so that the line
L=q+\mathbb{R}V(p_{Q})
satisfies

|L\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}}\cap Q|\gtrapprox\rho^{2\varepsilon_{4}}\rho^{\frac{\sigma+3}{2}}\gtrsim\rho^{\varepsilon_{2}+2\varepsilon_{4}}|E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}}\cap Q|.

(4.24)

For each cube
Q
, let
G_{Q}
be the union of grains that intersect
L\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}}\cap Q
, i.e.

G_{Q}=Q\cap\Big[\pi_{Q}^{-1}\Big(N_{\rho}\big(\pi(L\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}}\cap Q)\big)\Big)\Big].

Then by (
4.23
) and (
4.24
), we have

|E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}}\cap G_{Q}|\gtrapprox\rho^{\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{3}+4\varepsilon_{4}}|E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}}\cap Q|.

(4.25)

Let
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}=\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}
, and define the shading
\tilde{Y}_{5}
by
\tilde{Y}_{5}(\tilde{T})=\tilde{Y}_{4}(\tilde{T})\cap\bigcup_{Q}G_{Q}
. By (
4.25
) and the bound
\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{3}+4\varepsilon_{4}\leq 5\varepsilon_{4}
, we have
\sum_{\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}}|\tilde{Y}_{5}(\tilde{T})|\gtrapprox\rho^{5\varepsilon_{4}}\sum_{\tilde{T}\in\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{4}}|\tilde{Y}_{4}(\tilde{T})|
.

Unwinding definitions, we see that if
Q
is a
\rho^{1/2}
-cube and if
J\subset\mathbb{R}
is an interval, then

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\Big(J\cap\big(V(p_{Q})\cdot(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}}\cap Q)\big)\Big)\sim\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\Big(V(p_{Q})\cdot(L_{J}\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{3}})\Big),

where
L_{J}=L\cap\pi_{Q}^{-1}(J)
, and
L
is the line from (
4.24
). In particular, if
J
is an interval of length
\tau
, then
L_{J}
is contained in a union of
O(1)

\tau
-cubes
W
, and hence

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\Big(J\cap\big(V(p_{Q})\cdot(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}}\cap Q)\big)\Big)\lesssim\sum_{W}\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\big(V(p)\cdot(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}}\cap W)\big),

where the sum is taken over the
O(1)

\tau
-cubes
W
described above. Since the contribution from each of these
\tau
-cubes is
\leq\rho^{-\varepsilon_{2}}(\tau/\rho)^{1-\sigma},
we conclude that

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\Big(J\cap\big(V(p_{Q})\cdot(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}}\cap Q)\big)\Big)\lesssim\rho^{-\varepsilon_{2}}(\tau/\rho)^{1-\sigma}.

(4.26)

We claim that the sub-collection
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
induced by
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5},\tilde{Y}_{5})_{\rho}
satisfies the conclusions of Lemma
4.10
. First, if
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{4}
are chosen appropriately, then
\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}|Y^{\prime}(T)|\geq\delta^{\varepsilon}
, so
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
is
\varepsilon
-extremal. Next, let
Q
be a
\rho^{1/2}
cube, let
p\in Q
, and let
J
be an interval of length
\tau
. Then since
E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\subset E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}}
, we have

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\Big(J\cap\big(V(p)\cdot(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\cap Q)\big)\Big)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\Big(J\cap\big(V(p)\cdot(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{5}}\cap Q)\big)\Big).

(4.27)

(
4.20
) now follows from (
4.26
).
∎

For a fixed
\rho
, the next lemma records what happens when we iterate Lemma
4.10
for many different scales
\tau
.

Lemma 4.11
.

For all
\varepsilon>0,
there exists
\eta>0,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a
\eta
-extremal collection of tubes with
\delta^{-\eta}
-Lipschitz plane map
V
. Let
\rho\in[\delta^{1-\varepsilon},\delta^{\varepsilon}]
.

Then there exists a
\varepsilon
-extremal sub-collection
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
, so that the following holds. Let
Q
be a
\rho^{1/2}
-cube. Then
V(Q)\cdot(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\cap Q)
is a
(\rho,1-\sigma;\delta^{-\varepsilon})_{1}
-ADset.

Proof.

Let
N\geq 1/\varepsilon
be an integer. For each
0\leq j<N
, iteratively apply Lemma
4.10
to
(\mathbb{T},Y)
with
\varepsilon/2
in place of
\varepsilon
,
\rho
as above, and
\tau=\tau_{j}=\rho^{(j+N)/2N}
. We will select
\eta>0
sufficiently small so that the resulting refinement
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
is
\varepsilon
-extremal. Let
F=V(Q)\cdot(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\cap Q)
.

Let
Q
be a
\rho^{1/2}
-cube and let
J\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval of length at least
\rho
. Shrinking
J
if needed, we may suppose
J\subset V(Q)\cdot Q
, so in particular
|J|\leq 2\rho^{1/2}
. Thus there is an integer
0\leq j<N
so that
\rho^{(j+N+1)/2N}<|J|\leq 2\rho^{(j+N)/2N}
. Let
\tilde{J}
be an interval of length
\tau_{j}=\rho^{(j+N)/2N}
that contains
J
(if
|J|>\rho^{1/2}
then a slight modification is needed; we need to cover
J
by a union of two intervals of length
\rho^{1/2}
). By Lemma
4.10
, we have

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}(J\cap F)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\rho}(\tilde{J}\cap F)\leq\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}(\rho^{(j+N)/2N}/\rho)^{1-\sigma}\leq\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\rho^{-1/2N}(|J|/\rho)^{1-\sigma}.\qed

The next lemma records what happens when we iterate Lemma
4.11
for many different scales
\rho
.

Lemma 4.12
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta_{0},\eta>0
so that the following holds. Let
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
and let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be an
\eta
-extremal collection of tubes with a
\delta^{-\eta}
-Lipschitz plane map
V
.

Then there exists a
\varepsilon
-extremal sub-collection
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
, so that

V(p)\cdot\big(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\cap B(p,\rho^{1/2}\big)\ \textrm{is a}\ (\rho,1-\sigma;\delta^{-\varepsilon})_{1}\textrm{-ADset}\quad\textrm{for all}\ \rho\in[\delta,1],\ p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

(4.28)

Proof.

Let
N\geq 2/\varepsilon
be an integer. For each
1\leq j<N
, iteratively apply Lemma
4.11
to
(\mathbb{T},Y)
with
\varepsilon/3
in place of
\varepsilon
,
C=N
, and
\rho=\rho_{j}=\delta^{j/N}
. We will select
\eta>0
sufficiently small so that the resulting refinement
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
is
\varepsilon
-extremal.

Next, let
\rho\in[\delta,1]
, let
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and let
J
be an interval of length at least
\rho
. Our goal is to show that

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\Big(J\ \cap\ \Big[V(p)\cdot\big(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\cap B(p,\rho^{1/2}\big)\Big]\Big)\leq\delta^{-\varepsilon}(|J|/\rho)^{1-\sigma}.

(4.29)

Case 1:
\rho\geq\delta^{1/N}
. In this case, (
4.29
) follows from the simple observation that
E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\subset B(0,1)
, and
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}([-1,1])\leq 2\rho^{-1}\leq\delta^{-\varepsilon}
.

Case 2:
\rho\in[\delta^{1-1/N},\delta^{1/N})
. In this case, there is an integer
1\leq j\leq N-2
so that
\delta^{(j+1)/N}\leq\rho<\delta^{j/N}
.
B(p,\rho^{1/2})
can be covered by a union of
O(1)

\rho_{j}^{1/2}
-cubes. By Lemma
4.11
, for each such cube
Q
we have

\mathcal{E}_{\rho_{j}}\Big(J\ \cap\ \big(V(Q)\cdot(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\cap Q)\big)\Big)\leq\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}(|J|/\rho_{j})^{1-\sigma}.

(4.30)

Recall that
V(Q)=V(p_{Q})
, where
p_{Q}
is the center of
Q
. Since
Q
intersects
B(p,\rho^{1/2})
, we have that
|p_{Q}-p|\lesssim\rho_{j}^{1/2}
. Since
V
is
\delta^{-\eta}
-Lipschitz, this implies
\angle(V(Q),V(p))\lesssim\delta^{-\eta}\rho_{j}^{1/2}
. Finally, since
Q
has diameter
\leq 2\rho_{j}^{1/2}
, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{\delta^{-\eta}\rho_{j}}\Big(J\ \cap\ \big(V(p)\cdot(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\cap Q)\big)\Big)&\sim\mathcal{E}_{\delta^{-\eta}\rho_{j}}\Big(J\ \cap\ \big(V(Q)\cdot(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\cap Q)\big)\Big)\\
&\leq\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}(|J|/\rho_{j})^{1-\sigma},\end{split}

where the final inequality used (
4.30
).

Since (
4.31
) holds for each of the
O(1)
cubes whose union covers
B(p,\rho^{1/2})
, we have

\mathcal{E}_{\delta^{-\eta}\rho_{j}}\Big(J\ \cap\ \Big[V(p)\cdot\big(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\cap B(p,\rho^{1/2}\big)\Big]\Big)\lesssim\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}(|J|/\rho_{j})^{1-\sigma}.

(4.31)

This is almost what we want, except we have bounded the
\delta^{-\eta}\rho_{j}
-covering number, rather than the
\rho
-covering number. But since each interval of length
\delta^{-\eta}\rho_{j}
can be covered by
\leq\delta^{-1/N-\eta}
intervals of length
\rho
, (
4.31
) implies

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\Big(J\ \cap\ \Big[V(p)\cdot\big(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\cap B(p,\rho^{1/2}\big)\Big]\Big)\lesssim\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}-1/N-\eta}(|J|/\rho_{j})^{1-\sigma}.

(
4.29
) now follows, provided
\delta_{0}
and
\eta
are chosen sufficiently small.

Case 3:
\rho\in[\delta,\delta^{1-1/N})
. This reduces to Case 2, using the observation that
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}(X)\leq\delta^{-1/N}\mathcal{E}_{\delta^{1-1/N}}(X)
.

∎

4.4
Proof of Proposition
4.1

We are now ready to prove Proposition
4.1
. In brief, we use the results from Sections
4.1
and
4.2
to find an extremal set of
\delta
-tubes
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
with a plane map. Next, we use the results from Section
4.3
to show that this set of tubes arranges into grains of dimensions
\sqrt{\delta}\times\sqrt{\delta}\times\delta
. We select a tube
T_{0}
whose
\sqrt{\delta}
neighborhood covers about
\delta^{-1}
tubes from
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
, and consider the unit rescaling (recall Definition
3.2
) of this collection of tubes with respect to the
\sqrt{\delta}
neighborhood
T_{0}
. This is a set of
\sqrt{\delta}
tubes, which we will denote by
(\tilde{T},\tilde{Y})_{\sqrt{\delta}}
.

In what follows, we define
\rho=\sqrt{\delta}
. Note that the
\sqrt{\delta}
neighborhood of
T_{0}
can be covered by about
\delta^{-1/2}
balls of radius
\sqrt{\delta}
, and the restriction of
E_{\mathbb{T}}
to each of these balls consists of a union of parallel grains of dimensions
\sqrt{\delta}\times\sqrt{\delta}\times\delta
. After re-scaling, the situation is as follows: The unit ball can be covered by
\rho^{-1}
“slabs” (rectangular prisms) of dimensions
1\times 1\times\rho
, and the restriction of
E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}}
to each slab consists of a union of parallel (re-scaled) grains of dimensions
1\times\rho\times\rho
; these will be called
global grains
, and they will satisfy the properties described in Item
1
from the statement of Proposition
4.1
.

Finally, we will apply the results from Section
4.3
to show that
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
arranges into grains of dimensions
\sqrt{\rho}\times\sqrt{\rho}\times\rho
; these will be called
local grains
, and they will satisfy the properties described in Item
2
from the statement of Proposition
4.1
. We now turn to the details.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Let
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{4}>0
be small constants to be chosen below. We will select
\varepsilon_{i}
very small compared to
\varepsilon_{i+1},
and and
\varepsilon_{4}
very small compared to
\varepsilon
.

Step 1: Global grains.

Define
\tau_{0}=\delta_{0}^{\frac{2+\sigma}{\sigma}}
. By Lemma
4.8
followed by Lemma
4.12
, there exists a
\varepsilon_{1}
-extremal collection of tubes
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\tau}
for some
\tau\in(0,\tau_{0}]
that has a
\tau^{-\varepsilon_{1}}
-Lipschitz plane map
V
, and satisfies (
4.28
) with
\varepsilon_{1}
in place of
\varepsilon
.

Define
\delta=\tau^{\frac{\sigma}{2+\sigma}}
, so
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Apply Proposition
3.2
to
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\tau}
, with
\tau
in place of
\delta
,
\delta
in place of
\rho
, and
\varepsilon_{2}
in place of
\varepsilon
. We obtain a refinement
(\mathbb{T}_{2},Y_{2})_{\tau}
of
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})_{\tau}
, and a
\varepsilon_{2}
-extremal collection of
\delta
-tubes
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1},\tilde{Y}_{1})_{\delta}
.

Fix a choice of
\delta
-tube
\tilde{T}
; define
\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}=\mathbb{T}_{2}[\tilde{T}]
, and let
Y^{\dagger}
be the restriction of
Y_{2}
to
\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}
. Since the unit re-scaling of
(\mathbb{T}^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger})_{\tau}
relative to
\tilde{T}
is
\varepsilon_{2}
-extremal, we can find a sub-collection
(\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\dagger},Y_{1}^{\dagger})_{\tau}
of
(\mathbb{T}^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger})_{\tau}
, with
\sum|Y_{1}^{\dagger}(T)|\gtrapprox\tau^{\varepsilon_{2}}\sum|Y^{\dagger}(T)|,
and a tube
T_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\dagger}
with the following property (P): for each
\delta
-cube
Q
that intersects
E_{\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\dagger}}
, we have
Y_{1}^{\dagger}(T_{0})\cap Q\neq\emptyset
. After enlarging
\tilde{T}
slightly (we might have to replace
\delta
by
2\delta
, but this is harmless), we may suppose that
T_{0}
and
\tilde{T}
have the same coaxial line.

Let
(\hat{\mathbb{T}},\hat{Y})_{\tau/\delta}
be the unit re-scaling of
(\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\dagger},Y_{1}^{\dagger})_{\tau}
relative to
\tilde{T}
. This re-scaling sends
T_{0}
to a tube
\hat{T}_{0}
whose coaxial line is the
z
-axis. Let

Z=\{z\in\delta\mathbb{Z}\colon(0,0,z)\in\hat{Y}(\hat{T}_{0})\}.

Then since
T_{0}
has Property (P), for each
p=(x,y,z)\in E_{\hat{\mathbb{T}}}
there is
z_{0}\in Z
with
|z-z_{0}|\lesssim\delta
.

Next, we will analyze what (
4.28
) says about
(\hat{\mathbb{T}},\hat{Y})_{\tau/\delta}
. For each
z\in Z
, let
\phi^{-1}(z)
be the inverse image of
z
under the unit-rescaling associated to
\tilde{T}
. Then
\phi^{-1}(z)
is contained in the coaxial line of
T_{0}
, and there is a point
p_{z}\in Y_{1}^{\dagger}(T_{0})
with
|\phi^{-1}(z)-p_{z}|\lesssim\delta
. By (
4.28
), we have

V(p_{z})\cdot\big(E_{\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\dagger}}\cap B(p_{z},\delta)\big)\ \textrm{is a}\ (\delta^{2},1-\sigma;\tau^{-\varepsilon_{1}})_{1}\textrm{-ADset}.

(4.32)

But if we consider the image of
B(p_{z},\delta)
under
\phi
, (
4.32
) says that there is a unit vector
\hat{V}(z)\in S^{2}
so that

\hat{V}(z)\cdot\Big(E_{\hat{\mathbb{T}}}\cap\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[z,z+\delta)\big)\Big)\ \textrm{is a}\ (\delta,1-\sigma;O(\tau^{-\varepsilon_{1}}))_{1}\textrm{-ADset}.

(4.33)

Since
|V(p_{z})\cdot\operatorname{dir}(L_{0})|\leq\tau
, we have
|\hat{V}(z)\cdot(0,0,1)|\leq\tau/\delta\leq 1/10
for all
z\in Z
. In particular, since
V
is
\tau^{-\varepsilon_{1}}
-Lipschitz, we have that that
\hat{V}
is also
\tau^{-\varepsilon_{1}}
-Lipschitz, and thus for each interval
I\subset[-1,1]
of length
\sim\tau^{\varepsilon_{1}}
, we must have either
|\hat{V}(z)\cdot(1,0,0)|\geq\frac{1}{3}
or
|\hat{V}(z)\cdot(0,1,0)|\geq\frac{1}{3}
for all
z\in I\cap Z
. Interchanging the
x
and
y
axes if necessary, we may replace
Z
with
Z\cap I
, and define
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{1}=\hat{\mathbb{T}}
,
\hat{Y}_{1}(\hat{T})=\hat{Y}(\hat{T})\cap(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times I)
. If we choose the interval
I
appropriately, then
\sum_{\hat{T}\in\hat{\mathbb{T}}}|\hat{Y}_{1}(\hat{T})|\gtrsim\tau^{\varepsilon_{1}}\sum_{\hat{T}\in\hat{\mathbb{T}}}|\hat{Y}(\hat{T})|
, and
|\hat{V}(z)\cdot(1,0,0)|\geq\frac{1}{3}
for all
z\in Z
. In particular, there are
3\tau^{-2\varepsilon_{1}}
-Lipschitz functions
f,g\colon Z\to\mathbb{R}
so that for all
z\in Z
, we have that
\hat{V}(z)
is parallel to
(1,f(z),g(z))
(
f
is given by dividing the second component of
\hat{V}
by the first, and
g
is defined by dividing the third component of
\hat{V}
by the first). Currently
f(z)
is defined on
Z
, but we will extend it slightly be defining
f(z+t)=f(z)
, if
z\in Z
and
t\in[0,\delta)
. Then by (
4.33
), for each
p=(x,y,z)\in E_{\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{1}}
we have

(1,f(z),0)\cdot\Big(E_{\hat{\mathbb{T}}}\cap\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[z,z+\delta)\big)\Big)\ \textrm{is a}\ (\delta,1-\sigma;O(\tau^{-\varepsilon_{1}}))_{1}\textrm{-ADset}.

(4.34)

Step 2: Local grains.

Apply Lemma
4.3
to
(\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{1},\hat{Y}_{1})_{\tau/\delta}
with
\tau/\delta
in place of
\delta
and
\varepsilon_{3}
in place of
\varepsilon
; we obtain a refinement
(\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{2},\hat{Y}_{2})_{\tau/\delta}
and a map
U\colon E_{\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}\to S^{2}
that satisfies

|U(p)\cdot\operatorname{dir}(\hat{T})|\leq(\tau/\delta)^{\sigma/2}\quad\textrm{for all}\ p\in E_{\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{2}},\ \hat{T}\in\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{2}(p).

Next, apply Lemma
4.4
with
\tau/\delta
in place of
\delta
,
\delta
in place of
\rho
(note that this is a valid choice, since
\delta=(\tau/\delta)^{\sigma/2}
), and
\varepsilon_{3}
in place of
\varepsilon
. We obtain a
\varepsilon_{3}
-extremal collection of
\delta
-tubes
(\tilde{T},\tilde{Y})_{\delta}
that covers
(\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{2},\hat{Y}_{2})_{\tau/\delta}
and a plane map
W\colon E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}}\to S^{2}
. Each
\delta
-cube
Q\subset E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}}
intersects
E_{\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{2}}
, and thus by (
4.34
), for each
z\in Z
we have

(1,f(z),0)\cdot\Big(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}}\cap\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[z,z+\delta)\big)\Big)\ \textrm{is a}\ (\delta,1-\sigma;O(\tau^{-\varepsilon_{1}}))_{1}\textrm{-ADset}.

(4.35)

Indeed, to verify (
4.35
) let
\tilde{X}=\big(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}}\cap\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[z,z+\delta)\big)\big)
and let
\hat{X}=\big(E_{\hat{\mathbb{T}}}\cap\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[z,z+\delta)\big)\big)
. It suffices to show that each
w\in(1,f(z),0)\cdot\tilde{X}
is contained in the
O(\delta)
-neighborhood of
(1,f(z),0)\cdot\hat{X}
. For such a
w
, there exists a
\delta
-cube
Q
that intersects
\tilde{X}
with
w\in(1,f(z),0)\cap Q
. But since
Q
is a
\delta
-cube, it must be contained in
\tilde{X}
. Since
Q\cap E_{\hat{\mathbb{T}}}\neq\emptyset,
there must be a point
p\in Q\cap\hat{X}
. Thus
|(1,f(z),0)\cdot w-(1,f(z),0)\cdot p|\lesssim|w-p|\lesssim\delta
.

We will re-write (
4.35
) as

(1,f(z_{0}),0)\cdot\Big(E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}}\cap\{z=z_{0}\}\Big)\ \textrm{is a}\ (\delta,1-\sigma;O(\tau^{-\varepsilon_{1}}))_{1}\textrm{-ADset},

(4.36)

for all
z_{0}
in the domain of
f
. Abusing notation slightly, we can replace
f
with its extension to
[-1,1]
; this extension is also
3\tau^{-2\varepsilon_{1}}
-Lipschitz, and (
4.36
) continues to hold, since the set on the LHS of (
4.36
) is empty if
z_{0}
is not contained in the original domain of definition of
f
.

To complete the proof, apply Lemma
4.12
to
(\tilde{T},\tilde{Y})_{\delta}
with
\varepsilon_{4}
in place of
\varepsilon
. If
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{4}
are chosen sufficiently small, then the resulting refinement of
(\tilde{T},\tilde{Y})_{\delta}
almost satisfies the conclusions of Proposition
4.1
, except the function
f
and the plane map
W
are not
1
-Lipschitz. This can be fixed via a mild rescaling, using Lemma
3.5
.
∎

5
The global grains slope function is
C^{2}

Our goal in this section is to strength the conclusions of Proposition
4.1
by increasing the regularity of the global grains slope function. More precisely, we will prove the following.

Proposition 5.1
.

For all
\varepsilon,\delta_{0}>0
, there exists
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
and a
\varepsilon
-extremal set of tubes
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
with the following properties:

1^{\prime}.

Global grains with
C^{2}
slope function.

There is a function
f\colon[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\|f\|_{C^{2}}\leq 1
so that
\big(E_{\mathbb{T}}\cap\{z=z_{0}\}\big)\cdot(1,f(z_{0}),0)
is a
(\delta,1-\sigma,\delta^{-\varepsilon})_{1}
-ADset for each
z_{0}\in[-1,1]
.

2.

Local grains with Lipschitz plane map

(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
has a 1-Lipschitz plane map
V
. For all
\rho\in[\delta,1]
and all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
V(p)\cdot\big(B(p,\rho^{1/2})\cap E_{\mathbb{T}}\big)
is a
(\rho,1-\sigma,\delta^{-\varepsilon})_{1}
-ADset.

Our main tool for proving Proposition
5.1
will be a new result in projection theory. To state this result at an appropriate level of generality, we introduce a new type of discretized set.

Definition 5.1
.

For
n\geq 1,\ \alpha\in(0,n]
,
C\geq 1
and
\delta>0
, We say a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
(\delta,\alpha,C)_{n}
-set if
E
is a union of
\delta
-cubes, and for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
r\geq\delta
we have

|E\cap B(x,r)|\leq Cr^{\alpha}|E|.

(5.1)

We form an analogous definition if
E\subset S^{1}
is a union of
\delta
-arcs.

Definition
5.1
is closely related to Katz and Tao’s notion of a
(\delta,\alpha)_{n}
-set from
[
18
]
. However, in Katz and Tao’s definition, the quantity
|E|
in the RHS of (
5.1
) is replaced by
\delta^{n-\alpha}
; the former could be much larger. Definition
5.1
is also closely related to our definition of a
(\delta,\alpha,C)_{n}
-ADset from Definition
4.2
. In particular, if
E
is a union of
\delta
-cubes that is also a
(\delta,\alpha,C)_{n}
-ADset, and
|E|\geq c\delta^{n-\alpha}
for some
c>0
, then
E
is a
(\delta,\alpha,C/c)_{n}
-set. In practice we will always consider sets
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, so we will drop the subscript
2
from our notation. The reason for this definition (which is slightly at odds with the traditional literature) is that several recent results in projection theory can be more easily stated with this terminology.

Theorem 5.2
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\subset[0,1]^{2}
be
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\eta})
-sets. Then at least one of the following must hold.

(A)

There exist orthogonal lines
\ell
and
\ell^{\perp}
whose
\delta
-neighborhoods cover a significant fraction of
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
and
A_{3}
respectively. In particular,

\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(N_{\delta}(\ell)\cap A_{1})\geq\delta^{\varepsilon-1},\quad\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(N_{\delta}(\ell)\cap A_{2})\geq\delta^{\varepsilon-1},\ \ \textrm{and}\ \ \mathcal{E}_{\delta}(N_{\delta}(\ell^{\perp})\cap A_{3})\geq\delta^{\varepsilon-1}.

(5.2)

(B)

Let
G\subset A_{1}\times A_{2}\times A_{3}
be any set satisfying
\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(G)\geq\delta^{\eta-3}
.
Then there exists
\rho\geq\delta
and an interval
I
of length at least
\delta^{-\eta}\rho
such that

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\big(I\cap\{(a_{1}-a_{2})\cdot a_{3}\colon(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in G\}\big)\geq\big(|I|/\rho\big)^{1-\varepsilon}.

(5.3)

The proof of Theorem
5.2
uses ideas and results that are different in character from the rest of the proof of Theorem
1.1
, so we will defer the proof to Section
8
.

5.1
The slope function is locally linear

In this section we will use Theorem
5.2
to show that if
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
is an extremal collection of tubes that satisfies the conclusions of Proposition
4.1
, then the restriction of the global grains slope function to an interval of length
\rho^{1/2}
looks linear at scale
\rho
.

To begin, we will study the interplay between the “global grains” and “local grains” structures from Proposition
4.1
. If we restrict
E_{\mathbb{T}}
to the
\delta^{1/2}
neighborhood of a global grain, then we obtain a set
E\subset E_{\mathbb{T}}
that has the following two structures: The global grains property (Item
1
from Proposition
4.1
) says that each “horizontal” (i.e.
z=z_{0}
) slice of
E
is a union of
1\times\delta
rectangles pointing in the direction
(1,f(z_{0}),0)
, and the spacing of these rectangles forms a ADset in the sense of Definition
4.2
. The local grains property (Item
2
from Proposition
4.1
) says that the restriction of
E
to a ball of radius
\delta^{1/2}
is a union of
\delta^{1/2}\times\delta^{1/2}\times\delta
grains, whose normal direction is given by the plane map
V
. We will use these two structures to construct sets
F
,
G
, and
\mathcal{H}\subset F\times G\times G
that satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem
5.2
. We will show that Conclusion
(B)
from Theorem
5.2
would contradict the ADset spacing condition from the global grains structure, and hence the sets
F
and
G
must satisfy Conclusion
(A)
. This in turn implies that the slope function
f
is linear at scale
\delta
over intervals of length
\delta^{1/2}
. The precise version is stated in Lemma
5.3
below. The precise hypotheses of this lemma are slightly technical; they are formulated in this fashion to allow the lemma to be applied at many scales and locations.

Lemma 5.3
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
I\subset[-1,1]
be an interval of length
\delta^{1/2}
with left endpoint
z_{0}
, and let
Z\subset I
. Let
f\colon I\to[0,1]
be 1-Lipschitz. Let
E\subset[-1,1]^{2}\times Z
be a union of
\delta
-cubes contained in the
\delta^{1/2}
-neighborhood of a line pointing in direction
(-f(z_{0}),1,0)
, with
|E|\geq\delta^{1+\sigma/2+\eta}
. Let
V\colon E\to S^{2}
be 1-Lipschitz, with
|V(p)\cdot(0,0,1)|\leq 1/2
for all
p\in E
. Suppose that for each
z_{0}\in Z
,

\big(E\cap\{z=z_{0}\}\big)\cdot\big(1,f(z_{0}),0\big)\quad\textrm{is a}\ (\delta,1-\sigma,\delta^{-\eta})_{1}\textrm{-ADset},

(5.4)

and for each
p\in E
,

\big|\big(E\cap B(p,\delta^{1/2})\big)\cdot V(p)\big|\leq\delta^{\frac{1+\sigma}{2}-\eta}.

(5.5)

Then there exists a set
Z_{\operatorname{lin}}\subset Z
with
|Z_{\operatorname{lin}}|\geq\delta^{1/2+\varepsilon}
, and a linear function
L\colon[0,\delta^{1/2}]\to[0,1]
so that
|f(z)-L(z)|\leq\delta
for all
z\in Z_{\operatorname{lin}}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we may suppose that
I=[0,\delta^{1/2}]
,
f(0)=0
(and hence
f(Z)\subset[-\delta^{1/2},\delta^{1/2}]
and
Z\subset[0,\delta^{1/2}]
), and that
E
is contained in the
\delta^{1/2}
-neighborhood of the
y
-axis. If not, then we can apply a (harmless) translation so that
E
is contained in the
\delta^{1/2}
-neighborhood of a line passing through the origin, and then we can apply a rotation around the
z
-axis so that
f(0)=0
.

Note that the image
E^{\prime}
of
E
under this rotation is no longer a union of
\delta
-cubes (since by definition,
\delta
-cubes are aligned with the standard grid). However, we can replace
E^{\prime}
by the union
E^{\prime\prime}
of
\delta
cubes that intersect
E^{\prime}
, and (
5.4
) and (
5.5
) will continue to hold for
E^{\prime\prime}
(the quantities
\delta^{-\eta}
and
\delta^{\frac{1+\sigma}{2}-\eta}
in (
5.4
) and (
5.5
) are respectively weakened to
100\delta^{-\eta}
and
100\delta^{\frac{1+\sigma}{2}-\eta},
but this is harmless). Note that our rotation fixes each plane
\{z=z_{0}\}
, so after the initial shift
Z\to Z-z_{0}
, the set
Z
remains unchanged by this procedure.

Step 1:
V
has large
x
-coordinate

Divide
[0,\delta^{1/2}]\times[0,1]\times[0,\delta^{1/2}]
into
\delta^{-1/2}
cubes. After pigeonholing and refining
E
, we may suppose that each
\delta^{1/2}
-cube
Q
that intersects
E
satisfies
|Q\cap E|\gtrsim\delta^{3/2+\sigma/2+\eta}
, and these
\delta^{1/2}
cubes are
\delta^{1/2}
separated. For each such cube
Q
, select a point
p_{Q}\in Q\cap E
; since the cubes are
\delta^{1/2}
separated, so are the points
p_{Q}
. For each such cube
Q
, define
\pi_{Q}(q)=q\cdot V(p_{Q})
.

Comparing with (
5.5
), we may further refine
E
so that each local grain is almost full: if
Q
is
\delta^{1/2}
-cube that intersects
E,
and
w\in V(p_{Q})\cdot(Q\cap E)
, then

|E\cap Q\cap N_{\delta}(\pi_{Q}^{-1}(w))|\gtrsim\delta^{2+2\eta}.

(5.6)

Fix such a cube
Q
. By (
5.6
) and Fubini, there is some
z_{1}\in[0,\delta^{1/2}]
so that

\Big|\{z=z_{1}\}\cap\big(E\cap Q\cap N_{\delta}\big(\pi_{Q}^{-1}(w)\big)\big)\Big|\gtrsim\delta^{3/2+2\eta}

(5.7)

(note that
|\cdot|
in (
5.6
) refers to 3-dimensional Lebesgue measure, while
|\cdot|
in (
5.7
) refers to 2-dimensional Lebesgue measure).

Since
|V(p_{Q})\cdot(0,0,1)|\leq 1/2
, the set on the LHS of (
5.7
) is contained in a rectangle
R
(in the
z=z_{1}
plane) of dimensions roughly
\delta^{1/2}\times\delta
. Thus
R\cdot(1,f(z_{1}))
is an interval
I
, whose length depends on the angle between the vectors
V(p_{Q})
and
(1,f(z_{1}),0)
. More precisely, we have

|I|\sim\delta^{1/2}|V(p_{Q})\cdot(-f(z_{1}),1,0)|.

(5.8)

By (
5.7
), we have

\Big|(1,f(z_{1}),0)\cdot\Big(\{z=z_{1}\}\cap\big(E\cap Q\cap N_{\delta}(\pi_{Q}^{-1}(w))\big)\Big)\Big|\gtrsim\delta^{2\eta}|I|,

(5.9)

but the set on the LHS of (
5.9
) is contained in
I\cap\big(E\cap\{z=z_{1}\}\big)\cdot\big(1,f(z_{1}),0\big)
, and (
5.4
) says that

\Big|I\cap\big(E\cap\{z=z_{1}\}\big)\cdot\big(1,f(z_{1}),0\big)\Big|\leq\delta^{1-\sigma-\eta}|I|^{\sigma}.

Re-arranging, we conclude that
|I|\leq\delta^{1-3\eta/(1-\sigma)}
, and thus by (
5.8
),

|V(p_{Q})\cdot(-f(z_{1}),1,0)|\lesssim\delta^{1/2-3\eta/(1-\sigma)}.

(5.10)

Since
f(z_{1})\in[0,\delta^{1/2}]
and
V(p_{Q})
is a unit vector, if
\eta
is selected sufficiently small then (
5.10
) implies that
|V(p_{Q})\cdot(0,1,0)|\leq 1/10.
Since
V(p_{Q})
is a unit vector and
|V(p_{Q})\cdot(0,0,1)|\leq 1/2
, we conclude that
|V(p_{Q})\cdot(1,0,0)|\geq 1/4
.

Step 2: Finding the function
g

Let
E_{\delta^{1/2}}
be the union of
\delta^{1/2}
-cubes that intersect
E
, and let
J\subset[0,1]
be the projection of
E_{\delta^{1/2}}
to the
y
-axis. Each
y\in J
is in the image of precisely one
\delta^{1/2}
-cube that intersects
E
; we will denote this cube by
Q(y)
. The set
J
is a union of
\delta^{1/2}
intervals, which are
\delta^{1/2}
separated.

Define the functions
g(y),h(y)\colon J\to\mathbb{R}
as follows: For each
y\in J
,
(1,h(y),g(y))
is parallel to
V(p_{Q})
, where
Q=Q(y)
.
g
and
h
are constant on each
\delta^{1/2}
interval of
J
. Since
V
is 1-Lipschitz and
|V(p_{Q})\cdot(1,0,0)|\geq 1/4
for each cube
Q
, we have that
g
and
h
are
4
-Lipschitz. Abusing notation, we will replace
g
and
h
by their
4
-Lipschitz extension to
[0,1]
; the image of
g
and
h
are contained in
[-5,5]
.

If
p=(x,y,z)\in E
is contained in a
\delta^{1/2}
-cube
Q
, then
|V(p)-V(p_{Q})|\leq\sqrt{3}\delta^{1/2}
, and hence by (
5.5
) we have

\big|\big(E\cap B(p,\delta^{1/2})\big)\cdot(1,h(y),g(y))\big|\lesssim\delta^{\frac{1+\sigma}{2}-\eta}.

In particular, for each
y_{0}\in[0,1]
we have

\Big|\big(E\cap\{y=y_{0}\}\big)\cdot\big(1,0,g(y_{0})\big)\Big|\lesssim\delta^{\frac{1+\sigma}{2}-\eta}.

(5.11)

Step 3: Paths of length 3

After refining the set
E
, we can select a set
Z_{\operatorname{popular}}\subset\delta\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,\delta^{1/2}]
so that each
\delta
-cube
Q\subset E
has center with
z
-coordinate in
Z_{\operatorname{popular}}
, and each
z\in Z_{\operatorname{popular}}
is the center of
\gtrsim\delta^{-3/2+\sigma/2+\eta}
such cubes. After this refinement
E
is still a union of
\delta
-cubes, and it’s volume has decreased by at most a factor of
1/2
.

After pigeonholing and applying a shift of the form
(x,y,z)\mapsto(x,y-y_{0},z)
for some
y_{0}\in[0,\delta^{1/2}]
, we can find a set
E_{1}\subset E
that is a union of
\delta
-cubes, with
|E_{1}|\geq\delta^{1/2}|E|
, so that each
\delta
-cube
Q\subset E_{1}
has center
p_{Q}
whose
y
-coordinate is contained in
\delta^{1/2}\mathbb{Z}
. Note that (
5.4
) and (
5.11
) continue to hold with
E_{1}
in place of
E
.

We say that two
\delta
-cubes
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
contained in
E_{1}
are
in the same global grain
if their midpoints
p_{Q}
and
p_{Q^{\prime}}
have the same
z
-coordinate, which we will denote by
z_{Q}
, and

\big|p_{Q}\cdot(1,f(z_{Q}),0)-p_{Q^{\prime}}\cdot(1,f(z_{Q}),0)\big|\leq\delta.

We say that two
\delta
-cubes
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
contained in
E_{1}
are
in the same local grain
if their midpoints
p_{Q}
and
p_{Q^{\prime}}
have the same
y
-coordinate, which we will denote by
y_{Q}
, and

\big|p_{Q}\cdot(1,0,g(y_{Q}))-p_{Q^{\prime}}\cdot(1,0,g(y_{Q}))\big|\leq\delta.

Recall that
E_{1}
is a union of at least
\delta^{-3/2+\sigma/2+\eta}

\delta
-cubes. For each such cube
Q
, the
y
-coordinate of its center is an element of
\delta^{1/2}\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,1]
, and thus takes one of
\delta^{-1/2}
values. Once the
y
-coordinate has been fixed, by (
5.5
) there are at most
\delta^{-1/2+\sigma/2-\eta}
choices for
p_{Q}\cdot\big(1,0,g(y)\big)
. Thus we can partition
E_{1}
into
O(\delta^{-1+\sigma/2-\eta})
sets, each of which are unions of
\delta
-cubes, so that any two
\delta
-cubes from a common set are in the same local grain. Thus after discarding the sets with fewer than an average number of cubes, we obtain a refinement
E_{2}\subset E_{1}
that is also a union of
\delta
-cubes, with
|E_{2}|\gtrsim|E_{1}|
, so that for each
\delta
-cube
Q\subset E_{2}
, there are
\gtrsim\delta^{-1/2+2\eta}
cubes
Q^{\prime}\subset E_{2}
so that
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
are in the same local grain.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
denote the set of quadruples
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{4}
of
\delta
-cubes contained in
E_{2}
that have the following relations

•

Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are in the same local grain.

•

Q_{2}
and
Q_{3}
are in the same global grain.

•

Q_{3}
and
Q_{4}
are in the same local grain.

•

The midpoints of
Q_{4}
and
Q_{1}
have the same
z
-coordinate.

We claim that

\#\mathcal{Q}\gtrsim\delta^{-5/2+\sigma/2+5\eta}.

(5.12)

To justify (
5.12
), we argue as follows. Let
s\colon Q\mapsto(z_{Q},r_{Q})
, where
r_{Q}
is the number of the form
\delta\mathbb{Z}
closest to
p_{Q}\cdot(1,f(z_{Q}),0)\big)
. There are
\leq\delta^{-1/2}
possible values for
z_{Q}
, and by (
5.4
), for each such value of
z
there are at most
\delta^{-1/2+\sigma/2-\eta}
values for
r_{Q}
. Thus
s
takes at most
\delta^{-1+\sigma/2-\eta}
values. Thus by Cauchy-Schwartz, there are
\gtrsim\delta^{-2+\sigma/2+3\eta}
pairs
(Q_{2},Q_{3})
of
\delta
-cubes in
E_{2}
with
s(Q_{2})=s(Q_{3})
. Hence there are
\gtrsim\delta^{-2+\sigma/2+3\eta}
pairs
(Q_{2},Q_{3})
that are contained in a common global grain. For each such pair
(Q_{2},Q_{3})
, there are
\delta^{-1/2+2\eta}
choices for
Q_{1}
and
\delta^{-1/2+2\eta}
choices for
Q_{4}
, so that
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
(resp.
Q_{3}
and
Q_{4}
) are in the same local grain, for a total of
\gtrsim\delta^{-3+\sigma/2+7\eta}
quadruples
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{4}
. Note that for the quadruples we have described,
Q_{1}
and
Q_{4}
need not have the same
z
-coordinate. Since there are
\delta^{-1/2}
choices for
z
-coordinate, we obtain (
5.12
) by pigeonholing.

Step 4: a skew transform

By (
5.12
), pigeonholing, and (
5.4
), we can select a choice
(z_{0},w)
so that there are at least
\gtrsim\delta^{-3/2+8\eta}
quadruples
(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{4})
where the center of
Q_{1}
has
z
-coordinate
z_{0}
, and

\big|p_{Q_{1}}\cdot(1,f(z_{0}),0)-w|\leq\delta.

(5.13)

Denote this set of quadruples by
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.

Define

A=\big(E\cap\{z=z_{0}\}\big)\cdot\big(1,f(z_{0}),0\big)-w.

(5.14)

By (
5.4
),
A
is a
(\delta,1-\sigma,\delta^{-\eta})_{1}
-ADset.

Define

\phi(x,y,z)=\big(x+f(z_{0})y+g(0)(z-z_{0}),\ y,\ z-z_{0}\big).

Define
f^{\dagger}(z)=f(z)-f(z_{0})
and
g^{\dagger}(y)=g(y)-g(0)
. Then
f^{{\dagger}}(0)=g^{{\dagger}}(0)=0
. Let
E^{{\dagger}}=\phi(E)
. Then for each
z_{1}\in[-z_{0},\ \delta^{1/2}-z_{0}]
, we have

\big(E^{\dagger}\cap\{z=z_{1}\}\big)\cdot\big(1,f^{\dagger}(z_{1}),0\big)=\big(E\cap\{z=z_{1}+z_{0}\}\big)\cdot\big(1,f(z_{1}),0\big)+g(0)(z_{1}-z_{0}),

(5.15)

so by (
5.4
), the LHS of (
5.15
) is a
(\delta,1-\sigma,\delta^{-\eta})_{1}
-ADset. Similarly, for each
y_{1}\in[0,1]
,

\big(E^{\dagger}\cap\{y=y_{1}\}\big)\cdot\big(1,0,g^{\dagger}(y_{1})\big)=\big(E\cap\{y=y_{1}\}\big)\cdot\big(1,0,g(y_{1})\big)+f(z_{0})y_{1}-g(0)z_{0},

(5.16)

so by (
5.5
), the LHS of (
5.16
) has
\delta
-covering number
\lesssim\delta^{\frac{-1+\sigma}{2}-\eta}
.

Step 5: Few dot products

Let
\mathcal{W}^{\dagger}
be the images of the centers of the cubes in
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
under
\phi
. Note that for each quadruple
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{4})\in\mathcal{W}^{\dagger}
,
p_{1}
and
p_{4}
have
z
-coordinate 0, and by (
5.13
), the
x
-value of
p_{1}
(which we will denote by
x_{1}
) satisfies
|x_{1}-w|\leq\delta
.

For each
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{4})\in\mathcal{W}^{\dagger}
, we will associate a tuple
(y_{1},z_{2},y_{3})
as follows:
y_{1}
will be the
y
-coordinate of
p_{1}
;
z_{2}
will be the
z
-coordinate of
p_{2};
and
y_{3}
will be the
y
-coordinate of
p_{3}
. We will show that

z_{2}\big(g^{{\dagger}}(y_{1})-g^{{\dagger}}(y_{3})\big)+\big(y_{1}-y_{3}\big)f^{{\dagger}}(z_{2})\in N_{4\delta}(A).

(5.17)

To establish (
5.17
), we will argue as follows. For each index
i=1,\ldots,4
, write
p_{i}=(x_{i},y_{i},z_{i})
. First, we have
|x_{1}-w|\leq\delta
and
z_{1}=0
. Since
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are in the same local grain,
y_{2}=y_{1}
, and
x_{1},x_{2},z_{2}
are related by
|(x_{1},y_{1},0)\cdot(1,0,g^{{\dagger}}(y_{1}))-(x_{2},y_{2},z_{2})\cdot(1,0,g^{{\dagger}}(y_{1}))|\leq\delta
i.e.

\big|x_{2}-\big(w-z_{2}g^{{\dagger}}(y_{1})\big)\big|\leq 2\delta.

Next, since
Q_{2}
and
Q_{3}
are in the same global grain,
z_{2}=z_{3}
, and
x_{2},x_{3},y_{2},y_{3}
are related by
|(x_{2},y_{2},z_{2})\cdot(1,f^{{\dagger}}(z_{2}),0)-(x_{3},y_{3},z_{2})\cdot(1,f^{{\dagger}}(z_{2}),0)|\leq\delta,
i.e. (since
y_{1}=y_{2}
)

\Big|x_{3}-\big(w-z_{2}g^{{\dagger}}(y_{1})+(y_{1}-y_{3})f^{{\dagger}}(z_{2})\big)\Big|\leq 3\delta.

Finally, we have
z_{4}=0
. since
Q_{3}
and
Q_{4}
are in the same local grain,
y_{4}=y_{3}
, and
x_{3},x_{4},z_{3}
are related by
|(x_{3},y_{3},z_{3})\cdot(1,0,g^{{\dagger}}(y_{3}))-(x_{4},y_{4},0)\cdot(1,0,g^{{\dagger}}(y_{3}))|\leq\delta
i.e. (since
z_{2}=z_{3}
)

\Big|x_{4}-\Big(w-z_{2}\big(g^{{\dagger}}(y_{1})-g^{{\dagger}}(y_{3})\big)+\big(y_{1}-y_{3}\big)f^{{\dagger}}(z_{2})\Big)\Big|\leq 4\delta.

(5.18)

Since
p_{4}=\phi(p_{Q})
for some
\delta
-cube
Q\subset E
whose center has
z
-coordinate
z_{0}
, after unwinding definitions we see that
x_{4}\in\big(E\cap\{z=z_{0}\}\big)\cdot\big(1,f(z_{0}),0\big)
and hence (
5.17
) follows from (
5.18
).

Define

\begin{split}F&=\{\big(\delta^{-1/2}z,\ \delta^{-1/2}f^{{\dagger}}(z)\big)\colon z-z_{0}\in Z_{\operatorname{popular}}\},\\
G&=\{\big(y,\ g^{{\dagger}}(y)\big)\colon y\in[0,1]\cap\delta^{1/2}\mathbb{Z}\}.\end{split}

Since
f^{{\dagger}}
is 1-Lipschitz and
g^{{\dagger}}
is 4-Lipschitz,
F
and
G
are
(\delta^{1/2},1,C)_{2}
-ADsets for some absolute constant
C
.
Let
\mathcal{H}
be the set of
6
-tuples

\big(y_{1},\ g^{{\dagger}}(y_{1}),\ \delta^{-1/2}z_{2},\ \delta^{-1/2}f^{{\dagger}}(z_{2}),\ y_{3},\ g^{{\dagger}}(y_{3})\big),

where
(y_{1},z_{2},y_{3})
corresponds to a triple from
\mathcal{W}^{{\dagger}}
. By construction
\mathcal{H}\subset F\times G\times G
, and
\#\mathcal{H}=\#\mathcal{W}^{{\dagger}}=\#\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\gtrsim\delta^{-3/2+8\eta}\geq\delta^{8\eta}(\#F)(\#G)^{2}
.

Let
\tilde{A}=N_{4\delta^{1/2}}\big(\{\delta^{-1/2}a\colon a\in A\}\big)
. Then
\tilde{A}\subset[0,1]
is a
(\delta^{1/2},1-\sigma,8\delta^{-\eta})_{1}
-ADset, and (
5.17
) says that

\{p\cdot(q-q^{\prime})\colon(p,q,q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{H}\}\subset\tilde{A}.

In particular, if
\eta>0
is selected sufficiently small, then
A_{3}=F
and
A_{1}=A_{2}=G
satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem
5.2
with
\varepsilon_{1}/2
in place of
\varepsilon
and
\delta^{1/2}
in place of
\delta
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{H}
satisfies the hypotheses of Item
(B)
from Theorem
5.2
with
\varepsilon_{1}/2
in place of
\varepsilon
and
\delta^{1/2}
in place of
\delta
. The conclusion of Item
(B)
cannot hold, since (
5.3
) violates the property that
\tilde{A}
is a
(\delta^{1/2},1-\sigma,8\delta^{-\eta})_{1}
-ADset.

We conclude that Conclusion
(A)
from Theorem
5.2
must hold. Let
\ell
and
\ell^{\perp}
be the orthogonal lines from Conclusion
(A)
of Theorem
5.2
. Then by (
5.2
), we have

\mathcal{E}_{\delta}\big(\delta^{1/2}(F\cap N_{\delta^{1/2}}(\ell))\big)\geq\delta^{\varepsilon_{1}/2-1/2}.

Define

Z_{\operatorname{lin}}=\{z\in Z_{\operatorname{popular}}\colon\big(\delta^{-1/2}(z+z_{0}),\delta^{-1/2}f^{{\dagger}}(z+z_{0})\big)\in F\cap R\}.

Then the linear function
L
from the statement of Lemma
5.3
is given by
R
(indeed the graph of
L
is a re-scaling of major axis of
R
) and for each
z\in Z_{\operatorname{lin}},

|f(z)-L(z)|\leq\delta
.

We have
\#Z_{\operatorname{lin}}\geq(\delta^{1/2})^{\varepsilon_{1}}\#Z_{\operatorname{popular}}
, and thus if
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\eta
are sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
), then have
|E\cap(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times Z_{\operatorname{lin}})|\geq\delta^{1+\sigma/2+\varepsilon}
.
∎

The next lemma records what happens when we slice an extremal Kakeya set
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
into horizontal slabs of thickness
\rho^{1/2}
; select the
\rho^{1/2}
neighborhood of a global grain from each of these slices, and apply Lemma
5.3
: we conclude that the slope function
f
is linear at scale
\rho
on each interval of length
\rho^{1/2}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta>0,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a
\eta
-extremal collection of tubes, and let
\rho\in[\delta^{1-\varepsilon},\delta^{\varepsilon}]
. Suppose that there are functions
f\colon[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
and
V\colon E_{\mathbb{T}}\to S^{2}
that satisfy Items
1
and
2
from the statement of Proposition
4.1
, with
\eta
in place of
\varepsilon
.

Then there is a
\varepsilon
-extremal sub-collection
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
and a function
L\colon[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
with the following properties.

•

L
is 2-Lipschitz, and linear on each interval of the form
[n\rho^{1/2},(n+1)\rho^{1/2})
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•

If
p=(x,y,z)\in E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}
, then
|f(z)-L(z)|\leq\rho
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}>0
be small constants to be chosen below. We will select
\eta
very small compared to
\varepsilon_{1}
,
\varepsilon_{1}
very small compared to
\varepsilon_{2}
,
\varepsilon_{2}
very small compared to
\varepsilon_{3}
, and
\varepsilon_{3}
very small compared to
\varepsilon
.

Apply Proposition
3.2
to
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
with
\varepsilon_{1}
in place of
\varepsilon
, and
\rho
as above. We obtain a refinement of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
, and a
\varepsilon_{1}
-extremal collection of
\rho
-tubes,
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
. In what follows, we will describe various sub-collections of
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
. Since
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
is a balanced cover of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
, each of these sub-collections of
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
will induce an analogous sub-collection of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
, of the same relative density.

Apply Proposition
3.2
to
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
with
\varepsilon_{2}
in place of
\varepsilon_{1}
, and
\rho^{1/2}
in place of
\rho
. We obtain a refinement of
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
, and a
\varepsilon_{2}
-extremal collection of
\rho^{1/2}
-tubes,
(\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{T}},\accentset{\approx}{Y})_{\rho^{1/2}}
. After further refining each of these three collections, we may suppose that
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
has constant multiplicity;
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
is a balanced cover of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
, and
(\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{T}},\accentset{\approx}{Y})_{\rho^{1/2}}
is a balanced cover of
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
(and hence also a balanced cover of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
).

Recall that each shading
\accentset{\approx}{Y}(\accentset{\approx}{Y})
is a union of
\rho^{1/2}
-cubes. After further refining
(\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{T}},\accentset{\approx}{Y})_{\rho^{1/2}}
(which again induces a refinement of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
and
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\rho}
), we may suppose that
E_{\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{T}}}
is contained in a union of “slabs” of the form
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[n\rho^{1/2},(n+1)\rho^{1/2}]
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and for each such slab
S
that meets
E_{\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{T}}}
, we have
|S\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}}|\gtrapprox_{\rho}\rho^{1/2+\varepsilon_{1}}
and hence
|S\cap E_{\mathbb{T}}|\gtrapprox_{\delta}\rho^{1/2+\varepsilon_{1}}\delta^{\eta}
.

We shall consider each slab
S
in turn. Let
Z_{S}\subset[n\rho^{1/2},(n+1)\rho^{1/2}]
be the set of values
z_{0}
for which
|E_{\mathbb{T}}\cap\{z=z_{0}\}|\gtrapprox_{\delta}\rho^{2\varepsilon_{1}}\delta^{\eta}
. By Fubini,
|Z_{S}|\geq\rho^{1/2+\varepsilon_{1}}\delta^{\eta}
. Select a choice of
z_{0}\in Z_{S}
. Since
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
satisfies Item
1
from the statement of Proposition
4.1
, by pigeonholing there must be some
w\in\mathbb{R}
so that

|\{p\in E_{\mathbb{T}}\cap\{z=z_{0}\}\colon|p\cdot(1,f(z_{0}),0)-w|\leq\delta\}|\gtrapprox_{\delta}\delta^{1+2\eta}\rho^{2\varepsilon_{1}},

(5.19)

where
|\cdot|
denotes 2-dimensional Lebesgue measure. The set in the LHS of (
5.19
) is contained in the
\delta
-neighborhood of a unit line segment in the
\{z=z_{0}\}
plane, that points in the direction
(1,f(z_{0}),0)
; denote this line segment by
\ell
. Then
\ell
must intersect
\gtrapprox\delta^{2\eta}\rho^{-1/2+2\varepsilon_{1}}

\rho^{1/2}
-cubes from
E_{\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{T}}}
, and hence

\big|N_{\rho^{1/2}}(\ell)\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}}\big|\gtrapprox\big(\delta^{2\eta}\rho^{-1/2+\varepsilon_{1}}\big)\big(\rho^{\varepsilon_{1}}\rho^{\frac{\sigma-3}{2}}\big)=\delta^{2\eta}\rho^{\sigma/2-1+2\varepsilon_{1}}.

We can now verify that if
\eta
and
\varepsilon_{1}
are chosen sufficiently small compared to
\varepsilon_{3}
, then the set
N_{\rho^{1/2}}(L)\cap E_{\tilde{\mathbb{T}}},
along with the function
f
(restricted to
[n\rho^{1/2},(n+1)\rho^{1/2}]
) and the function
V
(restricted to
N_{\rho^{1/2}}(\ell)\cap E_{\mathbb{T}}
) satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma
5.3
, with
\rho
in place of
\delta
and
\varepsilon_{3}
in place of
\varepsilon
.

Applying Lemma
5.3
, we obtain a set
Z_{\operatorname{lin},S}\subset[n\rho^{1/2},(n+1)\rho)
and a linear function
L_{S}
with domain
[n\rho^{1/2},(n+1)\rho)
, so that
|f(z)-L_{S}(z)|\leq\rho
for all
z\in Z_{\operatorname{lin},S}
, and
|Z_{\operatorname{lin},S}|\geq\rho^{1/2+\varepsilon_{3}}
. In particular,
Z_{\operatorname{lin},S}
contains two points
z_{1},z_{2}
that are at least
\rho^{1/2+\varepsilon_{3}}
-separated. Since
f
is 1-Lipschitz,
L_{S}
is linear, and
|f(z_{i})-L_{S}(z_{i})|\leq\rho
for
i=1,2
, we conclude that
L_{S}
has slope at most 2.

Since
Z_{\operatorname{lin},S}\subset Z_{S}
and
|Z_{\operatorname{lin},S}|\geq\rho^{1/2+\varepsilon_{3}}
, we have

\big|E_{\mathbb{T}}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times Z_{\operatorname{lin},S})\big|\geq\rho^{1/2+\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{3}}.

(5.20)

Define
Z_{\operatorname{lin}}=\bigcup_{S}Z_{\operatorname{lin},S}
, where the union is taken over all slabs
S
that meet
E_{\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{T}}}
. Then

\big|E_{\mathbb{T}}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times Z_{\operatorname{lin}})\big|\gtrapprox\rho^{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{3}},

and hence if we define
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}=\mathbb{T}
and
Y^{\prime}(T)=Y(T)\cap(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times Z_{\operatorname{lin}})
, then
\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}|Y^{\prime}(T)|\gtrapprox_{\delta}\delta^{\eta}\rho^{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{3}}
. If
\eta,\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}
are chosen sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
), then
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
is
\varepsilon
-extremal. Finally, we define
L
on
Z_{\operatorname{lin}}
by
L(y)=L_{S}(y)
if
y
is contained in the slab
S
, and we extend
L
to
[-1,1]
by defining
L
to be piecewise linear in each connected interval in
[-1,1]\backslash Z_{\operatorname{lin}}
. If
p=(x,y,z)\in E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}
then
|f(z)-L(z)|\leq\rho
. By construction,
L
is 2-Lipschitz on each interval corresponding to a slab
S
. But since
f
is 1-Lipschitz and the slabs
S
are
\rho^{1/2}
-separated, we conclude that
L
is 2-Lipschitz on
[-1,1]

∎

For the arguments that follow, it will be helpful to restate Lemma
5.4
as follows.

Corollary 5.5
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta>0,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a
\eta
-extremal collection of tubes, and let
\rho\in[\delta^{1-\varepsilon},\delta^{\varepsilon}]
. Suppose that there are functions
f\colon[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
and
V\colon E_{\mathbb{T}}\to S^{2}
that satisfy Items
1
and
2
from the statement of Proposition
4.1
, with
\eta
in place of
\varepsilon
.

Then there is a
\varepsilon
-extremal sub-collection
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
and a set
\mathcal{R}
of
\rho\times\rho^{1/2}
rectangles in
[0,1]^{2}
with the following properties:

(a)

Each rectangle has slope in
[-2,2]
.

(b)

The projections of the rectangles to the
x
-axis are disjoint.

(c)

If
p=(x,y,z)\in E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}
, then
(z,f(z))\in\bigcup_{\mathcal{R}}R
.

(d)

For each
R\in\mathcal{R}
, there are points
p=(x,y,z),p^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}
with
|z-z^{\prime}|\geq\rho^{1/2+\varepsilon}
, and
(z,f(z)),(z^{\prime},f(z^{\prime}))\in R

Corollary
5.5
follows immediately by applying Lemma
5.4
with
\rho/2
in place of
\rho
; the rectangles
R
are precisely the
\rho\times(\rho/2)^{1/2}
rectangles whose major axis is the graph of
L
above intervals of the form
[n(\rho/2)^{1/2},(n+1)(\rho/2)^{1/2}]
(these are rectangles of length
(\rho/2)^{1/2}
, but they can be extended to rectangles of length
\rho^{1/2}
and made to have disjoint projections to the
x
-axis after a refinement). Conclusion
(d)
follows by further refining
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
and using the fact that
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
is
\varepsilon
-extremal.

The next lemma records what happens when we iterate Corollary
5.5
for many different scales.

Lemma 5.6
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
and
N\geq 1
, there exists
\eta>0,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a
\eta
-extremal collection of tubes. Suppose that there are functions
f\colon[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
and
V\colon E_{\mathbb{T}}\to S^{2}
that satisfy Items
1
and
2
from the statement of Proposition
4.1
, with
\eta
in place of
\varepsilon
.

For each
j=1,\ldots,N
, define
\rho_{j}=\delta^{j/N}
. Then there is a
\varepsilon
-extremal sub-collection
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
and a sets
\{\mathcal{R}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
of
\rho_{j}\times\rho_{j}^{1/2+\varepsilon}
rectangles in
[0,1]^{2}
with the following properties:

(a)

Each rectangle has slope in
[-2,2]
.

(b)

For each
j
, the intervals
\{\pi(R)\colon R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}\}
are
\rho_{j}^{1/2}
-separated, where
\pi\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is the projection to the
x
-axis.

(c)

For each
j>1
and each
R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}
, there is a (necessarily unique) parent rectangle
\tilde{R}\in\mathcal{R}_{j-1}
with
R\subset\tilde{R}
.

(d)

If
p=(x,y,z)\in E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}
, then
(z,f(z))\in\bigcup_{\mathcal{R}_{N}}R
.

Proof.

We iteratively apply Corollary
5.5

N
times, with
\rho=\rho_{j}/2,

j=1,\ldots,N
. We obtain an
\varepsilon
-extremal sub-collection
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
, and sets of
\rho_{j}/2\times(\rho_{j}/2)^{1/2}
rectangles
\mathcal{R}_{j},

j=1,\ldots,N
that satisfy Conclusions
(a)
and
(d)
from Lemma
5.6
. Our next task is to construct new sets of rectangles
\mathcal{R}_{j}^{\prime},

j=1,\ldots,N
that satisfy Conclusions
(a)
,
(c)
, and
(d)
.

First, we refine the collections
\{\mathcal{R}_{j}\},\ j=2,\ldots,N
so that for each
j>1
, each
R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}
intersects at most one rectangle from
\mathcal{R}_{j-1}
.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}_{1}
. For each index
j=2,\ldots,N
and each
R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}
, we will select a sub-rectangle
R^{\prime}\subset R
as follows. Let
\pi_{z}\colon(x,y,z)\mapsto z
. Let
l(R)
and
r(R)
be the leftmost and rightmost points in
\pi_{z}(E_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}})
that satisfy
(l(R),f(l(R))\in R
and
(r(R),f(r(R))\in R
; by Conclusion
(d)
of Corollary
5.5
, we have that
|l(R)-r(R)|\geq\rho_{j}^{1/2+\varepsilon}
. Let
R^{\prime}\subset R
be the smallest
\rho_{j}\times t
rectangle that contains the points
(l(R),f(l(R))
and
(r(R),f(r(R))
. Let
\mathcal{R}_{j}^{\prime}
be the set of all such rectangles
R^{\prime}
as
R
ranges over the elements of
\mathcal{R}_{j}
.

For each
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{j}^{\prime}
(which contains the points
(l(R),f(l(R))
and
(r(R),f(r(R))
), there is a unique
\tilde{R}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{j-1}^{\prime}
that intersects
R^{\prime}
; we have that
\tilde{R}^{\prime}
must also contain the points
(l(R),f(l(R))
and
(r(R),f(r(R))
). This implies that the 2-fold dilate
2R^{\prime}
is contained in the 2-fold dilate
2\tilde{R}^{\prime}
. See Figure
4

\begin{overpic}[figures/figRtildeR]
\put(32.0,6.0){ $\big(\ell(R),f(\ell(R)\big)$}
\put(69.0,18.0){ $\big(r(R),f(r(R)\big)$}
\put(51.0,20.0){ $R^{\prime}$}
\put(65.0,26.0){ $2R^{\prime}$}
\put(55.0,14.0){ $\tilde{R}^{\prime}$}
\put(67.0,7.0){ $2\tilde{R}^{\prime}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 4:
The rectangles
R^{\prime}
and
\tilde{R}^{\prime}
(blue), and their 2-fold dilates (black)

Abusing notation slightly, for each index
j=1,\ldots,N
, we will replace each rectangle
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{j}^{\prime}
with its 2-fold dilate. Then for each
j>1
, each rectangle
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{j}^{\prime}
is contained in a unique parent rectangle
\tilde{R}_{j-1}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{j-1}^{\prime}
.

The rectangles in
\mathcal{R}_{j}^{\prime}
might be longer than
\rho_{j}^{1/2+\varepsilon}
. To fix this, for each index
j
and each
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{j}^{\prime}
, cut
R^{\prime}
into sub-rectangles of dimensions
\rho_{j}\times\rho_{j}^{1/2+\varepsilon}
, and let
\mathcal{R}_{j}^{\prime\prime}
be the resulting set of rectangles. After refining each of the sets
\{\mathcal{R}_{j}^{\prime\prime}\}
by a constant factor (this induces a refinement of
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}
), we can ensure that the resulting collections of rectangles satisfy Conclusions
(b)
and
(c)
.
∎

5.2
Locally linear at many scales implies
C^{2}

In this section, we will prove that if the graph of a function
f\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
can be efficiently covered by rectangles of dimensions
\rho^{1/2}\times\rho
at many scales
\rho
, then
f
must have small
C^{2}
norm. The precise statement is as follows.

Proposition 5.7

(Rectangle condition implies
C^{2}
)
.

Let
\eta>0
, let
N\geq 2
, and let
\delta>0
. For each
j=1,\ldots,N
, define
\rho_{j}=\delta^{j/N}
and let
\mathcal{R}_{j}
be a set of
\rho_{j}\times\rho_{j}^{1/2+\eta}
rectangles in
[0,1]^{2}
with the following properties. In what follows,
\pi\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is the projection to the
x
-axis.

•

Each rectangle has slope in
[-1,1]
.

•

For each
j
, the intervals
\{\pi(R)\colon R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}\}
are
\rho_{j}^{1/2+\eta}
-separated.

•

For each
j>1
and each
R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}
, there is a (necessarily unique) parent rectangle
\tilde{R}\in\mathcal{R}_{j-1}
with
R\subset\tilde{R}
.

Let
D\subset[0,1]
and let
f\colon D\to\mathbb{R}
with
\operatorname{graph}(f)\subset\bigcup_{R\in\mathcal{R}_{N}}R
.

Then there exists a function
g\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
so that

1.

|f(x)-g(x)|\leq\delta
for all
x\in D
.

2.

|g^{\prime\prime}(x)|\lesssim\delta^{-1/N-2\eta}
for all
x\in[0,1]
.

3.

If
x\in\pi(R)
for some
R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}
, then
|g^{\prime}(x)-\operatorname{slope}(R)|\lesssim\frac{\rho_{j}}{\rho_{j+1}^{1/2+\eta}}
.

The next lemma describes the basic building block of the function
g
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
x_{1}<x_{2}
and let
L
be the line segment from
(x_{1},y_{1})
to
(x_{2},y_{2})
. Let
a>0
. Then there is a function
G\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with the following properties.

•

L\subset\operatorname{graph}(G)
.

•

G(x)=0
for
x\in\mathbb{R}\backslash[x_{1}-a,x_{2}+a]
.

•

Let
h=|y_{1}|+|y_{2}|
and let
s=\frac{y_{2}-y_{1}}{x_{2}-x_{1}}
. Then
|G^{\prime}(x)|\lesssim h/a+|s|
and
|G^{\prime\prime}(x)|\lesssim h/a^{2}+|s|/a
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
be a smooth (weakly) monotone increasing function with
\phi(x)=0
for
0\leq x\leq 1/3
and
\phi(x)=1
for
2/3\leq x\leq 1
. We will choose
\phi
so that
|\phi^{\prime}(x)|\leq 10
and
|\phi^{\prime\prime}(x)|\leq 10
for
0\leq x\leq 1
. Define
\phi_{a}(x)=\phi(x/a)
. Then
\phi_{a}(0)=0
,
\phi_{a}(a)=1
,
(\phi_{a})_{+}^{\prime}(0)=(\phi_{a})_{-}^{\prime}(a)=0
, and
(\phi_{a})_{+}^{\prime\prime}(0)=(\phi_{a})_{-}^{\prime\prime}(a)=0
. We also have
|\phi_{a}^{\prime}(x)|\lesssim a^{-1}
and
|\phi_{a}^{\prime\prime}(x)|\lesssim a^{-2}
for
x\in[0,a].

Define
\psi_{a}(x)=(x-a)\phi_{a}(x)
. Then
\psi_{a}(0)=\psi_{a}(a)=0
,
(\psi_{a})_{+}^{\prime}(0)=0
,
(\psi_{a})_{-}^{\prime}(a)=1
, and
(\psi_{a})_{+}^{\prime\prime}(0)=(\psi_{a})_{-}^{\prime\prime}(a)=0
. We also have
|\psi_{a}^{\prime}(x)|\lesssim 1
and
|\psi_{a}^{\prime\prime}(x)|\lesssim a^{-1}
for
x\in[0,a]
.

Let

G(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}y_{1}\phi_{a}(x-x_{1}+a)+s\psi_{a}(x-x_{1}+a),&\quad x\leq x_{1},\\
s(x-x_{1})+y_{1},&\quad x_{1}<x<x_{2},\\
y_{2}\phi_{a}(a+x_{2}-x)-s\psi_{a}(a+x_{2}-x),&\quad x\geq x_{2}.\end{array}\right.\qed

The function
g
from
5.7
will be a sum of the functions
G
from Lemma
5.8
, with one function
G
for each rectangle
R
. We turn to the details.

Proof of Lemma
5.7
.

For each
R\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
, let
\tilde{L}(R)
be the line segment coaxial with
R
. Next, for
2\leq k\leq N
and for each
R\in\mathcal{R}_{k}
, let
\tilde{s}(R)=\operatorname{slope}(R)-\operatorname{slope}(\tilde{R})
, where
\tilde{R}\in\mathcal{R}_{k-1}
is the parent of
R
. Let
(x_{1},y_{1})
,
(x_{2},y_{2})
be the endpoints of the line segment coaxial with
R
. Let
\tilde{y}_{1},\tilde{y}_{2}
be selected so that
(x_{1},\tilde{y}_{1}),(x_{2},\tilde{y}_{2})
lie on the line segment coaxial with
\tilde{R}
. Let
\tilde{L}(R)
be the line segment from
(x_{1},y_{1}-\tilde{y}_{1})
to
(x_{2},y_{2}-\tilde{y}_{2})
. Finally, let
G_{R}(x)
be the function defined in Lemma
5.8
with
L=\tilde{L}(R)
and
a=\rho_{k}^{1/2+\eta}/2
.

Define

g(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}_{k}}G_{R}(x).

We will verify that this function satisfies the three conclusions of Lemma
5.7
. Conclusion 1 follows immediately from the definition of
g
and the definition of the functions
\{G_{R}\}
.

For conclusion 2, observe that for each
1\leq k\leq N
and each
R\in\mathcal{R}_{k}
, we have
x_{2}-x_{1}\gtrsim\rho_{k}^{1/2+\eta}
and
|y_{1}-\tilde{y}_{1}|,|y_{2}-\tilde{y}_{2}|\leq\rho_{k-1}
. We also have
|\operatorname{slope}(\tilde{L}(R))|\leq\rho_{k-1}/\rho_{k}^{1/2+\eta}
. This implies that

|G_{R}^{\prime\prime}(x)|\lesssim\frac{\rho_{k-1}}{\rho_{k}^{1+2\eta}}\leq\delta^{-\frac{1}{N}-2\eta\frac{k}{N}}\quad\textrm{for all}\ x\in\mathbb{R}.

(5.21)

We have

|g^{\prime\prime}(x)|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Big|\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}_{k}}G_{R}^{\prime\prime}(x)\Big|.

(5.22)

Recall that for each index
k=1,\ldots,N
, the intervals
\{\pi(R)\colon R\in\mathcal{R}_{k}\}
are
\rho_{k}^{1/2+\eta}
-separated. This implies that the functions
\{G_{R}\colon R\in\mathcal{R}_{k}\}
have disjoint support. Thus for each
k=1,\ldots,N
, at most one function from
\{G_{R}:R\in\mathcal{R}_{k}\}
can contribute to the inner sum in (
5.22
). Thus by (
5.21
) we have

|g^{\prime\prime}(x)|\lesssim\sum_{k=1}^{N}\delta^{-\frac{1}{N}-2\eta\frac{k}{N}}\lesssim\delta^{-\frac{1}{N}-2\eta}.

For conclusion 3, observe that if
R\in\mathcal{R}_{k}
, then

|G_{R}^{\prime}(x)|\lesssim\frac{\rho_{k-1}}{\rho_{k}^{1/2+\eta}}\quad\textrm{for all}\ x\in\mathbb{R}.

(5.23)

Next, if
R_{0}\in\mathcal{R}_{k}
and
x\in\pi(R_{0})
, then

\operatorname{slope}(R_{0})=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}}G^{\prime}_{R}(x).

Thus

|g^{\prime}(x)-\operatorname{slope}(R_{0})|\leq\sum_{j=k+1}^{N}\Big|\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}}G^{\prime}_{R}(x)\Big|.

Again, for each index
k=1,\ldots,N
the functions
\{G_{R}\colon R\in\mathcal{R}_{k}\}
have disjoint support. Thus for each
j=k+1,\ldots,N
, at most one function
G_{R},\ R\in\mathcal{R}_{j}
can contribute to the above sum. Thus by (
5.23
) we have

|g^{\prime}(x)-\operatorname{slope}(R_{0})|\leq\sum_{j=k+1}^{N}\frac{\rho_{j-1}}{\rho_{j}^{1/2+\eta}}\lesssim\frac{\rho_{k}}{\rho_{k+1}^{1/2+\eta}}.\qed

5.3
Proof of Proposition
5.1

Proposition
5.1
now follows easily from Lemma
5.6
and Proposition
5.7
. The details are as follows.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Let
\delta_{0}>0^{\prime}
and
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}>0
be small constants to be determined below. We will select
\varepsilon_{1}
very small compared to
\varepsilon_{2}
, and
\varepsilon_{2}
very small compared to
\varepsilon
. Let
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}^{\prime}]
and let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a collection of
\delta
-tubes that satisfies the conclusions of Proposition
4.1
, with
\varepsilon_{1}
in place of
\varepsilon
. Apply Lemma
5.6
to
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
with
\varepsilon_{2}
in place of
\varepsilon
and
N=1/\varepsilon_{2}
, and let
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})_{\delta}
and
\{\mathcal{R}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
be the output of this lemma.

Apply Proposition
5.7
to the family of rectangles
\{\mathcal{R}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
. The resulting function
g
almost satisfies Conclusion
1^{\prime}
of Proposition
5.1
, except we only have the bound
\|g\|_{C^{2}}\lesssim\delta^{-3\varepsilon_{2}}.
This can be remedied by restricting
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})_{\delta}
to a slab of dimensions roughly
1\times 1\times\delta^{3\varepsilon_{2}}
and then applying a vertical re-scaling using Lemma
3.5
. After this re-scaling, the re-scaled function
g
will satisfy
\|g\|_{C^{2}}\leq 1
, as desired, and the resulting collection of tubes
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta^{\prime}}
will continue to satisfy Conclusion 2 of Proposition
5.1
. If
\delta_{0}^{\prime}
is chosen sufficiently small (depending on
\delta_{0}
and
\varepsilon
) and if
\varepsilon_{2}
is chosen sufficiently small, then we will have
\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta_{0}
, and
(\mathbb{T}^{\prime},Y^{\prime})_{\delta}^{\prime}
will be
\varepsilon
-extremal.
∎

6
The slope function has large slope

Our goal in this section is to strength the conclusions of Proposition
5.1
by showing that the global grains slope function has derivative bounded away from 0. More precisely, we will prove the following.

Proposition 6.1
.

For all
\varepsilon,\delta_{0}>0
, there exists
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
and a
\varepsilon
-extremal set of tubes
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
with the following properties:

1^{\prime\prime}.

Global grains with nonsingular
C^{2}
slope function.

There is a function
f\colon[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
with
1\leq|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2
and
|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|\leq 1/100
for all
z\in[-1,1],
so that
\big(E_{\mathbb{T}}\cap\{z=z_{0}\}\big)\cdot(1,f(z_{0}),0)
is a
(\delta,1-\sigma,\delta^{-\varepsilon})_{1}
-ADset for each
z_{0}\in[-1,1]
.

2.
