Local grains with Lipschitz plane map

(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
has a 1-Lipschitz plane map
V
. For all
\rho\in[\delta,1]
and all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
V(p)\cdot\big(B(p,\rho^{1/2})\cap E_{\mathbb{T}}\big)
is a
(\rho,1-\sigma,\delta^{-\varepsilon})_{1}
-ADset.

We will begin with a technical lemma, which says that if
U
is a segment of a tube
T
, and if the image of a large subset of
U
under a linear projection
p\mapsto p\cdot v
is an ADset, then
v
must be nearly orthogonal to
\operatorname{dir}(T)
. The precise version is as follows.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\varepsilon,\eta>0
,
0\leq\alpha<1,
and
0<\delta\leq\rho\leq 1
. Let
T
be a
\delta
-tube, let
U\subset T
be a tube segment of length
\rho^{1/2}
, and let
F\subset U
with

|F|\geq\delta^{\eta}|U|.

(6.1)

Let
v\in S^{2}
, and suppose
v\cdot F
is a
(\rho,\alpha,\delta^{-\eta})_{1}
-ADset. Then

|v\cdot\operatorname{dir}(T)|\lesssim\delta^{\frac{-2\eta}{1-\alpha}}\rho^{1/2}.

(6.2)

Proof.

We will suppose that
|v\cdot\operatorname{dir}(T)|\geq\rho^{1/2}
, since otherwise there is nothing to prove. Let
\tau=|v\cdot\operatorname{dir}(T)|
. Divide
U
into sub-segments of length
\rho/\tau
(by hypothesis
\rho/\tau\leq\rho^{1/2}
, so there is at least one such segment), and let
\mathcal{S}
be this set of segments. For each
S\in\mathcal{S}
,
v\cdot S
is an interval of length
\sim\rho
, and no point
x\in\mathbb{R}
is contained in more than 10 of the sets
\{v\cdot S\colon S\in\mathcal{S}\}
. By (
6.1
), we have

\#\{S\in\mathcal{S}\colon S\cap F\neq\emptyset\}\geq\delta^{\eta}\tau\rho^{-1/2},

and thus

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}(v\cdot F)\gtrsim\delta^{\eta}\tau\rho^{-1/2}.

(6.3)

On the other hand,
v\cdot F\subset v\cdot U
is contained in an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
of length
\sim\rho^{1/2}\tau
, and hence

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}(v\cdot F)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\rho}((v\cdot F)\cap I)\leq\delta^{-\eta}(|I|/\rho)^{\alpha}\lesssim\delta^{-\eta}(\rho^{-1/2}\tau)^{\alpha}.

(6.4)

Comparing (
6.3
) and (
6.4
), we conclude that
\tau\lesssim\delta^{\frac{-2\eta}{1-\alpha}}\rho^{1/2}
, as desired.
∎

The next lemma says that if
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
is an extremal set of tubes pointing in different directions with
C^{2}
slope function
f
, then
f
cannot have small derivative on any “thick” slab that contains (at least) an average amount of the mass of
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
. This lemma contains the key geometric argument needed to prove Proposition
6.1
.

Lemma 6.3
.

For all
\varepsilon>0,
there exists
\eta>0,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a set of tubes that satisfies the conclusions of Proposition
5.1
, with
\eta
in place of
\varepsilon
. Suppose in addition that the tubes in
\mathbb{T}
point in
\delta
-separated directions. Let
J\subset[-1,1]
be an interval, with
\delta^{1/2}\leq|J|\leq\delta^{\varepsilon}
. Suppose that

\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|Y(T)\cap(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times J)|\geq\delta^{\eta}|J|,

(6.5)

and that
(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times J)\cap\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}}Y(T)
can be covered by a union of
\leq\delta^{-\eta}|J|^{-2+\sigma}
cubes of side-length
J
.

Then the slope function
f\colon[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
from Proposition
5.1
satisfies

|f^{\prime}(z)|\geq|J|\quad\textrm{for all}\ z\in J.

(6.6)

Proof.

Suppose not, i.e. there exists
z_{0}\in J
with
|f^{\prime}(z_{0})|<|J|
. Since
\|f\|_{C^{2}}\leq 1
, we have
|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2|J|
for all
z\in J
. We will obtain a contradiction.

Define
\rho=|J|^{2}
, so
\delta\leq\rho\leq\delta^{2\varepsilon}
. After a translation and rotation, we may suppose that
J=[0,\rho^{1/2}]
and
f(0)=0
(note that after this rotation, our shadings
Y(T)
will be unions of cubes that are no longer axis parallel, but this is harmless for the arguments that follow).

After a refinement, we may suppose there is a number
\delta^{-\sigma+\eta}\leq\mu\leq\delta^{-\sigma-\eta}
so that for each point
p\in(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times J)\cap\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}}Y(T)
, we have
\#\mathbb{T}(p)=\mu
. Define
F=(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times J)\cap\bigcup_{T\in\mathbb{T}}Y(T)
. By (
6.5
), we have
\delta^{\sigma+2\eta}|J|\lessapprox|F|\lessapprox\delta^{\sigma-2\eta}|J|
. In the arguments that follow, we will describe several refinements of
F
that endow the set with additional structural properties. Each of these refinements will continue to be a union of (rotated)
\delta
-cubes.

Refinement 1: Every global grain is popular.
Recall that
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
satisfies Item
1^{\prime}
from Proposition
5.1
. Informally, this says that
E_{\mathbb{T}}
is a union of global grains (rectangular prisms of dimensions
1\times\delta\times\delta
), and that most of these grains are almost full. More precisely, we can select a subset
F_{1}\subset F
with
|F_{1}|\geq\delta^{2\eta}|F|
so that the following property holds: For each
z_{1}\in J
and each
r\in(F\cap\{z=z_{1}\})\cdot(1,f(z_{1}),0)
, define the the
1\times\delta\times\delta
rectangular prism

G_{r,z_{1}}=\{|z-z_{1}|\leq\delta\}\cap\{|(x,y)\cdot(1,f(z_{1}))-r|\leq\delta\}.

(6.7)

Then
G_{r,z_{1}}
has large intersection with
F_{1}
; specifically, we have

|G_{r,z_{1}}\cap F_{1}|\geq\delta^{2\eta}|G_{r}|\gtrsim\delta^{2+2\eta}.

(6.8)

Refinement 2: Every global grain intersects a common
\{y=y_{0}\}
plane.

Each of the global grains
G_{r,z_{1}}
from (
6.7
) will intersect each plane of the form
\{y=y_{0}\}
for
0\leq y_{0}\leq 1
. While it may not be true that every set
G_{r,z_{1}}\cap F_{1}
will intersect every plane
\{y=y_{0}\}
, it is true that a typical set of this form is likely to intersect a typical plane. Thus we can find a choice of plane
\{y=y_{0}\}
that intersects a large proportion of the sets
G_{r,z_{1}}\cap F_{1}
. We turn to the details.

Recall that
F
(and hence
F_{1}
) can be covered by a union of
\leq\delta^{-\eta}\rho^{-1+\sigma/2}
cubes of side-length
\rho^{1/2}
; weakening this inequality by a constant factor, we may suppose that these cubes are aligned with the
\rho^{1/2}
-grid. Denote the union of these cubes by
K
. Let
Y_{\operatorname{bad}}\subset[-1,1]
be the set of
y_{0}
for which
|\{y=y_{0}\}\cap K|\geq\delta^{-7\eta}\rho^{1/2+\sigma/2}
. By Fubini we have
|Y_{\operatorname{bad}}|\leq\delta^{4\eta}.
If
G_{r,z_{1}}
is a set of the form (
6.7
), then
|G_{r,z_{1}}\cap(\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\operatorname{bad}}\times\mathbb{R})|\lesssim\delta^{4\eta+2}
, and hence if
G_{r,z_{1}}
satisfies (
6.8
), then

|(G_{r,z_{1}}\cap F_{1})\backslash(\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\operatorname{bad}}\times\mathbb{R})|\geq|G_{r,z_{1}}\cap F_{1}|/2.

Thus if we define
F_{1}^{\prime}=F_{1}\backslash(\mathbb{R}\times Y_{\operatorname{bad}}\times\mathbb{R})
, then (in light of Refinement 1) we have
|F_{1}^{\prime}|\geq|F_{1}|/2
.

By Fubini, there is a choice of
y_{0}\in[-1,1]\backslash Y_{\operatorname{bad}}
so that

\Big|\{y=y_{0}\}\cap F_{1}^{\prime}\Big|\geq\frac{1}{2}|F_{1}^{\prime}|,

where on the LHS,
|\cdot|
denotes 2-dimensional Lebesgue measure and on the RHS,
|\cdot|
denotes 3-dimensional Lebesgue measure. Let
X=\bigcup G_{r,z_{1}}
, where the union is taken over all grains
G_{r,z_{1}}
with
\{y=y_{0}\}\cap G_{r,z_{1}}\cap F_{1}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, and let
F_{2}=F_{1}^{\prime}\cap X
. By (
6.8
), we have
|F_{2}|\geq\delta^{4\eta}|F|
.

Step 3: Local grains at scale
\rho
extend to global grains.

Recall that by Item
1^{\prime}
from Proposition
5.1
, each slice
E_{\mathbb{T}}\cap\{z=z_{0}\}
is a union of global grains. A priori, the global grains for one value of
z_{0}
need not be related to the grains for a different value of
z_{0}
(though of course the slopes of these grains are related by the regularity of the slope function
f
). In this step, we will show that the local grains coming from Item 2 of Proposition
5.1
ensure consistency between the global grains for different slices
\{z=z_{0}\}
. We turn to the details.

Cover the rectangle
[-1,1]\times\{y=y_{0}\}\times J
by interior-disjoint squares of side-length
\rho^{1/2}=|J|
. Since
y_{0}\not\in Y_{\operatorname{bad}}
, at most
\delta^{-10\eta}\rho^{\frac{\sigma-1}{2}}
of these squares intersect
F_{2}\cap\{y=y_{0}\}.
Denote this set of squares by
\mathcal{S}
.

Let
S\in\mathcal{S}
be a square, with center
p_{S}=(x_{S},y_{0},\rho^{1/2}/2)
. By Item
2
from Proposition
5.1
, we have that
(S\cap F_{2})\cdot V(p_{S})
is a
(\rho,1-\sigma,\delta^{-\eta})_{1}
-ADset. Write
V(p_{S})=(V_{x}(p_{S}),V_{y}(p_{S}),V_{z}(p_{S}))
. Since
S\cap F_{2}\subset\{y=y_{0}\}
, if we define
\tilde{V}(p_{S})=\big(V_{x}(p_{S}),0,V_{z}(p_{S})\big)
, then

(S\cap F_{2})\cdot\tilde{V}(p_{S})\ \textrm{is a}\ (\rho,1-\sigma,\delta^{-\eta})_{1}\textrm{-ADset}.

(6.9)

In particular,
S\cap F_{2}
is contained in a union of at most
\delta^{-\eta}\rho^{\frac{\sigma-1}{2}}
rectangles of dimensions
\rho^{1/2}\times\rho
. If
R\subset S
is such a rectangle, and if
G_{r,z_{1}}
is a grain of the form (
6.7
) that intersects
R
, then since
|f(z_{1})|\leq\rho
, we have
G_{r,z_{1}}\subset\{(x,y,z)\colon(x,z)\in 2R\}
, where
2R
is the
\rho^{1/2}\times 2\rho
rectangle that has the same major axis as
R
. But in light of (
6.9
), this implies that if we define the prism
P_{S}=\{(x,y,z)\colon(x,z)\in S\}
, then

(F_{2}\cap P_{S})\cdot\tilde{V}(p_{S})\ \textrm{is a}\ (\rho,1-\sigma,2\delta^{-\eta})_{1}\textrm{-ADset}.

See Figure
5

\begin{overpic}[figures/figLargeSlope]
\put(5.0,12.0){{\footnotesize$\Delta x:\rho^{1/2}$}}
\put(30.0,12.0){{\footnotesize$\Delta x:\rho^{1/2}$}}
\put(40.0,6.0){{\footnotesize$\Delta x:1$}}
\put(0.0,19.0){{\footnotesize\rotatebox{90.0}{$\Delta z:\rho^{1/2}$}}}
\put(4.0,40.0){{\footnotesize\rotatebox{63.0}{$\Delta y:1$}}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 5:
The rectangular prisms
\{P_{S}\colon S\in\mathcal{S}\}
, and the tubes from
\mathbb{T}
(blue lines) that intersect them. For clarity, only a few tubes (blue lines) have been drawn.

Step 4:
\mathbb{T}
contains parallel tubes.

Many tubes from
\mathbb{T}
must intersect each of the parallel slabs in Figure
5
, and if a tube
T
intersects a slab, this imposes constraints on the possible directions of
v(T)
. In this step, we will show that these constraints force many tubes from
\mathbb{T}
to point in almost the same direction. This contradicts the assumption that the tubes in
\mathbb{T}
point in
\delta
-separated directions. We turn to the details.

For each
T\in\mathbb{T}
, define
F_{2}(T)=Y(T)\cap F_{2}
. Then recalling (
6.5
), we have

\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|F_{2}(T)|\geq\delta^{5\eta}|J|=\delta^{5\eta}\rho^{1/2}.

Recall that each
T\in\mathbb{T}
intersects at most 3 prisms
P_{S},S\in\mathcal{S}
, and for such a prism we have
|F_{2}(T)\cap P_{S}|\leq\rho^{1/2}\delta^{2}
. Thus by pigeonholing there exists a square
S\in\mathcal{S}
and a set
\mathbb{T}_{S}\subset\mathbb{T}
with

\#\mathbb{T}_{S}\gtrsim\delta^{-2}(\#\mathcal{S})^{-1}\gtrsim\delta^{11\eta+2}\rho^{\frac{1-\sigma}{2}},

(6.10)

so that
|F_{2}(T)|\gtrsim\delta^{11\eta}\rho^{1/2}\delta^{2}
for each
T\in\mathbb{T}_{S}
.

But for each such
T\in\mathbb{T}_{S}
, we have that
F_{2}(T)\cdot\tilde{V}(p_{S})\subset(F_{2}\cap P_{S})\cdot\tilde{V}(p_{S})
is a
(\rho,1-\sigma,2\delta^{-\eta})_{1}
-ADset. Thus by Lemma
6.2
, we have

|\operatorname{dir}(T)\cdot\tilde{V}(p_{S})|\leq\delta^{-C_{0}\eta}\rho^{1/2},

where the constant
C_{0}
depends on
\sigma
. But comparing with (
6.10
) and pigeonholing, we conclude that there is a direction
v\in S^{2}
so that

\#\{T\in\mathbb{T}_{S}\colon|\operatorname{dir}(T)-v|\leq\delta\}\geq\delta^{C_{1}\eta}\rho^{-\sigma/2}\geq\delta^{C_{1}\eta-\sigma\varepsilon},

where
C_{1}
is a constant that depends on
\sigma
. If
\eta>0
is selected sufficiently small (depending on
\sigma
and
\varepsilon
), then this contradicts the assumption that the tubes in
\mathbb{T}
point in
\delta
-separated directions.
∎

We are almost ready to prove Proposition
6.1
. First we will prove an intermediate result, which constructs an extremal set of tubes
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
with the property that the shadings
Y(T)
are supported on a thick slab, and the global grains slope function
f
is large on this slab.

Lemma 6.4
.

For all
\varepsilon,\delta_{0}>0
, there exists
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
and a set of tubes
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
that satisfy the conclusions of Proposition
5.1
, as well as the following additional properties. First, for each
T\in\mathbb{T}
we have
Y(T)\subset[-1,1]^{2}\times[0,\delta^{\varepsilon}]
. Second, there is a number
m\geq\delta^{\varepsilon}
so that
m\leq|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2m
for all
z\in[0,\delta^{\varepsilon}]
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}>0
be small constants to be chosen below. We will select
\varepsilon_{1}
very small compared to
\varepsilon_{2}
, and
\varepsilon_{2}
very small compared to
\varepsilon
.

Let
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
and let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
be a collection of tubes that satisfy the conclusions of Proposition
5.1
, with
\varepsilon_{1}
in place of
\varepsilon
. After passing to a sub-collection, we may suppose that the tubes point in
\delta
-separated directions. If
\varepsilon_{1}
is selected sufficiently small, then we may apply Proposition
3.2
with
\delta^{\varepsilon_{2}}
in place of
\rho
, and we can select a refinement
(\mathbb{T}_{1},Y_{1})
of
(\mathbb{T},Y)
so that
E_{\mathbb{T}_{1}}
is covered by
\lesssim\delta^{-\varepsilon_{2}+\sigma-3}
cubes of side-length
\delta^{\varepsilon_{2}}
.

By pigeonholing, we can find an interval
J\subset[-1,1]
of length
\delta^{\varepsilon_{2}}
so that
E_{\mathbb{T}_{1}}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times J)
is covered by
\lesssim\delta^{-2\varepsilon_{2}+\sigma-2}
cubes of side-length
\delta^{\varepsilon_{2}}
, and
\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{1}}|Y_{1}(T)\cap(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times J)|\gtrsim\delta^{2\varepsilon_{2}}|J|
.

Finally, if
\varepsilon_{2}
is selected sufficiently small compared to
\varepsilon
, then we can apply Lemma
6.3
to conclude that
|f^{\prime}(z)|\geq\delta^{\varepsilon}
for all
z\in I
. Since
|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|\leq 1
, there must be a number
m\geq\delta^{\varepsilon}
so that
m\leq|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2m
on
I
. Finally, replace each shading
Y_{1}(T)
by
Y_{1}(T)\cap(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times J)
, and apply a translation in the
z
-direction so that
J=[0,\delta^{\varepsilon}]
.
∎

Proposition
6.1
follows by applying a re-scaling to the collection of tubes fro Lemma
6.4
. The details are as follows.

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

Let
\varepsilon_{1}>0
be a small constant to be chosen below. We will select
\varepsilon_{1}
very small compared to
\varepsilon
. Let
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}^{2}]
and let
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
satisfy the conclusions of Lemma
6.4
with
\varepsilon_{1}
in place of
\varepsilon
(and some
m\geq\delta^{\varepsilon_{1}}
).

Define
\phi(x,y,z)=(x,\frac{m^{2}y}{100},\frac{100z}{m}),
and define
\tilde{f}(z)=100m^{-2}f(mz/100)
. Then

1\leq|\tilde{f}^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2\quad\textrm{and}\quad|\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}|\leq 1/100,\qquad z\in[0,m^{-1}\delta^{\varepsilon_{1}}].

(6.11)

We can modify
\tilde{f}
on
[-1,1]\backslash[0,m^{-1}\delta^{\varepsilon}]
so that (
6.11
) holds for all
z\in[0,1]
. Furthermore, if
(x,y,z)\in[-1,1]^{2}\times J
, then

(x,y,z)\cdot(1,f(z),0)=x+yf(z)=\phi(x,y,z)\cdot(1,\tilde{f}(\tilde{z}),0).

It remains to construct the
\varepsilon
-extremal collection of tubes
(\tilde{\mathbb{T}},\tilde{Y})_{\tilde{\delta}}
that satisfies the conclusions of Proposition
6.1
. We will do this (for some
\tilde{\delta}\leq\delta^{1-C\varepsilon_{1}}
; if
\varepsilon_{1}
is selected sufficiently small, then
C\varepsilon_{1}\leq 1/2
and hence
\tilde{\delta}\leq\delta_{0}
, as required) by applying the scaling
\phi\colon[-1,-1]^{2}\times[0,\delta^{\varepsilon_{1}}]\to[-1,1]^{3}
to
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
, and using Lemma
3.5
.
∎

6.1
Twisted projections

We will re-interpret the conclusion of Proposition
6.1
as a statement regarding twisted projections. First a definition.

Definition 6.1

(Twisted projection)
.

For
f\colon[-1,1]
, we define the
twisted projection

\pi_{f}\colon[-1,1]^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\pi_{f}(x,y,z)=(x+f(z)y,z).

With this definition, the pair
(\mathbb{T},Y)_{\delta}
and the function
f
from the conclusion of Proposition
6.1
satisfies
|\pi_{f}(E_{\mathbb{T}})|\leq\delta^{\sigma-\varepsilon}
.

Recall that at most
\delta^{-\varepsilon}
tubes from an
\varepsilon
-extremal collection of
\delta
-tubes can point in a common direction. Thus after selecting a suitable sub-collection and applying Hölder’s inequality, we have the following consequence of Proposition
6.1
.

Corollary 6.5
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
,
\delta_{0}>0
, there exists
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
; a set
\mathbb{T}
of direction-separated
\delta
-tubes; and a function
f\colon[-1,1]
with
1\leq|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2
and
|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|\leq 1/100
for all
z\in[-1,1],
so that

\Big\|\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}\chi_{\pi_{f}(T)}\Big\|_{L^{3/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\geq\delta^{-1-\sigma/3+\varepsilon}.

(6.12)

7
A
C^{2}
projection theorem

In this section we will introduce a version of Wolff’s circular maximal theorem
[
32
,
21
]
for
C^{2}
curves, and show how this theorem can bound expressions like the LHS of (
6.12
). The goal is to prove the following.

Proposition 7.1
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
\mathbb{T}
be a set of
\delta
-tubes pointing in
\delta
-separated directions. Let
f\colon[-1,1]
with
1\leq|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2
and
|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|\leq 1/100
for all
z\in[-1,1]
. Then

\Big\|\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}\chi_{\pi_{f}(T)}\Big\|_{L^{3/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq\delta^{-1-\varepsilon}.

(7.1)

Comparing Corollary
6.5
and Proposition
7.1
, we conclude that
\sigma=0
. Combined with Proposition
2.1
, this concludes the proof of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.1
′
. All that remains is to prove Proposition
7.1
.

Our main tool for proving Proposition
7.1
is a version of Wolff’s
L^{3/2}
circular maximal function bound for
C^{2}
curves, which is a special case of Theorem 1.7 in
[
25
]
. Before stating the result, we recall Definition 1.6 from
[
25
]
.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a compact interval and let
\mathcal{F}\subset C^{2}(I)
. We say
\mathcal{F}
is a family of cinematic functions, with cinematic constant
K
and doubling constant
D
if the following conditions hold.

1.

\mathcal{F}
has diameter at most
K
.

2.

\mathcal{F}
is a doubling metric space, with doubling constant at most
D
.

3.

For all
f,g\in\mathcal{F},
we have

\inf_{t}\big(|f(t)-g(t)|+|f^{\prime}(t)-g^{\prime}(t)|+|f^{\prime\prime}(t)-g^{\prime\prime}(t)|\big)\geq K^{-1}\|f-g\|_{C^{2}(I)}.

(7.2)

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a compact interval and let
\mathcal{F}\subset C^{2}(I)
be a family of cinematic functions. Then for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
F\subset\mathcal{F}
be a
\delta
-separated set that satisfies the Frostman non-concentration condition

\#\big(F\cap B\big)\leq\delta^{-\varepsilon}(r/\delta)\quad\textrm{for all balls}\ B\subset C^{2}(I)\ \textrm{of diameter}\ r.

(7.3)

Then

\Big\|\sum_{f\in F}\chi_{f^{\delta}}\Big\|_{3/2}\leq\delta^{-\varepsilon},

where
f^{\delta}
is the
\delta
-neighborhood of
\operatorname{graph}(f)
.

We will be interested in the following family of functions. For
f\in C^{2}(I)
and
a,b,d\in\mathbb{R}
, define

g_{a,b,d}(t)=a+bf(t)+dtf(t).

(7.4)

Define
M_{f}=\{g_{a,b,d}\colon a,b,d\in[-1,1]\}
. We will show that under certain conditions on
f
, this is a family of cinematic functions

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
f\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
with
f(0)=0
,
1\leq|f^{\prime}(t)|\leq 2
, and
|f^{\prime\prime}(t)|\leq 1/100
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Then
M_{f}
is a family of cinematic functions, and the implicit constants in Definition
7.1
are bounded independently of the choice of
f
.

Proof.

Let
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2},d_{1},d_{2}\in[-1,1]
, and let
g_{i}=g_{a_{i},b_{i},d_{i}}
. We have
\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{C^{2}}\sim|a_{1}-a_{2}|+|b_{1}-b_{2}|+|d_{1}-d_{2}|
, so it suffices to show that

\inf_{t\in[0,1]}|g_{1}(t)-g_{2}(t)|+|g_{1}^{\prime}(t)-g_{2}^{\prime}(t)|+|g_{1}^{\prime\prime}(t)-g_{2}^{\prime\prime}(t)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\big(|a_{1}-a_{2}|+|b_{1}-b_{2}|+|d_{1}-d_{2}|\big).

(7.5)

Case 1:
|b_{1}-b_{2}|\leq 10|d_{1}-d_{2}|
.

We have

|g_{1}^{\prime\prime}(t)-g_{2}^{\prime\prime}(t)|\geq|d_{1}-d_{2}||f^{\prime}(t)|-\big(|b_{1}-b_{2}|+|d_{1}-d_{2}|t\big)|f^{\prime\prime}(t)|\geq|d_{1}-d_{2}|-11|d_{1}-d_{2}|\ |f^{\prime\prime}(t)|\geq\frac{1}{2}|d_{1}-d_{2}|.

Thus (
7.5
) holds and we are done.

Case 2:
|b_{1}-b_{2}|>10|d_{1}-d_{2}|
.

We have

|g_{1}^{\prime}(t)-g_{2}^{\prime}(t)|\geq|b_{1}-b_{2}|\ |f^{\prime}(t)|-|d_{1}-d_{2}|\big(|f(t)|+|tf^{\prime}(t)|\big)\geq|b_{1}-b_{2}|-\frac{1}{10}|b_{1}-b_{2}|\big(|f(t)|+|tf^{\prime}(t)|\big)\geq\frac{1}{2}|b_{1}-b_{2}|.

Again (
7.5
) holds and we are done.
∎

We can now prove Proposition
7.1
.

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.

Each
T\in\mathbb{T}
has a coaxial line pointing in the direction
(c,d,1)
. Without loss of generality, we may suppose that
c,d\in\delta\mathbb{Z}\cap[-1,1]
, and the directions
(c,d,1)
are distinct for distinct
T\in\mathbb{T}
. By the triangle inequality, for each
c\in[-1,1]
it suffices to prove the estimate

\Big\|\sum_{i=-N}^{N}\chi_{\pi_{f}(T_{i})}\Big\|_{L^{3/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq\delta^{-\varepsilon},

(7.6)

where
N\leq 1/\delta
, and
T_{i}
is a
\delta
-tube whose coaxial line
L_{i}
is of the form
(a_{i},b_{i},0)+\mathbb{R}(c,i\delta,1)
with
a_{i},b_{i}\in[-1,1]
(the original set
\mathbb{T}
might not have a tube pointing in direction
(c,i\delta,1)
for each
-N\leq i\leq N
, but it is harmless to add some additional tubes). For each tube
T_{i}
,
\pi_{f}(T_{i})
is contained in the
2\delta
-neighborhood of the plane curve
\pi_{f}(L_{i})
, which is the graph of the function
g_{i}=g_{a_{i},b_{i},d_{i}}(t)+ct
(recall that
g_{a_{i},b_{i},d_{i}}
is as defined in (
7.4
)). By Lemma
7.3
, the set
\{g_{a,b,d}(t)+ct\colon a,b,d\in[-1,1]\}\subset C^{2}([0,1])
is a family of cinematic functions, and the implicit constants in Definition
7.1
are bounded independently of
f
and
N
(and hence
\delta
).

The estimate (
7.6
) thus follows from Theorem
7.2
, since the set of functions
\{g_{i}\}_{i=-N}^{N}
clearly satisfies the Frostman condition (
7.3
).
∎

8
Proof of Theorem
5.2

In this section we prove Theorem
5.2
. For the reader’s convenience we will recall it here.

Theorem
5.2
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\subset[0,1]^{2}
be
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\eta})
-sets. Then at least one of the following must hold.

(A)

There exist orthogonal lines
\ell
and
\ell^{\perp}
whose
\delta
-neighborhoods cover a significant fraction of
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
and
A_{3}
respectively. In particular,

\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(N_{\delta}(\ell)\cap A_{1})\geq\delta^{\varepsilon-1},\quad\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(N_{\delta}(\ell)\cap A_{2})\geq\delta^{\varepsilon-1},\ \ \textrm{and}\ \ \mathcal{E}_{\delta}(N_{\delta}(\ell^{\perp})\cap A_{3})\geq\delta^{\varepsilon-1}.

((
5.2
))

(B)

Let
G\subset A_{1}\times A_{2}\times A_{3}
be any set satisfying
\mathcal{E}_{\delta}(G)\geq\delta^{\eta-3}
.
Then there exists
\rho\geq\delta
and an interval
I
of length at least
\delta^{-\eta}\rho
such that

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\big(I\cap\{(a_{1}-a_{2})\cdot a_{3}\colon(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in G\}\big)\geq\big(|I|/\rho\big)^{1-\varepsilon}.

((
5.3
))

Our proof of Theorem
5.2
will proceed via a sequence of reductions. In brief, we will describe a sequence of slightly technical assertions, and we will show that Assertion 1 (if true) implies Theorem
5.2
; Assertion 2 implies Assertion 1, and so on. The final assertion we will prove directly. The first assertion will be a variant of Theorem
5.2
where the sets
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},
and
G
satisfy some mild separation and uniformity requirements.

8.1
Reduction to the well separated, uniform case

We begin with a few definitions and preliminary reductions that will simplify some of the technical arguments that follow. Our goal in this section is to state a variant of Theorem
5.2
, which we will call Assertion
1
, and prove that Assertion
1
implies Theorem
5.2
. Before stating Assertion
1
, we will need to introduce some additional notation.

8.1.1
Uniformly dense hypergraphs

Definition 8.1
.

Let
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}
be sets and let
G\subset A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{k}
.
For each
I\subset[k]=\{1,\ldots,k\},
define
A_{I}=\bigtimes_{i\in I}A_{i}
. For each
a_{I}=(a_{i})_{i\in I}\in A_{I}
, define

n_{G}[a_{I}]=\{(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k})\in G\colon a_{i}=g_{i}\ \textrm{for all}\ i\in I\}.

For example if
G\subset A_{1}\times A_{2}\times A_{3}
and
(a_{1},a_{3})\in A_{1}\times A_{3}
, then
n_{G}[(a_{1},a_{3})]
is the set of all edges of the form
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\in G
with
g_{1}=a_{1}
and
g_{3}=a_{3}
.

Definition 8.2
.

If
G\subset A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{k}
and
I\subset[k]
, we define
\pi_{I}\colon G\to A_{I}
to be the projection onto
A_{I}
. If
I=\{i\}
we write
\pi_{i}
instead of
\pi_{\{i\}}
. Finally, if
g\in G
, we write
g_{I}
to denote
\pi_{I}(g)
.

Definition 8.3
.

Let
0<c\leq 1
. We say a
k
-uniform
k
-partite hypergraph
G\subset A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{k}
is
uniformly

c
-
dense
if for each edge
g\in G
and each
I\subset[k]
we have

\#n_{G}[g_{I}]\geq c\prod_{i\not\in I}\#A_{i}.

(8.1)

This definition is useful because some pigeonholing arguments are simplified when the corresponding graph is uniformly
c
-dense, and if
G
is uniformly
c
-dense then its induced subgraphs will still be dense (though possibly not uniformly dense). As we will see below in Lemma
8.2
, every (hyper)graph
G
has a uniformly
c
-dense refinement, where
c
is comparable to the average density of
G
.

8.1.2
The first reduction

With these definitions, we can now state Assertion 1.

Assertion 1
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\subset[0,1]^{2}
be
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\eta})
-sets that satisfy the separation condition

\operatorname{diam}(A_{i})\leq\frac{1}{10},\ i=1,2,3;\quad\operatorname{dist}(A_{1},A_{2})\geq\frac{1}{2};\quad\operatorname{dist}(A_{3},0)\geq\frac{1}{2}.

(8.2)

Let
G\subset A_{1}\times A_{2}\times A_{3}
be uniformly
\delta^{\eta}
-dense. Then at least one of the following must hold.

(A)

There exist orthogonal lines
\ell
and
\ell^{\perp}
that satisfy (
5.2
).

(B)

There exists
\rho\geq\delta
and an interval
I
of length at least
\delta^{-\eta}\rho
that satisfies (
5.3
).

Proposition 8.1
.

Assertion
1
implies Theorem
5.2
.

The main tool we will use to prove Proposition
8.1
is the following hypergraph refinement lemma. It says that if a
k
-uniform
k
-partite hypergraph
G
has average density
d
, then it has a
c
-dense refinement for some
c\sim d
.

Lemma 8.2

(Hypergraph refinement lemma)
.

Let
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}
be sets, let
G\subset A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{k}
be a
k
-uniform hypergraph with density
d=\#G/\prod_{i=1}^{k}\#A_{i}
, and let
0<\varepsilon<1
. Then there exists a uniformly
(2^{-k}\varepsilon d)
-dense subgraph
G^{\prime}\subset G
with
\#G^{\prime}\geq(1-\varepsilon)\#G
.

Lemma
8.2
was proved by Dvir and Gopi
[
12
]
in the special case
k=2
and
\varepsilon=1/2
. A similar proof works in general; we give the details below.

Proof.

For each nonempty multi-index
I\subset[k]
, define
A_{I}=\bigtimes_{i\in I}A_{i}.
To begin, define
A_{I}^{\prime}=A_{I}
for each multi-index
I
, and define
G^{\prime}=G
. Next, if there exists a multi-index
I
and an element
g_{I}\in A_{I}^{\prime}
with

\#n_{G^{\prime}}[g_{I}]<\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{k}}\frac{\#G}{\prod_{i\in I}\#A_{i}},

(8.3)

then we remove
g_{I}
from
A_{I}^{\prime}
, and we remove all of the edges
n_{G^{\prime}}[g_{I}]
from
G^{\prime}
. We repeat this process until no
g_{I}\in A_{I}^{\prime}
fits the removal criteria (
8.3
) for any multi-index
I
.

Each
g_{I}\in A_{I}^{\prime}
that is removed deletes at most
\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{k}}\frac{\#G}{\prod_{i\in I}\#A_{i}}
edges from
G
. Thus the number of edges deleted by this procedure is at most

\sum_{I\subset[k]}\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{k}}\frac{\#G}{\prod_{i\in I}\#A_{i}}\#A_{I}=\sum_{I\subset[k]}\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{k}}\#G=\varepsilon\#G.\qed

After this procedure has finished, the surviving graph
G^{\prime}
is uniformly
(2^{-k}\varepsilon d)
-dense.

Remark 8.1
.

Let
G\subset A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{k}
be uniformly
c
-regular, let
1\leq j\leq k
, and suppose that the projection map
\pi_{j}
is onto. Let
A_{j}^{\prime}\subset A_{j}
, and define
\tilde{A}_{i}=A_{i}
for
i\neq j
, and
\tilde{A}_{j}=A_{j}^{\prime}
. Let
G^{\prime}=G\cap(\tilde{A}_{1}\times\ldots\times\tilde{A}_{k}),
be the corresponding induced subgraph. Then
\#G^{\prime}/\prod_{i=1}^{k}\#\tilde{A}_{i}\geq c
.

G^{\prime}\subset\tilde{A}_{1}\times\ldots\times\tilde{A}_{k}
need not be uniformly
c
-regular, but by Lemma
8.2
it has a uniformly
2^{-k+1}c
-regular subgraph
G^{\prime\prime}\subset G^{\prime}
with
\#G^{\prime\prime}\geq\frac{1}{2}\#G^{\prime}
. We will use this observation frequently in the arguments below.

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

Suppose Assertion
1
holds. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Let
\eta_{1},\delta_{1}
be the output from Assertion
1
, with
\varepsilon/2
in place of
\varepsilon
. Let
\eta=\eta_{1}/100
,
\delta_{0}=\delta_{1}
. Let
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},G
satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem
5.2
with this value of
\eta
and some
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. We must show that at least one of the Conclusions
(A)
or
(B)
from Theorem
5.2
hold for this value of
\varepsilon
and
\eta
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a cover of
[0,1]^{6}
by sets of the form
{\bf B}=B_{1}\times B_{2}\times B_{3}
, where
B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
are balls of radius
\delta^{6\eta}
; we can construct such a cover with
\#\mathcal{B}\lesssim\delta^{-36\eta}.
This induces a decomposition
G=\bigcup_{\mathcal{B}}G_{{\bf B}},
where
G_{{\bf B}}=G\cap(B_{1}\times B_{2}\times B_{3})
. Since
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
are
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\eta})
-sets, we have

\#\{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in G\colon|a_{3}|\geq\delta^{5\eta},\ |a_{1}-a_{2}|\geq\delta^{5\eta}\}\geq\frac{1}{2}\#G.

Thus by pigeonholing, there exists an element
{\bf B}=(B_{1},B_{2},B_{3})\in\mathcal{B}
with
\operatorname{dist}(B_{1},0)\geq\delta^{5\eta}-\delta^{6\eta}
and
\operatorname{dist}(B_{1},B_{2})\geq\delta^{5\eta}-\delta^{6\eta}
so that
\#G_{{\bf B}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\#G/\#\mathcal{B}\gtrsim\delta^{36\eta}\#G
. Let
A_{i}^{\prime}=A_{i}\cap B_{i},\ i=1,2,3
. After applying a common translation to the sets
A_{1}^{\prime}
and
A_{2}^{\prime}
(this translation is harmless since it preserves expressions of the form
a_{1}-a_{2}
with
a_{1}\in A_{1}^{\prime}
and
a_{2}\in A_{2}^{\prime}
; we may also assume it sends
\delta
-squares to
\delta
-squares), we may assume that
A_{1}^{\prime}
and
A_{2}^{\prime}
are contained in
B(0,r),
with
r=\operatorname{dist}(B_{2},B_{3})\gtrsim\delta^{5\eta}
. Let
A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
(resp
A_{2}^{\prime\prime}
) be the image of
A_{1}^{\prime}
under the map
(x,y)\mapsto(x/r,y/r)
. Then
A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
A_{2}^{\prime\prime}
are
(\delta,1,\delta^{-12\eta})_{2}
-ADsets that are contained in balls of radius
\leq 1/100
that are
1/2
-separated. Similarly, let
A_{3}^{\prime\prime}
be the image of
A_{3}^{\prime}
under the map
(x,y)\mapsto(x/r^{\prime},y/r^{\prime})
, with
r^{\prime}=\operatorname{dist}(B_{1},0)\gtrsim\delta^{5\eta}
; then
A_{3}^{\prime\prime}
is a
(\delta,1,\delta^{-12\eta})_{2}
-ADset that is contained in a ball of radius
\leq 1/100
that has distance
\geq 1/2
from the origin.

Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the translation and dilation described above that we applied to
A_{1}^{\prime}
and
A_{2}
, and let
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the dilation that was applied to
A_{3}^{\prime}
. Define
G^{\prime}=\phi\otimes\phi\otimes\psi(G_{{\bf B}})
. The sets
A_{1}^{\prime\prime},A_{2}^{\prime\prime},A_{3}^{\prime\prime}
and
G^{\prime}
might no longer be unions of
\delta
-cubes, but it is harmless to replace them with the unions of
\delta
-cubes that they intersect. The sets
A_{1}^{\prime\prime},A_{2}^{\prime\prime},A_{3}^{\prime\prime}
now satisfy (
8.2
).

Apply Lemma
8.2
with
\varepsilon=\delta^{20\eta}
to
G^{\prime}
, and let
G^{\prime\prime}
denote the resulting refinement; this set is uniformly
\delta^{40\eta}
-dense. We now apply Assertion
1
to
G^{\prime\prime}\subset A_{1}^{\prime\prime}\times A_{2}^{\prime\prime}\times A_{3}^{\prime\prime}
; we have that
A_{1}^{\prime\prime},A_{2}^{\prime\prime},A_{3}^{\prime\prime}
satisfy (
8.2
), and since each
A_{i}^{\prime\prime}
is a
(\delta,1,\delta^{-12\eta})
-ADset, it is also a
(\delta,1,\delta^{-100\eta})
-set. We have that
G^{\prime\prime}
is uniformly
\delta^{100\eta}
-dense.

Suppose Conclusion
(A)
of Assertion
1
is true, i.e. there are orthogonal lines
\ell_{0}
and
\ell_{0}^{\perp}
with

|N_{\delta}(\ell_{0})\cap A_{1}^{\prime\prime}|\geq\delta^{\varepsilon/2}|A_{1}|,\quad|N_{\delta}(\ell_{0})\cap A_{2}^{\prime\prime}|\geq\delta^{\varepsilon/2}|A_{2}|,\ \ \textrm{and}\ \ |N_{\delta}(\ell_{0}^{\perp})\cap A_{3}^{\prime\prime}|\geq\delta^{\varepsilon/2}|A_{3}|.

Let
\ell=\phi^{-1}(\ell_{0})
,
\ell^{\perp}=\phi^{-1}(\ell_{0}^{\perp})
. Then
|N_{\delta}(\ell)\cap A_{1}|\geq\delta^{100\eta}|N_{\delta}(\ell)\cap A_{1}^{\prime\prime}|\geq\delta^{1+\varepsilon/2+100\eta}\geq\delta^{1+\varepsilon}
and similarly for
|N_{\delta}(\ell)\cap A_{2}|
and
|N_{\delta}(\ell^{\perp})\cap A_{3}|
; thus Conclusion
(A)
from Theorem
5.2
holds.

Next, suppose Conclusion
(B)
of Assertion
1
is true, i.e. there exists
\rho\geq\delta
and an interval
I
of length at least
\delta^{-100\eta}\rho
so that (
5.3
) holds for
A_{1}^{\prime\prime},A_{2}^{\prime\prime},A_{3}^{\prime\prime}
and
G^{\prime\prime}
with
\varepsilon/2
in place of
\varepsilon
. Unwinding definitions, we can verify that this implies Conclusion
(B)
from Theorem
5.2
.
∎

8.2
A two-ends condition on tubes

If Conclusion
(A)
of Theorem
5.2
holds, then a significant fraction of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
must be contained in the thin neighborhood of a line. The next assertion says that if
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
satisfy a mild Frostman non-concentration condition on lines, then a strong version of Conclusion
(B)
must hold.

Assertion 2
.

For all
0<\varepsilon,\zeta<1
, there exists
\lambda=\lambda(\varepsilon)>0
,
\alpha=\alpha(\varepsilon,\zeta)>0
, and
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(\varepsilon,\zeta)>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\subset[0,1]^{2}
be
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\lambda})
-sets that satisfy the separation condition (
8.2
). Suppose that
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
satisfy the non-concentration near lines estimate

|A_{i}\cap N_{r}(\ell)|\leq(\delta^{-\lambda}r)^{\zeta}|A_{i}|\quad\textrm{for all lines}\ \ell\ \textrm{and all}\ r>0,\ \quad i=1,2.

(8.4)

Let
G\subset A_{1}\times A_{2}\times A_{3}
be uniformly
\delta^{\alpha}
-dense. Then

\mathcal{E}_{\delta}\big(\{(a_{1}-a_{2})\cdot a_{3}\colon(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in G\}\big)\geq\delta^{\varepsilon-1}.

(8.5)

Proposition 8.3
.

Assertion
2
implies Assertion
1
.

8.2.1
A two-ends reduction on tubes, and anisotropic rescaling

The new hypothesis in Assertion
2
is the Frostman condition (
8.4
). First, a standard “two-ends reduction” shows that every
A\subset[0,1]^{2}
has a large subset that satisfies a re-scaled analogue of (
8.4
) localized inside a rectangle of some width
w
. A precise version is given below. The proof is standard, and is omitted.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
A\subset[0,1]^{2}
be a union of
\delta
-squares and let
0<\zeta\leq 1/4
. Then there exists a number
w>0
and a line
\ell
, so that if we define
A^{\prime}=A\cap N_{w}(\ell)
, then
|A_{i}^{\prime}|\geq\frac{1}{2}w^{\zeta}|A|
, and for all
0<r\leq 1
and all lines
\ell
, we have

|A^{\prime}\cap N_{r}(\ell)|\leq(r/w)^{\zeta}|A^{\prime}|.

(8.6)

If we anisotropically re-scale the output set
A^{\prime}\subset N_{w}(\ell)
from Lemma
8.4
by a factor of
w^{-1}
in the
l^{\perp}
direction, then the non-concentration estimate (
8.6
) from Lemma
8.4
will resemble the non-concentration hypothesis (
8.4
) from Assertion
2
. The problem is that if
A
was a
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\eta})
-set, then
A^{\prime}
will be a
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\eta-\zeta})
-set, but the image of
A^{\prime}
under this anisotropic rescaling might not be a
(\delta,1,C)
-set for any reasonable value of
C
. The next lemma says that we can fix this problem at the cost of refining
A^{\prime}
slightly.

Lemma 8.5
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
w\in[\delta^{1-\varepsilon},1]
, let
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a rectangle of dimensions
1\times w
, and let
E\subset R
be a
(\delta,1,C)
-set for some
C\geq 1
. Let
A\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be an affine transform that maps
R
to
[0,1]^{2}
. Then there exists a subset
E^{\prime}\subset E
with
|E^{\prime}|\geq(\delta/w)^{\varepsilon}|E|
so that the union of
\delta/w
cubes that intersect
A(E^{\prime})
form a
(\delta/w,1,(w/\delta)^{\varepsilon}C)
–set.

Proof.

Let
\rho=\delta/w
and let
T=T(\varepsilon)>0
be a large integer. Define
r_{j}=2^{-Tj}
, for
j=0,\ldots,J
, with
J=\lceil\frac{\log_{2}(\rho)}{T}\rceil
. For each index
0\leq j\leq J
, let
\mathcal{S}_{j}
be the set of grid-aligned
r_{j}
-squares inside
[0,1]^{2}
and let
\mathcal{R}_{j}=\{A^{-1}(S)\colon S\in\mathcal{S}_{j}\}
, i.e.
\mathcal{R}_{j}
is a tiling of
R
using rectangles
R_{j}
that are translates of
r_{j}R
.

By pigeonholing, there exists a subset
E^{\prime}\subset E
with
|E^{\prime}|\gtrsim|\log\delta|^{-J}|E|
; a number
m>0
; and integers
M_{1},\ldots,M_{J-1}
such that the following holds:

•

For every
R_{J}\in\mathcal{R}_{J}
, either
R_{J}\cap E^{\prime}=\emptyset
or
|R_{J}\cap E^{\prime}|=m
.

•

For every
j=1,\ldots,J-1
and every
R_{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{j}
,

\textrm{either}\ R_{j}\cap E^{\prime}=\emptyset,\ \textrm{or}\ \#\{R_{J}\in\mathcal{R}_{J}\colon R_{J}\subset R_{j},\ R_{J}\cap E\neq\emptyset\}=M_{j}.

Observe that if
R_{j}\cap E\neq\emptyset
, then
|R_{j}\cap E^{\prime}|=M_{j}m
. On the other hand,
R_{j}\cap E^{\prime}
is contained in a ball of radius
2r_{j}
, and hence
|R_{j}\cap E^{\prime}|\leq 2Cr_{j}|E|\leq 2C|\log\delta|^{-J}r_{j}|E^{\prime}|,
i.e.

M_{j}\leq 2m^{-1}C|\log\delta|^{-J}r_{j}|E^{\prime}|.

(8.7)

Let
F
be the union of
\rho
-squares that intersect
A(E^{\prime})
. Then
|F|=m^{-1}|E^{\prime}|\rho^{2}
. We claim that it suffices to prove that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and all
r\geq\rho
,

|F\cap B(x,r)|\lesssim C|\log\delta|^{-J}r|F|\sim C|\log\delta|^{-J}rm^{-1}|E^{\prime}|\rho^{2},

(8.8)

where the implicit constant may depend on
T
. Indeed, once (
8.8
) has been established, then the lemma follows by selecting
T=T(\eta)
sufficiently large and
\delta_{0}>0
sufficiently small. Since the implicit constant in (
8.8
) is allowed to depend on
T
, it suffices to establish the inequality for radii of the form
r=r_{j}
for some
1\leq j\leq J
.

We now turn to the proof of (
8.8
) for balls of radius
r_{j}
. It will be more convenient to prove (
8.8
) for
r_{j}
-squares. Fix such a square
S
, and suppose
F\cap S
is non-empty (otherwise there is nothing to prove). Unwinding definitions and applying (
8.7
), we have

|F\cap S|=M_{j}\rho^{2}\leq 2m^{-1}C|\log\delta|^{-J}r_{j}|E^{\prime}|\rho^{2}.\qed

We will sometimes need to move from scale
\delta
to a coarser scale. The next lemma says that after a refinement,
(\delta,\alpha,C)
-sets are well-behaved under coarsening.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
0<\delta\leq\rho\leq 1
and let
E
be a
(\delta,\alpha,C)
-set for some
C\geq 1
. Then there is a set
E^{\prime}\subset E
with
|E^{\prime}|\geq|\log\delta|^{-1}|E|
, so that the union of
\rho
-cubes intersecting
E^{\prime}
is a
(\rho,\alpha,C^{\prime})
-set, with
C^{\prime}\sim|\log\delta|C
.

Proof.

By dyadic pigeonholing, we can select
E^{\prime}\subset E
and
m>0
so that for each
\rho
-square
Q
, either
|E^{\prime}\cap Q|=\emptyset
or
|E^{\prime}\cap Q|=m
. Let
E_{\rho}
be the union of
\rho
-squares that intersect
E^{\prime}
. Then
|E_{\rho}|=(|E^{\prime}|/m)\rho^{2}\leq|\log\delta|^{-1}(|E^{\prime}|/m)\rho^{2}
. Let
B
be a ball of radius
r\geq\rho
, and let
B^{\prime}
be its
2
-fold dilate. Then
|B^{\prime}\cap E^{\prime}|\leq|B^{\prime}\cap E|\leq C(2r)^{\alpha}|E|
. In particular, there are at most
C4r^{\alpha}|E|/m

\rho
-squares that are contained in
B^{\prime}\cap E
, and hence at most
4Cr^{\alpha}|E|/m
such cubes that intersect
C\cap E
. We conclude that

|E_{\rho}\cap B|\lesssim\rho^{2}Cr^{\alpha}|E|/m\lesssim|\log\delta|Cr^{\alpha}|E_{\rho}|.\qed

8.2.2
Kaufman’s projection theorem

The last major tool we will need is Kaufman’s projection theorem
[
16
]
. The precise version we will need is given below; we include a proof for completeness.

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
0<\alpha\leq\beta\leq 1
and let
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists
\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
C\geq 1
, let
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a
(\delta,\alpha,C)
-set and let
\Lambda\subset S^{1}
be a
(\delta,\beta,C)
-set. Let
H\subset\Lambda\times F
and let
d=|H|/(|\Lambda|\,|F|)
. Then there exists
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda
with
|\Lambda^{\prime}|\gtrsim d|\Lambda|
such that for every
\theta\in\Lambda^{\prime}
, we have

|\theta\cdot F_{\theta}|\geq C^{-2}d^{3}\delta^{1-\alpha+\varepsilon},

where
F_{\theta}=\{x\in F\colon(\theta,x)\in H\}
.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
8.2
to
H
with
\varepsilon=1/2
, let
H^{\prime}\subset H
be the resulting refinement, and let
\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda
be the projection of
H^{\prime}
to the first coordinate. Then
|\Lambda_{0}|\gtrsim d|\Lambda|
(so in particular
\Lambda_{0}
is a
(\delta,\beta,C/d)
-set) and for each
\theta\in\Lambda_{0}
we have
|F_{\theta}|\gtrsim d|F|
.

Define
\nu=\frac{\chi_{\Lambda_{0}}}{|\Lambda_{0}|}
,
\mu=\frac{\chi_{F}}{|F|}
,
\mu_{\theta}=\frac{\chi_{F_{\theta}}}{|F_{\theta}|}
. If
\theta\in\Lambda_{0}
then
\frac{d\mu_{\theta}}{d\mu}\lesssim d^{-1}
. For
x\neq y
, define
e_{x,y}=\frac{x-y}{|x-y|}
.
Fix
0<r<\alpha
. Since
\mu
and
\nu
satisfy the Frostman conditions
\mu(B(x,r))\leq Cr^{\alpha}
and
\nu(B(x,r))\leq(C/d)r^{\beta}
, there is a constant
K_{r}
so that

\int_{F}\int_{F}|x-y|^{-r}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)\leq K_{r}C,

and

\int_{\Lambda_{0}}|\theta\cdot e|^{-r}d\nu\leq K_{r}d^{-1}C\quad\textrm{for every}\ e\in S^{1}.

In what follows, all implicit constants are allowed to depend on
r
. We compute

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda_{0}}\int_{F}\int_{F}

\displaystyle|\theta\cdot x-\theta\cdot y|^{-r}d\mu_{\theta}(x)d\mu_{\theta}(y)d\nu(\theta)

\displaystyle\lesssim d^{-2}\int_{F}\int_{F}\int_{\Lambda_{0}}|\theta\cdot x-\theta\cdot y|^{-r}d\nu(\theta)d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle=d^{-2}\int_{F}\int_{F}\int_{\Lambda_{0}}|x-y|^{-r}\cdot|\theta\cdot e_{x,y}|^{-r}d\nu(\theta)d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim Cd^{-3}\int_{F}\int_{F}|x-y|^{-r}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim C^{2}d^{-3}.

By Fubini, there exists a subset
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda_{0}
with
|\Lambda^{\prime}|\gtrsim|\Lambda_{0}|
, so that for every
\theta\in\Lambda^{\prime}
we have

\int_{F}\int_{F}|\theta\cdot x-\theta\cdot y|^{-r}d\mu_{\theta}(x)d\mu_{\theta}(y)\lesssim C^{2}d^{-3}.

We conclude that
|\theta\cdot F_{\theta}|\gtrsim C^{-2}d^{3}\delta^{1-r}
. The result now follows by selecting
r=\alpha-\varepsilon/2
and choosing
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
sufficiently small.
∎

8.2.3
Localization to rectangles of comparable width

We now consider the following situation. Suppose we have sets
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},G
that satisfy the hypotheses of Assertion
1
, and suppose that
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are contained in strips of width
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
, respectively. The next lemma says that either Conclusion
(B)
of Assertion
1
is true, or
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
are almost identical and
A_{1},A_{2}
are contained in a common strip, and furthermore
A_{3}
is contained in an orthogonal strip of comparable thickness.

Lemma 8.8
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta,\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}].
Let
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\subset[0,1]^{2}
be
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\eta})
-sets that satisfy the separation condition (
8.2
), and let
G\subset A_{1}\times A_{2}\times A_{3}
be uniformly
\delta^{\eta}
-dense. Suppose
A_{1}\subset N_{w}(\ell)
for some
w>0
and some line
\ell
. Let
\ell^{\perp}
be the line through the origin that is perpendicular to
\ell
.

Then at least one of the following two things must happen

(A)

\pi_{2}(G)\subset N_{\delta^{-\varepsilon}w}(\ell)
and
\pi_{3}(G)\subset N_{\delta^{-\varepsilon}w}(\ell^{\perp})
.

(B)

There exists
\rho\geq\delta
and an interval
I
of length at least
\delta^{-\eta}\rho
such that (
5.3
) holds.

Proof.

First, we may suppose that
w\leq\delta^{\varepsilon}
, or else Conclusion
(A)
holds and we are done.

Let
0<\eta<\!\!<\varepsilon_{1}<\!\!<\varepsilon
be small quantities that will be chosen below. Without loss of generality, we can suppose that the maps
\pi_{i}\colon G\to A_{i}
are onto (if not, replace each
A_{i}
by
\pi_{i}(G)
; this reduces the size of each
A_{i}
by at most a multiplicative factor of
\delta^{\eta}
, and hence the new sets are
(\delta,1,\delta^{-2\eta})
-sets). Let
t=\inf\{r\colon A_{1}\cup A_{2}\subset N_{r}(\ell)\}
, and let
U=N_{t}(\ell)
. Clearly
t\geq\delta
, since
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
contains at least one
\delta
-square. Let
U^{\perp}=N_{\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}t}(\ell^{\perp}).
Let
e
be a unit vector parallel to
\ell
.

Step 1. Trapping
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
and
A_{3}
inside thick orthogonal strips

First, we will show that either
A_{3}\subset U^{\perp}
, or Conclusion
(B)
holds. If
t\geq\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}
then
A_{3}\subset[0,1]^{2}\subset U^{\perp}
and we are done. Thus we will suppose
t<\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}
.
Suppose
A_{3}\not\subset U^{\perp}
, and let
a_{3}\in A_{3}\backslash U^{\perp}.
Since
A_{3}
satisfies (
8.2
), we have
\operatorname{dist}(a_{3},0)\geq\frac{1}{2}
, and hence
|a_{3}\cdot e|\geq\frac{1}{4}\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}t
. In particular, if
a_{1},a_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}
are
\geq 2t
separated, then
|e\cdot(a_{1}-a_{1}^{\prime})|\geq\frac{1}{4}|a_{1}-a_{1}^{\prime}|
and hence

|a_{3}\cdot(a_{1}-a_{1}^{\prime})|\geq|a_{3}\cdot e|\,|e\cdot(a_{1}-a_{1}^{\prime})|-|a_{3}\cdot e^{\perp}|\,|e^{\perp}\cdot(a_{1}-a_{1}^{\prime})|\geq\frac{1}{4}|a_{3}\cdot e|\,|a_{1}-a_{1}^{\prime}|-t.

(8.9)

Let
s=8t/|a_{3}\cdot e|
. Since
a_{3}\in[0,1]^{2}
, we have
|a_{3}\cdot e|\leq 2
and hence
s\geq 2
, and in particular
s>\delta
. We also have
s\leq 8t/(\frac{1}{4}\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}t)\leq 32\delta^{\varepsilon_{1}}
. Observe that if
|a_{1}-a_{1}^{\prime}|\geq s
, then by (
8.9
) we have

|a_{3}\cdot(a_{1}-a_{1}^{\prime})|\geq\frac{1}{8}|a_{3}\cdot e|\,|a_{1}-a_{1}^{\prime}|.

(8.10)

Fix an element
a_{2}\in A_{2}
, and let
A_{1}^{\prime}\subset A_{1}
be a maximal
s
-separated subset of
\pi_{1}(n_{G}[(a_{2},a_{3})])
. Since
G
is uniformly
\delta^{\eta}
-dense,
\pi_{1}(n_{G}[(a_{2},a_{3})])
has measure at least
\delta^{1-\eta}
, and since
A_{1}
is a
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\eta})
-set, we conclude that
\#A_{1}^{\prime}\geq\delta^{2\eta}s^{-1}
.
Thus by (
8.10
), if we define
\rho=2t=|a_{3}\cdot e|s
, then

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\big(\{(a_{1}-a_{2})\cdot a_{3}\colon a_{1}\in A_{1}\}\big)\geq\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\big(\{a_{3}\cdot a_{1}\colon a_{1}\in A_{1}^{\prime}\}\big)\gtrsim\#A_{1}^{\prime}\gtrsim\delta^{2\eta}s^{-1}.

Furthermore,
\{(a_{1}-a_{2})\cdot a_{3}\colon a_{1}\in A_{1}\}
is contained in the interval
J=\big([0,1]^{2}\cap(U-a_{2})\big)\cdot a_{3}
, which has length
|J|\sim|a_{3}\cdot e|\geq s^{-1}\rho\gtrsim\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\rho\geq\delta^{-\eta}\rho
. We conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\big(J\cap\{(a_{1}-a_{2})\cdot a_{3}\colon(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in G\}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\big(\{a_{3}\cdot(a_{1}-a_{2})\colon a_{2}\in A_{2}^{\prime\prime}\}\big)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\delta^{2\eta}s^{-1}\gtrsim\delta^{2\eta}\frac{|J|}{\rho}\gtrsim\delta^{2\eta-\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon}\Big(\frac{|J|}{\rho}\Big)^{1-\varepsilon}.

If
\eta\leq\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon/2
and
\delta_{0}
is selected sufficiently small, then Conclusion
(B)
holds.

Step 2.
A_{1}-A_{2}
spans many directions inside
U

If
t\leq\delta^{-\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{1}}w
, then Conclusion
(A)
holds and we are done. Henceforth we will assume that
t>\delta^{-\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{1}}w
. We will prove that Conclusion
(B)
holds. Fix a point
a_{2}\in A_{2}\,\backslash\,N_{t/2}(\ell)
—such a point must exist since otherwise we would have
t\leq 2w
. In particular, we have
t/2\leq\operatorname{dist}(a_{2},N_{w}(\ell))\leq t
. If
a_{1},a_{1}^{\prime}\in A_{1}
with
|a_{1}-a_{1}^{\prime}|\geq 10w/t
, then

|\pi_{a_{2}}(a_{1})-\pi_{a_{2}}(a_{1}^{\prime})|\geq\frac{1}{10}t|a_{1}-a_{1}^{\prime}|,

where
\pi_{a_{2}}(x)=\frac{x-a_{2}}{|x-a_{2}|}
denotes the radial projection from the vantage point
a_{2}
.

Recall that
A_{1}
is contained in a ball
B
of radius
1/10
, which has distance at least
1/2
from
a_{2}
. Thus
A_{1}\subset B\cap N_{w}(\ell)
, and
\pi_{a_{2}}(B\cap N_{w}(\ell))
is contained in an arc
J\subset S^{1}
of length
|J|\sim t
.

Let
u=10w/t
, and let
A_{1}^{\prime}\subset A_{1}
be a maximal
u
-separated subset of
\pi_{1}(n_{G}[(a_{2})])
. Since
G
is uniformly
\delta^{\eta}
-dense,
\pi_{1}(n_{G}[(a_{2})])
has measure at least
\delta^{\eta}|A_{1}|
. Since
A_{1}
is a
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\eta})
-set, we conclude that
\#A_{1}^{\prime}\geq\delta^{2\eta}u^{-1}
. Thus we have
\pi_{a_{2}}(A_{1}^{\prime})\subset J
, and
\pi_{a_{2}}(A_{1}^{\prime})
is dense inside
J
at scale
w
, in the sense that

\mathcal{E}_{w}\big(\pi_{a_{2}}(A_{1}^{\prime})\big)\gtrsim\#A_{1}\gtrsim\delta^{2\eta}u^{-1}\gtrsim\delta^{2\eta}tw^{-1}.

(8.11)

Let
I=\{1,3\}
and
X=\pi_{I}\big(n_{G}[(a_{2})]\big)\cap(A_{1}^{\prime}\times A_{3})
; this is the set of pairs
(a_{1},a_{3})\in A_{1}^{\prime}\times A_{3}
so that
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in G
. Since
G
is uniformly
\delta^{\eta}
-dense, we have
\#X\geq\delta^{\eta}\#(A_{1}^{\prime})(A_{3})
(recall that
A_{3}
is a union of
\delta
-squares, and
A_{1}^{\prime}
is a finite,
u
-separated set. Thus
\#X
refers to the number of cube-point pairs.) Apply Lemma
8.2
to
X
, with
\varepsilon=1/2
, let
X^{\prime}\subset X
be the output, and let
A_{3}^{\prime}=\pi_{3}(X^{\prime})
; thus for each
a_{3}\in A_{3}^{\prime}
, there are
\gtrsim\delta^{\eta}\#A_{1}^{\prime}
elements
a_{1}\in A_{1}^{\prime}
with
(a_{1},a_{3})\in X
.

Let
t^{\prime}=\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}t
. Let
\tilde{R}
be a
1\times t^{\prime}
rectangle pointing in direction
e
that contains
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
, and let
\tilde{R}^{\perp}
be a
1\times t^{\prime}
rectangle pointing in direction
e^{\perp}
that contains
A_{3}
(the existence of such a rectangle
\tilde{R}
is easy; just select a suitable subset of
N_{t^{\prime}}(\ell)
. The existence of
\tilde{R}^{\perp}
is guaranteed by the arguments in Step 1). Let
A\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be an affine-linear transformation that sends
\tilde{R}
to
[0,1]^{2}
, and let
A^{T}
be the transpose of
A
; then
A^{T}(\tilde{R}^{\perp})
is a unit square.

Apply Lemma
8.5
to
A_{3}\subset\tilde{R}^{\perp}
with
\eta
as above, and let
A_{3}^{\prime}\subset A_{3}
be the output of that lemma. Let
\tilde{A}_{3}
be the union of
\delta/t^{\prime}
squares that intersect
A^{\perp}(A_{3}^{\prime})
; then
\tilde{A}_{3}
is a
(\delta/t^{\prime},1,\delta^{-2\eta})
-set. Applying Lemma
8.6
with
\delta/t^{\prime}
in place of
\delta
and
w/t^{\prime}
in place of
\rho
(this is valid since
w/t^{\prime}\leq\delta^{\varepsilon}<1
), we can find a subset
Y\subset\tilde{A}_{3}
, so that if
\tilde{A_{3}}^{\prime}
denotes the set of
w/t^{\prime}
-squares that intersect
Y
, then
\tilde{A}_{3}^{\prime}
is a
(w/t^{\prime},1,\delta^{-3\eta})
-set.

Since
|\pi_{a_{2}}(a_{1})-\pi_{a_{2}}(a_{1}^{\prime})|\sim t^{\prime}|\pi_{A(a_{2})}(A(a_{1}))-\pi_{a_{2}}(A(a_{1}^{\prime}))|
, by (
8.11
) we have

\mathcal{E}_{w/t^{\prime}}\Big(\pi_{A(a_{2})}(A(A_{1}^{\prime}))\Big)\gtrsim\mathcal{E}_{w}\big(\pi_{a_{2}}(A_{1}^{\prime})\big)\gtrsim\delta^{2\eta}tw^{-1}=\delta^{2\eta+\varepsilon_{1}}t^{\prime}w^{-1}.

Thus if
\delta_{0}
is chosen sufficiently small, then the radial projection
\Lambda=\pi_{A(a_{2})}(A(A_{1}^{\prime}))
is a
(w/t^{\prime},1,\delta^{3\eta+\varepsilon_{1}})
-set. For each
\tilde{a}_{3}\in\tilde{A}_{3}^{\prime}
with pre-image
a_{3}
, there are
\gtrsim\delta^{\eta}\#A_{1}^{\prime}
unit vectors
\theta\in\Lambda
, so that
\theta=\pi_{A(a_{2})}(A(a_{1}))
for some
a_{1}\in A_{1}^{\prime}
with
(a_{1},a_{3})\in X^{\prime}
. In particular,
X^{\prime}
has density
d\geq\delta^{\eta}
.

This is precisely the setting for Theorem Theorem
8.7
. Applying Theorem
8.7
with
\varepsilon/2
in place of
\varepsilon
,
d=\delta^{\eta}
and
C=\delta^{3\eta+\varepsilon_{1}}
, we conclude that there exists a unit vector
\theta\in\Lambda
of the form
\theta=\pi_{A(a_{2})}(A(a_{1}))
, so that if
(\tilde{A}_{3}^{\prime})_{\theta}
denotes the set of
\tilde{a}_{3}
(with pre-image
a_{3}
) with
(a_{1},a_{3})\in X^{\prime}
, then

\mathcal{E}_{w/t^{\prime}}(\theta\cdot(\tilde{A}_{3}^{\prime})_{\theta})\gtrsim\delta^{9\eta+2\varepsilon_{1}}(t^{\prime}/w)^{1-\varepsilon/2},

and hence

\mathcal{E}_{w/t^{\prime}}\big(\{(A(a_{1})-A(a_{2}))\cdot A^{T}(a_{3})\colon a_{3}\in\pi_{3}(n_{G}[(a_{1},a_{2})])\}\big)\gtrsim\delta^{9\eta+2\varepsilon_{1}}(t^{\prime}/w)^{1-\varepsilon/2}.

But

\mathcal{E}_{w/t^{\prime}}\big(\{(A(a_{1})-A(a_{2}))\cdot A^{T}(a_{3})\colon a_{3}\in\pi_{3}(n_{G}[(a_{1},a_{2})])\}\big)=\mathcal{E}_{w}\big(\{(a_{1}-a_{2})\cdot a_{3}\colon a_{3}\in\pi_{3}(n_{G}[(a_{1},a_{2})])\}\big).

The latter set is contained in
\tilde{R}^{\perp}\cdot(\tilde{R}-\tilde{R})
, which is an interval
I
of length
t^{\prime}
. Setting
\rho=w
, we conclude that
I
has length
t^{\prime}=\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}t\geq\delta^{-\varepsilon}\rho
, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\big(I\cap\{(a_{1}-a_{2})\cdot a_{3}\colon(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in G\}\big)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\delta^{9\eta+2\varepsilon_{1}}(t^{\prime}/\rho)^{1-\varepsilon/2}

\displaystyle=\delta^{9\eta+2\varepsilon_{1}}(t^{\prime}/\rho)^{\varepsilon-\varepsilon/2}\Big(\frac{|I|}{\rho}\Big)^{1-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\geq\delta^{9\eta+2\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon^{2}/2}\Big(\frac{|I|}{\rho}\Big)^{1-\varepsilon}.

If
\varepsilon_{1}
is selected sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
) then
9\eta+2\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon^{2}/2<0
. Selecting
\delta_{0}
sufficiently small, we have that Conclusion
(B)
holds.
∎

8.2.4
The second reduction

Armed with the above lemmas, we are now ready to prove Proposition
8.3
. In brief, we apply Lemma
8.4
to localize
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
to strips
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
. We then use Lemma
8.8
to show that these strips must coincide, and furthermore, that
A_{3}
is localized to an orthogonal strip of the same width. We then re-scale these strips using Lemma
8.5
and apply Assertion
2
to the corresponding re-scaled sets. We now turn to the details.

Proof of Proposition
8.3
.

Suppose Assertion
2
holds. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Define

\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon\lambda(\varepsilon/2)/3,

(8.12)

where
\lambda
is the quantity from Assertion
2
with
\varepsilon/2
in place of
\varepsilon
. Define

\zeta=\min\big(\eta_{1}(\varepsilon_{1})/2,\ \varepsilon\lambda(\varepsilon/2)/3\big),

(8.13)

where
\eta_{1}(\varepsilon_{1})
is the quantity from Lemma
8.8
with
\varepsilon_{1}
in place of
\varepsilon
and
\lambda(\varepsilon/2)
is the quantity from Assertion
2
with
\varepsilon/2
in place of
\varepsilon
. Define

\eta=\min\big(\varepsilon_{1}\zeta,\ \varepsilon\alpha(\varepsilon/2,\zeta)/40\big),

(8.14)

where
\alpha(\varepsilon/2,\zeta)
is the is the quantity from Assertion
2
with
\zeta
as defined in (
8.13
) and
\varepsilon/2
in place of
\varepsilon
. We will be slightly less precise about our choice of
\delta_{0}
, since this plays a minor role.

Let
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
and let
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},G
satisfy the hypotheses of Assertion
1
for these values of
\eta
and
\delta
. We must show that at least one of Conclusion
(A)
or
(B)
from Assertion
1
is true for
\varepsilon
as above.

First, apply Lemma
8.4
to
A_{1}
, with
\zeta
as defined in (
8.13
); we obtain a width
w_{1}
, a line
\ell_{1}
, and a set
A_{1}^{\prime}=A_{1}\cap N_{w_{1}}(\ell_{1})
. Apply Lemma
8.2
to the induced subgraph
G\cap(A_{1}^{\prime}\times A_{2}\times A_{3})
with
\varepsilon=1/2
; we obtain a uniformly
c
-regular hypergraph
G^{\prime}
with
c\geq 2^{-4}\delta^{\eta}
. Replacing
A_{2}
by its image under
\pi_{2}(G^{\prime})
, we may suppose that
\pi_{2}\colon G^{\prime}\to A_{2}
is onto; this step might reduce the size of
A_{2}
by a multiplicative factor of
c
. Next, apply Lemma
8.4
(with
\zeta
as defined in (
8.13
)) to
A_{2}
; we obtain a width
w_{2}
, a line
\ell_{2}
, and a set
A_{2}^{\prime}=A_{2}\cap N_{w_{2}}(\ell_{2})
. Apply Lemma
8.2
to the induced subgraph
G^{\prime}\cap(A_{1}^{\prime}\times A_{2}^{\prime}\times A_{3})
with
\varepsilon=1/2
; we obtain a uniformly
c
-regular hypergraph
G^{\prime\prime}
with
c\geq 2^{-8}\delta^{\eta}
. Let
A_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\pi_{1}(G^{\prime\prime})
, and let
A_{3}^{\prime\prime}=\pi_{3}(G^{\prime\prime})
.

After this step has been completed, the sets
A_{i}^{\prime\prime}
,
i=1,2,3
are
(\delta,1,\delta^{-2\zeta}
)-sets;
G^{\prime\prime}
is uniformly
\delta^{-8}\delta^{\eta}
regular, and
A_{1}^{\prime\prime},A_{2}^{\prime\prime}
satisfy the following Frostman-type non-concentration on lines estimate:

|A_{i}^{\prime\prime}\cap N_{r}(\ell)|\leq 8\delta^{-\eta}(r/w_{i})^{\zeta}|A_{i}^{\prime\prime}|\quad\textrm{for all lines}\ \ell\ \textrm{and all}\ \delta\leq r\leq 1,\quad i=1,2.

(8.15)

For notational convenience, we will suppose that
w_{1}\geq w_{2}
(this is harmless since
A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
A_{2}^{\prime\prime}
play (anti) symmetric roles). By (
8.13
) and (
8.14
), we can apply Lemma
8.8
with
\varepsilon_{1}
in place of
\varepsilon
(once with
A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
A_{2}^{\prime\prime}
as stated, and once with their roles reversed) to conclude that at least one of the following is true.

•

Conclusion
(B)
of Assertion
1
holds (for
\varepsilon_{1}
, and hence also for
\varepsilon
).

•

w_{1}\leq\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}w_{2}
;
A_{1}^{\prime\prime}\cup A_{2}^{\prime\prime}
is contained in a strip
S
of width
w=\delta^{-\varepsilon_{1}}w_{2}
(and hence contained in an appropriately chosen rectangle
R
of dimensions
2\times w
); and and
A_{3}^{\prime\prime}
is contained in an orthogonal rectangle
R^{\perp}
of dimensions
2\times w
.

If the first item holds then we are done. Suppose instead that the second item holds. If
w\leq\delta^{1-\varepsilon/10}
, then by pigeonholing we can find orthogonal lines that satisfy (
5.2
) for the value of
\varepsilon
specified above, and hence Conclusion
(A)
of Assertion
1
holds.

Suppose instead that
w>\delta^{1-\varepsilon/10}
. Since
\eta<\varepsilon_{1}\zeta
, (
8.15
) can be re-written as

|A_{i}^{\prime\prime}\cap N_{r}(\ell)|\lesssim\Big(\frac{\delta^{-2\varepsilon_{1}}r}{w}\Big)^{\zeta}|A_{i}^{\prime\prime}|.

(8.16)

Let
A\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be an affine-linear transformation sending
R
to
[0,1]^{2}
and let
A^{T}
be its transpose; then
A^{T}(R^{\perp})
is a translate of the unit square. We also have

(a_{1}-a_{2})\cdot a_{3}=w(A(a_{1})-A(a_{2}))\cdot A^{T}(a_{3})\quad\textrm{for all}\ (a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in R^{2}\times R^{\perp}.

(8.17)

Let
\tau=\delta/w
. We would like to apply Assertion
2
to the sets
A(A_{1}^{\prime\prime}),

A(A_{2}^{\prime\prime})
and
A^{T}(A_{3}^{\prime\prime})
(and the corresponding image of
G^{\prime\prime}
) at scale
\tau
to conclude that Conclusion
(B)
of Assertion
1
must hold. The issue is that while
A_{i}^{\prime\prime}
,
i=1,2,3
are
(\delta,1,\delta^{-2\zeta})
-sets, their images under
A
(resp.
A^{T}
) might not be
(\tau,1,\delta^{-2\zeta})
-sets. We can fix this by sequentially applying Lemma
8.5
and Lemma
8.2
to each of the sets
A_{i}^{\prime\prime}
. We now turn to the details.

We apply Lemma
8.5
to
A_{1}^{\prime\prime}\subset R
, with
\varepsilon_{1}\eta/10
in place of
\varepsilon
; we let
A_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}
be the resulting subset. We then apply Lemma
8.2
to the induced subgraph
G_{1}=G^{\prime\prime\prime}\cap(A_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\times A_{2}^{\prime\prime}\times A_{3}^{\prime\prime})
, with
\varepsilon=1/2
; denote the output by
G_{1}^{\prime}
. Next we apply Lemma
8.5
to
A_{2}^{\prime\prime}\subset R
, with
\varepsilon_{1}\eta/10
in place of
\varepsilon
; we let
A_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}
be the resulting subset. We then apply Lemma
8.2
to the induced subgraph
G_{2}=G_{1}^{\prime}\cap(A_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\times A_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\times A_{3}^{\prime\prime})
, with
\varepsilon=1/2
; denote the output by
G_{2}^{\prime}
. Finally we apply Lemma
8.5
to
A_{3}^{\prime\prime}\subset R^{\perp}
, with
\varepsilon_{1}\eta/10
in place of
\varepsilon
; we let
A_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime}
be the resulting subset. We then apply Lemma
8.2
to the induced subgraph
G_{3}=G_{2}^{\prime}\cap(A_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\times A_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\times A_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime})
, with
\varepsilon=1/2
; denote the output by
G_{3}^{\prime}
.

Let
\tilde{A}_{1}
(resp.
\tilde{A}_{2}
,
\tilde{A}_{3}
) be the set of
\tau
-squares that intersect
A(A_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime})
(resp.
A(A_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime})
,
A^{\perp}(A_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime})
), let
\tilde{G_{0}}
be the set of
\tau
-cubes that intersect
(A\otimes A\otimes A^{T})(G_{3}^{\prime})
, and let
\tilde{G}_{3}
be the refinement obtained by applying Lemma
8.2
to
\tilde{G}_{0}
with
\varepsilon=1/2
. We have that
\tilde{G}
is uniformly
\delta^{4\eta}\geq\tau^{40\eta/\varepsilon}
-dense, and
\tilde{A}_{1},\tilde{A}_{2},\tilde{A}_{3}
are
(\tau,1,\tau^{-3\zeta/\varepsilon})
-sets.

After these refinements, (
8.16
) now says that for all
r\in[0,w]
and all lines
\ell
,

|A_{i}^{\prime\prime\prime}\cap N_{r}(\ell)|\leq\Big(\frac{\delta^{-3\varepsilon_{1}}r}{w}\Big)^{\zeta}|A_{i}^{\prime}|,\quad i=1,2.

(8.18)

Furthermore, since each
\delta\times\delta/w
rectangle that intersects
A_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}
(resp.
A_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}
) has the same size of intersection with
A_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}
(resp.
A_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}
), (
8.18
) implies that for all
r\in[0,1]
and all lines
\ell
,

|\tilde{A}_{i}\cap N_{r}(\ell)|\leq(\delta^{-3\varepsilon_{1}}r)^{\zeta}|\tilde{A}_{i}|\leq(\tau^{-30\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}r)^{\zeta}|\tilde{A}_{i}|,\quad i=1,2.

Let us now verify that the sets
\tilde{A}_{1},\tilde{A}_{2},\tilde{A}_{3}
and
\tilde{G}
now satisfy the hypotheses of Assertion
2
with
\varepsilon/2
in place of
\varepsilon
, and
\tau
in place of
\delta
. The estimate (
8.4
) holds with
\zeta
as above, provided
3\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon\leq\lambda(\varepsilon/2)
; this is guaranteed by (
8.12
). We have that
\tilde{A}_{1},\tilde{A}_{2},
and
\tilde{A}_{3}
are
(\tau,1,\tau^{-\lambda})
-sets, provided
3\zeta/\varepsilon\leq\lambda(\varepsilon/2)
; this is guaranteed by (
8.13
). Finally,
\tilde{G}
is uniformly
\tau^{\alpha}
-dense, provided
40\eta/\varepsilon\leq\alpha(\varepsilon/2,\zeta)
; this is guaranteed by (
8.14
). We conclude that there exists
\tau_{0}
so that

\mathcal{E}_{\tau}(\{(\tilde{a}_{1}-\tilde{a}_{2})\cdot\tilde{a}_{3}\colon(\tilde{a}_{1},\tilde{a}_{2},\tilde{a}_{3})\in\tilde{G}\})\geq\tau^{\varepsilon/2-1},

(8.19)

provided
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. We can ensure that
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
by selecting
\delta_{0}=\tau_{0}^{1/\varepsilon}
.

Comparing (
8.19
) and (
8.17
), we see that there is an interval
I
of length
w>\delta^{1-\varepsilon/10}
so that

\mathcal{E}_{\delta}\big(I\cap\{(a_{1}-a_{2})\cdot a_{3}\colon(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in G\}\big)\gtrsim\tau^{\varepsilon/2-1}=(|I|/\delta)^{1-\varepsilon/2}.

If
\delta_{0}>0
is sufficiently small, then Conclusion
(B)
of Assertion
1
holds.
∎

8.3
Non-concentration near lines, and the conclusion of the proof

In this section we will finish the proof of Theorem
5.2
by showing that Assertion
2
is true; see Proposition
8.14
below.

We will show that if two
(\delta,1,C)
-sets
A_{1},A_{2}
both satisfy a non-concentration near lines estimate of the form (
8.4
) for some (small) Frostman exponent
\zeta>0,
then a large subset of
A_{1}\times A_{2}
must satisfy a much stronger non-concentration estimate. The precise definition is as follows.

Definition 8.4
.

Let
t\geq 0
,
K\geq 1
, and
c\in[0,1)
. Let
A_{1},A_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be unions of
\delta
-squares. We say that
(A_{1},A_{2})
has
(t,K,1-c)
-
thin tubes
if there exists a union of
\delta
-cubes
E\subset A_{1}\times A_{2}
with
|E|\geq(1-c)|A_{1}|\,|A_{2}|
with the following property. For all
a_{1}\in A_{1}
, we have

\big|\pi_{2}(n_{E}[(a_{1})])\cap N_{r}(\ell)\big|\leq K\cdot r^{t}|A_{2}|\quad\text{for all }r>0\text{ and all lines $\ell$ containing $a_{1}$.}

(8.20)

We will sometimes suppress the parameters
K
and
c
, and just say that
(A_{1},A_{2})
have
t
-thin tubes.

8.3.1
Assertion
2
holds if
(A_{1},A_{2})
have
1
-thin tubes

The next lemma says that if
(A_{1},A_{2})
has
1
-thin tubes, then
(A_{1}-A_{2})\cdot A_{3}
will have
\delta
-covering number about
\delta^{-1}
, and hence the conclusion of Assertion
2
will hold.

Lemma 8.9
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta_{0}>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
c\in[0,1)
and
K\geq 1
. Let
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\subset[0,1]^{2}
be
(\delta,1,K)
-sets that satisfy the separation condition (
8.2
), and suppose that
G\subset A_{1}\times A_{2}\times A_{3}
is uniformly
2c
-dense. Suppose furthermore that
(A_{1},A_{2})
has
(1,K,1-c)
-thin tubes. Then

\mathcal{E}_{\delta}\big(\{(a_{1}-a_{2})\cdot a_{3}\colon(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in G\}\big)\geq c^{5}K^{-2}\delta^{\varepsilon-1}.

(8.21)

Proof.

Since
(A_{1},A_{2})
has
(1,K,1-c)
-thin tubes, there exists a set
E\subset A_{1}\times A_{2}
with
|E|\geq(1-c)|A_{1}|\,|A_{2}|
that satisfies (
8.20
) with
t=1
and
K
as above. Since
G
is
2c
dense, we have
|\pi_{(1,2)}(G)|\geq 2c|A_{1}|\,|A_{2}|
. Thus
|E\cap\pi_{(1,2)}(G)|\geq(2c-c)|A_{1}|\,|A_{2}|\geq c|A_{1}|\,|A_{2}|
; denote this new set by
E^{\prime}
.

By Fubini, there exists
a_{1}\in A_{1}
so that
|\pi_{2}(n_{E^{\prime}}[(a_{1})])|\geq c|A_{2}|
. Fix this choice of
a_{1}
and define
A_{2}^{\prime}=\pi_{2}(n_{E^{\prime}}[(a_{1})])
. Let
L
be a maximal set of lines containing
a_{1}
with
\delta
-separated directions, each of which intersects
A_{2}^{\prime}
. By (
8.20
) with
t=1
and
r=\delta
, we have
\#L\geq\frac{|A_{2}^{\prime}|}{K\delta|A_{2}|}\geq\frac{c}{K}\delta^{-1}.
Let
\Lambda\subset S^{1}
be the set of (grid-aligned)
\delta
-intervals that intersect
L
. Then
\Lambda
is a
(\delta,1,K/c)
-set, and for each
\theta\in\Lambda
there exists (at least one)
a_{2}\in A_{2}
so that
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\pi_{(1,2)}(G)
;
|a_{1}-a_{2}|\geq\frac{1}{2}
; and
\frac{a_{1}-a_{2}}{|a_{1}-a_{2}|}=\theta
. Choose such a
a_{2}\in A_{2}
for each
\theta\in\Lambda
, and let
H\subset\Lambda\times F
be the set of pairs
(\theta,a_{3})
so that
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in G
. Since
G
is
2c
dense, for each
\theta\in\Lambda
there are at least
2c\#A_{3}
choices of (
\delta
-separated)
a_{3}\in A_{3}
with
(\theta,a_{3})\in H
. Thus
|H|\geq c|\Lambda|\,|A_{3}|
.

We now apply Theorem
8.7
to
\Lambda
,
A_{3}
, and
H
, with
\alpha=\beta=1
and
\varepsilon
as above. Let
\delta_{1}>0
and
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda
be the output from that lemma. Select
\theta\in\Lambda^{\prime}
, let
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\pi_{(1,2)}(G)
be the pair of points associated to
\theta
, and let
r=|a_{1}-a_{2}|\geq\frac{1}{2}
. We have

\begin{split}\{(a_{1}-a_{2})\cdot a_{3}\colon(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in G\}&\subset\{a_{3}\cdot\theta r\colon a_{3}\in(A_{3})_{\theta}\}.\end{split}

(8.22)

By Theorem
8.7
the latter set has
\delta
-covering number
\geq c^{5}K^{-2}\delta^{\varepsilon-1}
.
∎

8.3.2
From weak non-concentration to
1/4
-thin tubes

In the previous section, we showed that the conclusion of Assertion
2
will hold if the pair
(A_{1},A_{2})
have 1-thin tubes. The problem is that the hypothesis of Assertion
2
appears to be much weaker—Assertion
2
only requires that
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
do not concentrate near lines with some (small) Frostman exponent
\zeta>0
. Surprisingly, this seemingly weaker nonconcentration hypothesis actually implies that
(A_{1},A_{2})
have 1-thin tubes. The first step in this direction is the following result from
[
22
]
(see also
[
23
]
); our formulation is slightly different, so we include a proof here for completeness.

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
\eta,\zeta,\alpha>0
. Then there exists
\delta_{0}>0
so that the following is true for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
A_{1},A_{2}\subset[0,1]^{2}
be
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\eta})
-sets with
\text{dist}(A_{1},A_{2})\geq 1/2
, and suppose that
A_{2}
satisfies the non-concentration near lines estimate

|A_{2}\cap N_{r}(\ell)|\leq(\delta^{-\eta}r)^{\zeta}|G_{2}|\quad\textrm{for all lines}\ \ell\ \textrm{and all}\ r>0.

(8.23)

Then
(A_{1},A_{2})
have
(\frac{1}{4},K,1-\delta^{\alpha})
thin tubes, where
K=\delta^{-\frac{3\alpha}{\zeta}-\eta}.

Proof.

Let
\delta\leq r\leq 1
; we say a pair of
\delta
-squares
(Q,S)\subset A_{1}\times A_{2}
is bad at scale
r
if there is an infinite strip
T
of width
r
that intersects
Q
and
S
, with
|T\cap A_{2}|\geq Kr^{1/4}|A_{2}|
. Observe that no pairs of
\delta
-squares are bad at any scale greater than
K^{-4}
.
Define

E_{\operatorname{bad}}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\leq r\leq K^{-4}\\
r\ \textrm{dyadic}\end{subarray}}\bigcup_{(Q,S)\ \textrm{bad}}Q\times S,

where the first union is taken over all numbers
\delta\leq r\leq K^{-4}
of the form
2^{k}\delta
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and the second union is taken over all pairs
(Q,S)
of
\delta
-squares that are bad at scale
r
. Define
E=(A_{1}\times A_{2})\backslash E_{\operatorname{bad}}
. By construction, each
a_{1}\in A_{1}
satisfies (
8.20
) for this set
E
, with
t=1/4
and
K
as above. Clearly
E
is a union of
\delta
-cubes. To prove the lemma, it remains to show that for
K
as above, we have
|E|\geq(1-\delta^{\alpha})|A_{1}|\,|A_{2}|
. To do this, it suffices to show that for each dyadic
\delta\leq r\leq K^{-4}
,

\Big|\bigcup_{(Q,S)\ \textrm{bad}}Q\times S\Big|\leq\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{|\log\delta|}|A_{1}|\,|A_{2}|.

(8.24)

Fix
r
. For each square
Q\subset A_{1}
, let
A_{2}^{Q}\subset A_{2}
be the union of those squares
S\subset A_{2}
so that
(Q,S)
is bad at scale
r
. Define
A_{1}^{\prime}
to be the union of those squares
Q\subset A_{1}
for which
|A_{2}^{Q}|\leq\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{2|\log\delta|}|A_{2}|
, and let
A_{2}^{\prime\prime}=A_{2}\backslash A_{2}^{\prime}
. The contribution to (
8.24
) from pairs
(Q,S)
with
Q\subset A_{1}^{\prime}
is at most
\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{|\log\delta|}|A_{1}|\,|A_{2}|
, which is acceptable.

It remains to analyze the contribution from those pairs
(Q,S)
with
Q\subset A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
, and in particular it suffices to show that

|A_{1}^{\prime\prime}|\leq\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{2|\log\delta|}|A_{1}|.

(8.25)

We will prove this by contradiction. Suppose that (
8.25
) fails. For each square
Q\subset A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
, select a set
\mathcal{T}_{Q}
of width-
r
strips pointing in
r
-separated directions, each of which intersect
Q
and each of which satisfy
|T\cap A_{2}|\geq Kr^{1/4}|A_{2}|
. We have
|A_{2}\cap\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{Q}}T|\gtrsim\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{|\log\delta|}|A_{2}|,
and since
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are
1/2
-separated, each point
a_{2}\in A_{2}
is contained in at most 10 of the strips from
\mathcal{T}_{Q}.
If
Q\subset A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
S\subset A_{2}
, write
Q\sim S
if
S
intersects one of the strips from
\mathcal{T}_{Q}
. Define

\mathcal{W}=\{(Q,Q^{\prime},S)\colon Q\sim S,\ Q^{\prime}\sim S\}.

If (
8.25
) fails, then by Cauchy-Schwartz we have

\#\mathcal{W}\gtrsim\Big(\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{|\log\delta|}\Big)^{4}(\#A_{1})^{2}(\#A_{2})

(8.26)

(recall that we use
\#A_{1}
to denote the number of
\delta
-squares in
A_{1}
, and similarly for
\#A_{2}
). We will show that for
K
as above, then (
8.26
) is impossible.

First we will bound the number of triples
(Q,Q^{\prime},S)\in\mathcal{W}
with
\operatorname{dist}(Q,Q^{\prime})\leq c_{0}\delta^{\eta}\delta^{-4\alpha}|\log\delta|^{4}
, where
c_{0}>0
is a small absolute constant. Since
A_{1}
is a
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\eta})
-set, after
Q
has been chosen there are at most
c_{0}\delta^{4\alpha}|\log\delta|^{-4}\#A_{2}
choices for
Q^{\prime}
, and thus the number of such triples is at most
c_{0}\delta^{4\alpha}|\log\delta|^{-4}(\#A_{1})^{2}(\#A_{2})
.

Next we will bound the number of triples
(Q,Q^{\prime},S)\in\mathcal{T}
where
S
is contained in the
s=\delta^{\eta}\big(c_{0}\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{|\log\delta|}\big)^{4/\zeta}
neighborhood of the line
\ell_{Q,Q^{\prime}}
that connects the centers of
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
. By (
8.23
), after
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
have been chosen there are at
(\delta^{-\eta}s)^{\zeta}\#A_{2}=c_{0}^{4}\big(\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{|\log\delta|}\big)^{4}\#A_{2}
choices for
S
, and thus the number of such triples is at most
c_{0}^{4}\big(\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{|\log\delta|}\big)^{4}(\#A_{1})^{2}(\#A_{2})
.

Finally, we will bound the number of remaining triples
(Q,Q^{\prime},S)\in\mathcal{W}
. For each such triple, there are
r
-tubes
T\in\mathcal{T}_{Q}
and
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{Q^{\prime}}
with
\angle(T,T^{\prime})\gtrsim s^{-1}
(by
\angle(T,T^{\prime})
we mean the angle between their coaxial lines), so that
S
intersects
T\cap T^{\prime}
. Thus for each pair
Q,Q^{\prime}\subset A_{1}^{\prime\prime}
, the corresponding set of squares
S\subset A_{2}
intersect the set

\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T,T^{\prime})\in\mathcal{T}_{Q}\times\mathcal{T}_{Q^{\prime}}\\
\angle(T,T^{\prime})\gtrsim s\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!T\cap T^{\prime}.

Each set
T\cap T^{\prime}
in the above union is contained in a ball of radius
\sim s^{-1}r
, and since
A_{2}
is a
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\eta})
-set, each set
A\cap A^{\prime}
can intersect at most
\delta^{-\eta}s^{-1}r\#A_{2}
squares from
A_{2}
. There are at most
(\#\mathcal{T}_{Q})(\#\mathcal{T}_{Q^{\prime}})\leq r^{-1/2}
such pairs
(T,T^{\prime})
, and hence the total number of triples of this type is at most

(\#A_{1})^{2}\big(\delta^{-\eta}s^{-1}r\#A_{2}\big)r^{-1/2}\leq\delta^{-\eta}s^{-1}K^{-2}(\#A_{1})^{2}(\#A_{2})\leq c_{0}\Big(\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{|\log\delta|}\Big)^{4}(\#A_{1})^{2}(\#A_{2}),

where we used the fact that
r\leq K^{-4}
. We conclude that if
\delta>0
is sufficiently small, then
\#\mathcal{W}\leq 3c_{0}\Big(\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{|\log\delta|}\Big)^{4}(\#A_{1})^{2}(\#A_{2})
. If
c_{0}>0
is chosen sufficiently small, then this contradicts (
8.26
), and completes the proof.
∎

8.3.3
From
1/4
-thin tubes to
1
-thin tubes

The conclusion of Lemma
8.10
is that
(A_{1},A_{2})
have
1/4
-thin tubes. But Lemma
8.9
only applies to pairs that have 1-thin tubes, which appears to be a much stronger hypothesis. In the arguments below, we will explain how to bridge the gap. The main tool we will use is the Bootstrap Lemma
[
24
, Lemma 2.8]
. The Bootstrap lemma is somewhat technical, so we will just state a special case.

Lemma 8.11
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta>0
and
K_{0}>0
such that the followig holds. Let
K\geq K_{0}
and let
A_{1},A_{2}\subset[0,1]^{2}
be
(\delta,1,K^{1/2})
-sets that are
1/2
-separated. Let
\sigma\in[1/4,1-\varepsilon]
. Suppose that both
(A_{1},A_{2})
and
(A_{2},A_{1})
have
(\sigma,K,1-c)
-thin tubes for some
c\in(1/K,1/10)
. Then
(A_{1},A_{2})
and
(A_{2},A_{1})
have
(\sigma+\eta,K^{1/\eta},1-5c)
-thin tubes.

Remark 8.2
.

Lemma 2.8, from
[
24
]
is stated using slightly different terminology; in the language of
[
24
]
, the above lemma is the special case where
\beta=1/4
,
s=1
,
C=K^{1/2}
,
\sigma\in[1/4,1-\varepsilon]
, and
\mu=\chi_{A_{1}}/|A_{1}|,\ \nu=\chi_{A_{2}}/|A_{2}|
(note also that
[
24
]
uses
\varepsilon
in place of
c
).

The next result records what happens if we iterate Lemma
8.11
multiple times.

Lemma 8.12
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
N,K_{0}\geq 1
so that the following holds. Let
K\geq K_{0}
and let
A_{1},A_{2}\subset[0,1]^{2}
be
(\delta,1,K^{1/2})
-sets that are
1/2
-separated. Suppose that both
(A_{1},A_{2})
and
(A_{2},A_{1})
have
(1/4,K,1-c)
-thin tubes for some
c\in(1/K,2^{-N})
. Then both
(A_{1},A_{2})
and
(A_{2},A_{1})
have
(1,\delta^{-\varepsilon}K^{N},1-2^{N}c)
-thin tubes.

Proof.

Let
\eta=\eta(\varepsilon)
and
K_{0}=K_{0}(\varepsilon)
be as described in Lemma
8.11
, with the value of
\varepsilon
given above. Let
M=\lfloor 1/\eta\rfloor
. Our hypotheses on
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
ensure that
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
also satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma
8.11
. Iteratively applying Lemma
8.11

M
times, we conclude that
(A_{1},A_{2})
and
(A_{2},A_{1})
have
(1-\varepsilon,K^{N},1-5^{M}c)
-thin tubes, for some
N=N(\varepsilon)\geq 1
. Increasing
N
if necessary, we can also ensure that
5^{M}\leq 2^{N}
.

Since
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are
(\delta,1,K^{1/2})
-sets, (
8.20
) is automatically satisfied for
t=1
when
r\leq\delta
. For
\delta<r\leq 1
,
(A_{1},A_{2})
and
(A_{2},A_{1})
satisfy (
8.20
) for
t=1-\varepsilon
and
K^{N}
in place of
K
. But this implies that
(A_{1},A_{2})
and
(A_{2},A_{1})
satisfy (
8.20
) for
t=1
and
\delta^{-\varepsilon}K^{N}
in place of
K
, i.e.
(A_{1},A_{2})
and
(A_{2},A_{1})
have
(1,\delta^{-\varepsilon}K^{N},1-2^{N}c)
thin tubes, as claimed.
∎

Combining Lemmas
8.10
and
8.12
, we obtain the following.

Lemma 8.13
.

For all
0<\varepsilon,\zeta<1
, there exists
\lambda=\lambda(\varepsilon)>0
,
\alpha=\alpha(\varepsilon,\zeta)>0
, and
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(\varepsilon,\zeta)>0
so that the following holds for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. Let
A_{1},A_{2}\subset[0,1]^{2}
be
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\lambda})
-sets with
\text{dist}(A_{1},A_{2})\geq 1/2
, and suppose that
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
satisfy the non-concentration near lines estimate

|A_{i}\cap N_{r}(\ell)|\leq(\delta^{-\lambda}r)^{\zeta}|A_{i}|\quad\textrm{for all lines}\ \ell\ \textrm{and all}\ r>0,\ \quad i=1,2.

Then
(A_{1},A_{2})
have
(1,\delta^{-\varepsilon},1-\delta^{\alpha})
thin tubes.

Proof.

Let
N
be the value from Lemma
8.12
, with
\varepsilon/2
in place of
\varepsilon
. Let
\alpha=\varepsilon\zeta/(24N)
and let
\lambda=\varepsilon/(4N)
. By Lemma
8.10
with
2\alpha
in place of
\alpha
, both
(A_{1},A_{2})
and
(A_{2},A_{2})
have
(\frac{1}{4},\delta^{-\varepsilon/2N},1-\delta^{2\alpha})
-thin tubes. Apply Lemma
8.12
with
\varepsilon/2
in place of
\varepsilon
(note that Lemma
8.12
requires that
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
be
(\delta,1,K^{1/2})
-sets; this is guaranteed by our choice of
\lambda
). We conclude that
(A_{1},A_{2})
and
(A_{2},A_{1})
have
(1,\delta^{-\varepsilon},1-2^{N}\delta^{2\alpha})
-thin tubes. The result now follows by selecting
\delta_{0}
sufficiently small so that
2^{N}\delta^{2\alpha}\leq\delta^{\alpha}
.
∎

Combining Lemmas
8.9
and
8.13
, we obtain the following.

Proposition 8.14
.

Assertion
2
is true.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
8.13
with
\zeta
as above and
\varepsilon/16
in place of
\varepsilon
; let
\lambda_{1},\alpha_{1}
and
\delta_{1}
be the output from this lemma. Let
\delta_{2}
be the output of Lemma
8.9
with
\varepsilon/2
in place of
\varepsilon
.

Define

\lambda=\min(\lambda_{1}/10,\ \varepsilon/16),\quad\alpha=\alpha_{1}/2,\quad\delta_{0}=\min(\delta_{1},\ \delta_{2}).

Let
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. If
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},G
satisfy the hypotheses of Assertion
2
for the values of
\lambda
and
\alpha
given above, then
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
are
(\delta,1,\delta^{-\varepsilon/16})
-sets;
(A_{1},A_{2})
have
(1,\delta^{-\varepsilon/16},1-\delta^{2\alpha})
-thin tubes; and
G
is uniformly
\delta^{\alpha}
dense. Thus we can apply Lemma
8.9
to obtain the estimate (
8.5
).
∎
